# Consideration of funding proposals - Addendum VI

## Funding proposal package for FP120

### Summary

This addendum contains the following seven parts:

a) A funding proposal titled "Chile REDD-plus results-based payments for results period 2019-2023";

b) No-objectiction letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or fiscal point(s);

c) Environmental and social report(s) disclosure;

d) Secretary's assessment;

e) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;

f) Request from an accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and

g) Gender documentation.

# A. Proposed and projected REDD+ plus results

Please provide the following information:

| Total volume of REDD+ plus results achieved in the results period as reported in the country's RBP technical annex (tCO2eq) | 16,634,475 tCO2eq 2014 |
|---|---|
| 16,634,475 tCO2eq 2014 | |
| Total 18,409,425 tCO2eq | |

## A+ Achieved volume of REDD+ plus results offered to the pilot programme in this proposal (tCO2eq)

Between 2014 and 2016, Chile achieved results reducing emissions from REDD+ totaling 18,494.45 tCO2eq. Chile has not received other payments for the results of 2014-2016. The National Forest Monitoring System (Bioclim) verified a total of 17,634.45 tCO2eq in the period between November 2014 and February 2018 (La Araucanía Region), 17,634.45 tCO2eq in the period between November 2014 and February 2018 (Los Lagos Region) verified 17 tCO2eq between 2014 and 2018. These results are not included in the total volume of REDD+ plus results offered to the pilot programme because they were attributed ERI as prevent a double payment situation [more details in section C.3.1.1, accounting rules]. In addition, an analysis of the reversal risk potential of the ENCCR, a reserve for reversals, was carried out and it was established that there is no buffer for reversals (more details in section C.3.1.1), accumulating 20 tCO2eq.

These considerations result in a total volume of REDD+ results offered to the pilot programme of 14,536.220 tCO2eq.

## B+ Expected volume of REDD+ plus results to be achieved in the following years of the eligibility period (tCO2eq)

Chile scheduled the second milestone of monitoring for 2018 in order to report its results by March 29, 2019 according to the PRE/FRE/FRIL by the UNFCCC.

The results to be achieved in the years of 2017 and 2018 have been measured. But, during January and February of 2017, Chile experienced a period of extreme drought (including the regions considered in the FRE/FRIL), presenting a significant number of forest fires that drove down the vegetation cover and increased soil moisture temperatures. These fires produced an amount of emissions that was not included in the results achieved by this country, estimating a total amount of emissions of 72.8 million tCO2eq. The regions most affected were Araucanía Bioclim, while only Los Biobío and Los Ríos Regions, 64,236 hectares, were affected. These results indicate that during the year 2017, and because of these extraordinary events, Chile will not be able to achieve the ERI as expected, so the year 2017 will not be considered for any results-based payment instance.

During the year 2018, Chile presented a number of forest fires that were not included in the baseline scenario. The results for 2018 are expected to have results of ER similar to what was reported for the years 2014, 2015 and 2016.

Given these mentioned considerations, the results of the following years of the eligible period would be 6.136.475 tCO2e instead of 8.931.916 tCO2e as reported in the baseline scenario.

Regarding the expected ER for 2018, Chile started negotiation of an Emission Reduction Purchase Agreement (ERPA) with the World Bank (WB). The total volume of emission reductions to be achieved under the ERPA goes from 2019 to 2025, while the total volume of emission reductions to be achieved under the current agreement is 5.208.000 tCO2e, therefore the indicative results for the year 2018 will be 3.746.000 tCO2e as set aside for the fulfillment of the objectives of the ERPA.

#### B.1.1 UNFCCC Technical Assessment and Analysis process

**(1) Consistency of the FREL/FRL:** Please provide any additional information that supplements the justification submitted in the FREL/FRL submission.

The Technical Assessment Report (TAR) of the modified Forest Reference Emission Level / Forest Reference Level (FREL/FRL) submitted with the first BIR and is supported by an entire chapter of the aforementioned document. The TAR provides a detailed analysis of the consistency between the FREL/FRL and the GGOI emissions/removal estimates, which have been duly justified, do exist, however.

For instance, the TAR points out that the emissions from deforestation and forest degradation of the modified FREL/FRL are 3 times higher than those reported in the baseline scenario. The emissions from the modified FREL/FRL emissions from deforestation were 3 times higher and emissions from forest degradation were 10 times higher compared to the baseline scenario. In the context of the REDD+ National Greenhouse Gas Inventory (NGGI) Release report, some editorial mistakes were related to the value corresponding to emissions from Forest Degradation. The TAR points out that the difference between the values reported in the NGGI Release and the TAR is 938.196 tCO2e of tons of CO2 instead of 938.196 tCO2e of tons of CO2, therefore the difference is no longer 10 times as was mentioned in

# Chapter 9 of the FREL

Chile has provided a detailed explanation of these differences, indicating that the differences in emissions from deforestation are due to better activity data information and a more intensive use of emission factors based on country-specific data. As an example, it could be said that activity data is the same in both the modified FREL/FRL and the G4-1, but the emission factors used for Chile were different from those ones used in the FREL/FRL. See Chapter 9 of the FREL/FRL document for more details.

Despite the above, it is important to consider that there are certain intrinsic differences between FREL/FRL and G4-1. The main reasons are:

* **Geographical scope:** While the G4-1 covers the national territory, the FREL/FRL has a subnational scope, covering five regions that represent the 22% of the national territory and contain the 41% of the total deforestation.
* **Treatment of forest plantations:** While G4-1 accounts for emissions from forests planted after 1980, FREL/FRL does not account for these activities.

A comparative matrix detailing (based on Chapter 9 of FREL/FRL) demonstrating forest land (ha) and emissions (tCO2) distribution by REDD activities (deferred areas, degraded areas, areas for conservation and areas for restoration) is provided in the G4-1. The same matrix can be found in the FREL/FRL document.

In the same matrix are also provided definitions for all these four activities considered in FREL and in G4-1.


## With regards to forest degradation

With regard to the forest definition, the FREL/FRL excluded planted forests, considering only native forests in the definition. The G4-1 considers both planted and native forests.

Furthermore, in the activity of enhancement of forest carbon stocks, there are significant differences between the FREL/FRL and the G4-1 due to the exclusion of removals from forest plantations from the FREL/FRL calculation, which only considers the increase in biomass. The G4-1 includes all activities related to forest degradation.

#### (i.a) Data source of the FRE/FRL: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the data used for the construction of the FRE/FRL. Specifically, the FRE/FRL is based on historical data and is equal to or below the average annual historical emissions during the reference period.

The subnational FRE/FRLs are based on an annual average emission/removals of CO2. The activity data used for its construction is based on the land use information from the Forest Inventory (FInvent) for the reference period 1997-2012. The Cadastre is a project that started between 1993 and 1997, with periodic updates of the updated cadastres being conducted since then. The data used in the FRE/FRL construction for the subnational data constitutes the baseline of the land use information of the country and is used in several reports as an input to calculate the emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (FDD). On one hand, the FRE/FRL uses images to obtain the ADs. On the other hand, the information on emissions factors were obtained from the Forest National Inventory (FIN) and the University of Adelaide Australia. The data used for the construction of the FRE/FRL is provided by the Forest Institute (INFNAT) and the University Australis of Chile.

During the TAr, the TE noted that the original FRE/FRL was constructed using different reference periods for each REDD+ activity. This difference in reference period is due to the different available land use information, in particular, of different cadastre years. This problem was partially solved by Chile, presenting a modified FRE/FRL with a single reference period (2010-2030) that includes all REDD+ activities and sub-activities related to land use change, and 2010-2018 for activities or sub-activities that occur in permanent forests.

#### (ii.b) The country is considered HFLD: Please provide the basis/justification for this classification: N/A

##### (i.c) FRE/FRL adjustments for a HFLD country: If adjustment mode, please provide information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report. Adjustments are made to ensure that the adjusted FRE/FRL does not exceed 1.3% of the carbon stock over the eligibility period in the relevant area and/or exceed 0.5% of the total carbon stock of the country over the eligibility period.

##### (ii) FRE/FRL, in accordance with 12/CP.17: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the transparency of the FRE/FRL and whether it is consistent with the requirements set out in Decision 12/CMA/3, paragraph 5.

Include whether the FRE/FRL was constructed in accordance with the guidance in Decision 12/CMP.17; if not, provide information on how the FRE/FRL has been developed to ensure that it meets the quantified estimates of the FDD.

The FRE/FRL construction was developed using the IPCC 2006 Guidelines for National Greenhouse Inventories and the guidelines provided by the TAr. The data and information used in the construction of the FRE/FRL for the four REDD+ activities are transparent, complete, consistent, accurate and in accordance with the guidance set out in Decision 12/CMP.17.

Furthermore, the TA highlighted (in paragraph 30 of the TAR) the willingness of Chile to implement a stepwise approach to the development of its FRE/FRL. The country has already developed a draft FRE/FRL and is currently making efforts to develop stock charts for different forest types and develop history and current land use maps. The country also plans to continue developing the FRE/FRL, with the aim of eventually developing a full FRE/FRL with an extended geographical coverage and improvements in the methodologies.

##### (iv.e) FRE/FRL transparency: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Assessment Report in relation to the transparency of the FRE/FRL and whether it is consistent with the requirements set out in Decision 12/CMA/3, paragraph 5.

(vi) FREL/FRL: consistency. Please provide any additional information that supplements the information extracted from the historical data, including the results of the validation exercise, which could be resolved due to time series and for the construction of the FREL/FRL, when significant issues were raised in the report and addressed by Chile.

The applied methodology in the FREL/FRL construction is based on the activity data extracted from the historical land use change data (1980-2015) provided by Chile (LandSat images, 1987-2015). Due to technical and operational limitations, the Cadastre has a database with different base years for Chile regions. This information is not consistent in time series and it is difficult to compare results between regions. The TTF recommended that Chile could ensure more consistent time series by using the same satellite data sources (eg from LandSat or RapidEye) for all regions, as well as ensuring consistency of land use change data across regions (see para 41 of the TAR). This recommendation was considered by Chile, and the new maps of land use and land use change were generated. The TTF recommended that Chile should ensure the consistency of results of analysis and are based in remote sensing analysis; this map will allow to increase the consistency of the FREL/FRL construction with the historical data. The new maps of land use and land use change current time series of data, but has different years of map for each region, using several sources of data.

During the TA, a comparison of datasets were carried out between the activity data for deforestation presented by Chile and the datasets from Global Forest Watch (GFW), that uses LandSat time series analysis. The TAR

### vi) FREI/FRL accuracy: Provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Document Report in relation to whether all activities listed in paragraph 79 of UNFCCC/CP.15/CMP.2/INF.3 were included and/or if there are significant emissions from FREL/FRL submissions that were not, justify whether activities that are significant sources of emissions were not included due to lack of data or other reasons.

### viii) Sources of Emission: Provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Document Report in relation to whether all activities listed in paragraph 79 of UNFCCC/CP.15/CMP.2/INF.3 were included and/or if there are significant emissions from FREL/FRL submissions that were not, justify whether activities that are significant sources of emissions were not included due to lack of data or other reasons.

#### The Activities indicated in paragraph 79 of decision 1/C.P.15 include by Chile as its FREL/FRL are reduced emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (REDD+), afforestation, reforestation, conservation and enhancement of forest carbon stocks.

Chile explained that, in the case of the activity of sustainable management of forests, using a lack of geographical information to determine areas subject to this activity, Chile decided to include all changes resulting from sustainable management of forests under the activity of reducing emissions from forest degradation (REDD+). This means that activities related to afforestation and reforestation were not removed; it means that activities were not omitted. The AT highly commends Chile for increasing the transparency of its REDD+ RBP by including all changes resulting from sustainable management in the carbon stock changes from this activity as two other selected activities in the modified submission.

However, in order to provide an idea of the scale of the significance of emissions / removals of native forests, Chile provided data on the average annual area under management for the five regions that have been achieved:

| Region | Average Area 2001-2010 (Ha) |
| --- | --- |
| Maule | 35975.6 |
| Biobío | 4850.1 |
| Araucanía | 75141.0 |
| Los Ríos | 118930.0 |
| Los Lagos | 128296.7 |
| **Total** | **392313.3** |

Source: CONAF, Legal and Administrative Department

The average annual area under management represents 67.6% of the total area of native forest in the five regions (total area of native forest: 584000 ha).

Besides, according to the legislation, the following are the management activities allowed in native forest:

- Thinning, elimination control of invasive alien species, felling forest burned, recovery fellings, preparatory fellings, regeneration fellings, stripped, ecological enrichment, supplementary planting, thinning to waste, reforestation and restocking.
- Intermediate fellings, sanitary fellings, trimming, low thinning, trimming for non-timber purposes, thinning, thinning for non-timber purposes, shape trimming and productive thinning.
- Tree handling and thinning.

In conclusion, it can be affirmed that on the one hand it is a non-significant activity in terms of emissions or removals because less than 1% of the total is under management. On the other hand, due to the permitted management activities it is difficult to consider the managed native forest as source of degradation as suggested by the team of experts in paragraph 3.2 of the technical assessment report.

### (b) Significant pools: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the original FREL/FRL presentation and/or the revised FREL/FRL presentation, if applicable. It is important to note which significant pools were not included due to lack of data and why the omission does not constitute emissions from forest land use change.

In addition, provide a table to include all significant pools in future FREL/FRL submissions.

The carbon pools considered in the modified FREL/FRL included the above and below ground biomass for all HIGECs, dead organic matter (DOM) pool, soil organic carbon (SOC), and emissions from forest land use change activities. This pool was not included in the enhancement of forest carbon stock due to the substantial uncertainty associated with the estimation of the emission of SOC for deforestation.

In the original FREL/FRL presentation Gloe included the SOC pool but it was excluded in the modified submission due to the lack of information at national level and also to guarantee consistency with the GPGC which also excludes SOC, hence consistency between these two reports is maintained. This exclusion is due to the fact that the emission factor for deforestation used in Gloe is 105 tCO2/ha/year.

This omission of the SOC pool was justified by the TIE due to the lack of national data to describe the changes of carbon stock in soil and also because the inclusion of this pool would have led to a significant increase in submission of the FREL/FRL, secured in the dead organic matter (DOM) pool, that was included in the estimation of forest land use change emissions.

The SOC pool is included in the modified FREL/FRL. Date excluded to this pool because significant changes are not expected in the activity data related to deforestation and reforestation.

Despite the TA comments that emissions from SOC could be significant when forest lands are converted to other land uses, the exclusion of SOC was considered as conservative in the case of enhancement of forest carbon stock due to the fact that the emission factor for deforestation used in Gloe is 105 tCO2/ha/year and the estimation of the emission from soil organic carbon (SOC) for deforestation was done using Tier 1 methodology, which considers a range between 30-60 tCO2/ha/year. The emission factor used by Gloe is 14.83±9.19 tCO2/ha/year emitted from the living biomass and dead organic matter pools, representing 7.5% of the total emissions for deforestation in the modified FREL/FRL. This value is consistent with the IPCC Good Practice Methodological Framework, of which Chile is a participant, which highlights that the fact the exclusion of this pool does not imply that it should be included.

In summary, there is a recognition for the efforts made to have better information regarding DOM and SOC, with the aim to include these pools in a stepwise process and in future submissions or exclude them if there is evidence that they are not significant. It has been agreed by the TIE that the inclusion of these pools should be stated that continuous efforts are underway to include emissions factors and activity data related to deforestation.

#### (iv) IPCC guidance for FREL/FRL: Please indicate whether the construction of the FREL/FRL (both, including the reference period) is in accordance with the IPCC 2006 guidelines.

The IPCC 2006 guidelines were applied for the construction of the FREL/FRL as a basis for the estimation of emissions and removals resulting from the four REDD+ activities.

#### (vii) Issues related to applying IPCC guidance: Please mention any significant issues related to the application of IPCC/GPGs as revised in the TA report. Include any significant issues that are material to the alignment with the methodology used by the SOE, including the reference period and the inclusion/exclusion of activities started and resolved (e.g., provide a plan to address and overcome issues raised in TA Report that were not addressed in the FREL/FRL).

The TAE of the FREL/FRL mentions, in paragraph 11.0, that OIke used the guidance and guidance provided in the IPCC Guidelines for National GHG as a basis for the estimation of changes in carbon stocks and non-CO2 emissions.

One of the main aspects discussed during the original FREL/FRL submission was the possibility of using the same reference period for all REDD+ activities in the selected regions. This is because historical data availability is different across the four selected REDD+ activities, and due to the large number of reference periods used. Complexity, the TTE suggested that OIke could use the same reference period by using the tools described in the IPCC 2006 Guidelines for National GHG (Annex I, paragraphs 41-53). The TA noted that the reference period is 2013 for activities and sub-activities that are due to land use changes, and 2001-2010 for activities in permanent forests. This was done because of the lack of historical data availability and the complexity involved regarding interpretation of data, allowing to have a single reference period (2011-2013) for activities that are due to land use changes and another one (2001-2010) for permanent forests. This was done because of the number of reference periods, but in future submission OIke could present a single common reference period for all REDD+ activities.

With regard to the SOC even though the TA considers OIke's efforts to obtain better information, the TAE noted that the IPCC 2006 Guidelines include a method for estimating changes in carbon stocks using default emission factors. The TA noted that this is because of the lack of historical data availability and the complexity involved and because of the lack of national information on the rate of changes in this pool, identifying this issue as an area for future improvement (paragraphs 41 and 42 of the TAE).

The subnational NREB/NPF presented by OIke considers two reference periods, that use different interpretation methods:

- 2013 (one year): for activities and sub-activities related to changes in land use (deforestation, substitution, increase in forest area and restitution). The interpolation was performed using the following equation, that allowed to obtain an annual area of change for the activities.

### Equation:
\[ A_{\mathrm{t}} = \frac{A_{p1} - A_{p1}\left(\frac{\beta_{20}}{\beta_{p3}}\right) + A_{p4} - A_{p2}\left(\frac{\delta_{25}}{\beta_{p2}}\right)}{t_{p1} + t_{p2} - \beta_{3} - \beta_{2}} \]

Where:
- \(A\) = Annual area of change (ha)
- \(A_{p}\) = Area of change in period p (ha)
- \(b\) = Interpolation time
- \(t\) = Period time p (years)

### 2. 2001–2018 (10 years) for activities that occur in permanent forest (conservation, degradation in permanent forest and recovery of degraded forest). In this case the activity data was estimated by dividing the total emissions/removals by the number of years of the reference period (10), obtaining an annual estimate.

#### [ix] If previous reference level submitted:
- *Please indicate whether a specific reference level applying to the current FRL/FRL has been used, describe the difference between the previous level and the current one. Describe any adjustments made to the current FRL/FRL compared to the previous one, if applicable.*

#### [iv] Uncertainties: Please indicate whether the country has provided information on aggregated uncertainty of 33.4%. This analysis uses non-linear regression to estimate the emissions from deforestation and degradation. The uncertainties are estimated by estimating the final result (i.e., the emission estimates) for a range of values of the input parameters used in the model. In this case, the following parameters were added to estimate the final result i.e., removals due to forest carbon increase resulting from the direct effect of land use change and the indirect effect of changes in soil organic matter (SOM) pools (IPCC Guidelines 2006, equation 3.1 and 3.2). The uncertainty calculation was presented separately by activity type and for each of the three main SOM pools (soil surface layer, mineral soil and deep soil pools).

- *The aggregated uncertainty (33.4%) was calculated by performing an error propagation through each individual activity and neglecting any correlation between them.*

#### Among the assumptions considered for the uncertainty calculation, the FRL/FREL considers, for instance, that:
- For the maximum increase in carbon of the alert Afore type, the highest level of the mean annual increment (MAI) was used to be conservative.
- The same criteria was applied with the annual mean increment of the young forest type. In this case, the FRL/FREL considers a more conservative scenario regarding uncertainty, taking into consideration the highest possible value of error. As another example, for the alert Afore type, it is assumed that the mean annual increment is the average of all forest types due to the lack of specific information.

#### [ix] Please indicate whether different FRLs/FRELs have been used for different funding sources or other purposes, and if so, list and describe them.
- *Chile has not used different FRLs/FRELs for different funding sources or other purposes. The same FRL/FREL was used as reference level to participate in the REDD+ approach of the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility.*

### (i) Consistency of results with FREL/FRL
* Provide any additional information that supplements the information provided in the technical annexes.

### Chile reports the results of the implementation of the four activities for the periods 2014–2016 for activities resulting from the REDD+ AD. The total reduction of emissions is estimated at 385,790 tCO2e per year, which amounts to a total reduction of emissions of 6,136,475 ± 382tCO2e per year and were measured against the assessed FREL/FRL for the same pools, activities and gases in the corresponding periods, 2003–2013 when there is a land use change and 2001–2010 when there is no land-use change.

The data and information provided in the technical annexes are in overall accordance with the guidelines contained in the REDD+ AD. The results presented in the technical annexes are consistent, transparent, and in the technical annexes are transparent and, to the extent possible, consistent with the assessed FREL/FRLs/technical annexes for the same pools, activities and gases.

During the technical analysis process of the technical annex (TA), the technical team of experts (TTE) noted that Chile was using different emission factors for the activities that involve land use change when comparing the FREL/FRL with the results presented in the technical annex. The TTE also noted that Chile was using data coming from the National Forest Inventory (NFI). Despite the existence of updated data, the experts considered that it would be more appropriate to use the same emission factors as those used for the FREL/FRL recalculation using the same emission factor as in the FREL/FRL; this recalculated data was submitted in a previous version of the TA. On 28th July of 2015, the revised TA presented updated results consistent with the assessed FREL/FRL.

The experts noted that, with the revised version of the technical annex, Chile ensured consistency to the extent possible between the results reported for the same pools, activities and gases in the technical annexes, FREL and estimation of results by: the inclusion of the same gases, the same area, the same forest definition, the methodology used (IPCC 2nd and 3rd approaches), the same emission factors, and the same reference ground biomass for deforestation and forest degradation by substitution; these EF were derived from the same source as those used in the FREL/FRL.

The experts also noted that Chile used consistent methodologies to estimate carbon stocks, using a mixture of IPCC Tier 2 and 3 approaches. To determine the degredation activity, Chile used a consistent 3 method default forest reference level (FRL) for each forest type. The country used a single chart for the estimation of results for other forest types. The country did not use a separate chart for each forest type.

Other issues regarding the consistency of the AD were raised during the TA; this topic and the ways Chile plans to overcome them are elaborated in the following points (iv) and v).

**(b) Consistency of the data: Please provide any additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Analysis Report in relation to the consistency of the data and results provided in the technical annexes (i.e. data and methodologies were applied consistently over the results time series). Include information on how you addressed or overcome issues raised in the Technical Analysis Report, if applicable. If not applicable, provide a plan on how to address and overcome issues related to the Technical Analysis Report, including material to the consistency of the data and results that could not be resolved due to time and data restrictions.**

The data used for the construction of the Technical Annexes of REDD+ results, in the case of the Emission Factors (EF) and the Forest Carbon Monitoring System (FCMS), were consistent with the data used by Chile in its land use changes, from the cadastre maps and the maps of land use change.

In the case of the EF, the experts noted that: Chile used different emission factors for the estimation of the results under the REDD+ FREL/FRLI and the Technical Analysis Report (TAR) and the Forest Carbon Monitoring System (FCMS), respectively. After the Technical Assessment week, Chile decided, in order to maintain consistency with the FREL/FRLI, to use the same emission factor used for the estimation of results under the REDD+ FREL/FRLI for the results estimation. With these changes, the experts noted that the results were consistent.

In regard to the activity data, the experts noted that Chile used an alternative methodology to gather the information regarding land-use changes for the estimates of results. The methodology that was used for the TAR and FCMS was based on high-resolution imagery (0.5 m) from Landsat Imagery (2013-2016), while for the estimation of the results, land use change maps were derived from spectral classification during the monitoring period (2014-2017). The experts noted that this methodology was used to identify the land-use changes which the land-use changes were detected. Additionally, the collection of imagery for both the cadastre and the land-use change maps was done in different periods: the cadastre imagery was collected from (October 2015 to November to March). This approach was used because it was not possible for Chile to generate AD homogeneously during this period. The experts noted that, taking into account the above-mentioned methodological considerations, the technical experts considered that the results presented by Chile were consistent to the extent of the data and methodology used.

Please see section (v) below on how Chile plans to implement and improve the second FREL/FRLI regarding this issue.


**(c) Accuracy of the data: Please provide additional information that supplements the information contained in the Technical Analysis Report in relation to the accuracy of the data and information provided in the technical annexes. The Technical Analysis Report showed that Chile has made several efforts to increase the accuracy of the data used for the construction of the Technical Annexes of REDD+ results, in the case of the Emission Factors (EF) and the Forest Carbon Monitoring System that, as an institutional instrumental arrangement, is able to produce and deliver temporary land-use change maps with a minimum map unit of 0.27 ha (3 points of Landsat Imagery), together with permanent forest, together with emissions factors extracted from the National Forest Inventory. The experts commented on this issue.

Regarding the accuracy of the Activity data, the experts noted that the methodologies for estimating land use changes were different. In response to that, Chile clarified that, the methodology used to estimate land use change maps was based on high-resolution imagery (0.5 m) from Landsat Imagery (2013-2016), while the FREL/FRLI is based on high and medium resolution imagery. The AD estimation for the FREL/FRLI has three steps: 1) land use change maps are derived using a spectral index to identify land-use changes; 2) land use change maps are identified to identify land-use changes area without setting a minimum map unit (3); acting upon this, the experts noted that Chile plans to implement and improve the second FREL/FRLI regarding this issue.


To estimate AD for the results period medium resolution imagery is used (i.e. 20 m) in Landsat Imagery with a spectral index used to identify land use changes. The minimum map unit was 0.27 ha (3 points of Landsat Imagery). The experts noted that Chile plans to implement and improve the second FREL/FRLI regarding this issue.

### B.2.2. Additional criteria related to the achieved results

(vi) Uncertainties: Explain whether the country has provided information on aggregate uncertainties of the results (e.g., as in the FREL/FRL), or if this is not possible, explain why and provide information on assumptions and sources. If applicable, indicate the process implemented to minimize these uncertainties.

Chile presented an aggregated uncertainty of the results of 18.4%, as in the FRES/FRL; thus, this analysis was performed using weighted error propagation when the parameters were added to estimate the final result, while considering the relative importance of each parameter and its associated uncertainty to determine the final result. For these calculations, the equations 3.1 and 3.2 of the IPCC 2006 Guidelines were applied.

The uncertainty calculation was presented separately by activity, sub-activity, carbon pool and region, including the and/or sub-regions. The aggregated uncertainty for each activity was calculated as a weighted average, which was calculated by performing an error propagation through each individual activity and weighting the uncertainties.

The years included in the period considered for the current results-based payments from the GCF (2013, 2015 and 2016), are not being offered to other instances of results-based payments and are neither going to be offered to future instances. The REDD+ RBP is a separate mechanism that has been established by the Fund to charge to manage REDD+ RBP, and the legal and institutional arrangements established at regional and local level will continue to apply in order to ensure the implementation of the project and to mitigate the risk of competing ER title among different agencies. As an additional measure to avoid any risk of competing ER title, the GCF has agreed with the World Bank (WB) that the negotiation of an Emission Reduction Purchase Agreement (ERPA) will take into account mitigation measures.

After the submission of the ER Program to the FCB's Carbon Fund, Chile started the negotiation of and Emission Reduction Purchase Agreement (ERPA) with the World Bank. This ERA considers the same area as the GCF results-based payments (Maule to Los Lagos Regions) but different periods. The total volume of emission reductions that will be purchased by the World Bank for the ERPA goes from 2018 to 2025.

This safeguard refers to the complementarity or compatibility of the actions implemented and/or the overall National Forests and Climate Change and Vegetation Resources Strategy (ENCIF) objectives with national forest and environmental programmes, and with the Conventions and International Agreements in which Chile is a party.

The safeguards were applied consistently in a broader context than the pilot projects of the strategy, including activities that led to emission reductions, based on compliance with national legislation and the international agreements. The safeguard was addressed and respected throughout the preparation of the ENCIF and the actions associated with the ER. The safeguard was addressed and respected throughout the preparation of the ENCIF and the actions associated with the ER.

For addressing the safeguards, complementarity, compatibility and articulation of the actions with the Programmes and National Action Plans on climate change, as well as with International Conventions and Agreements, a document called the "Summary of Information for the Application of Safeguards" was prepared. A description of the documents analyzed and generated is available in the "First Summary of Information: Assessment of the Complementary and Compatible Actions with National Forests and Climate Change and Vegetation Resources Strategy (ENCIF), Reporting Period 2013 - 2017" (hereinafter the Safeguards Summary). This document includes the main international conventions, and other relevant instruments to which the ENCIF contributes to are:

- Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 2015-2030: specifically, goal 13 on climate action and goal 14 on life below water.
- United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification (UNCCD) ratified by Chile on 11 November 1997.
- The United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) ratified by Chile on 22 December 1995, and the Kyoto Protocol (KP) signed by Chile on 10 February 2002 and 10 February 2017 (COP21 in the final point to the UNCFC).
- Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD) signed by Chile on 14 December 1994 and Aichi Targets (Strategic Plan for Biodiversity 2011 - 2020) coordinated by the Ministry of the Environment, in its capacity as the National Focal Point.
- United Nations Forum on Forests (UNFF), adhering to the UN Strategic Plan for Forests (2030).
- Convention on International Trade in Endangered Species of Wild Fauna and Flora (CITES) ratified by Chile on 14 February 1987.

At the national level, the main legal instruments relevant in the context of the ENCIF include:

The safeguards were then fully respected, by applying the national and international policies, laws and regulations towards the preparation of the ENCGRY and implementation of actions to achieve the ER reported. - National legislation or establish a new forestry regulation that includes aspects related to climate change adaptation and mitigation, forest management, and sustainable use of forests.

The ENCGRY actions focus on resolving the need to enhance the current legislation or establish a new forestry regulation, in the specific action MTI, include provisions related to climate change, desertification, land degradation and drought (CCDLD), forest management and sustainable use of forests. The national law No 20/2008 and its regulations: IFS includes elements of preventive management and restoration after fires in the region of Aserrí, Corinto, Othigüín, Aracataca and Los Ríos; IIIF includes elements related to sustainable agricultural recovery; RII, Incorporation of forest conservation variables in Law No 1456/1982; RIII. Limit the application of firewood as a source of energy for rural communities is included in the national legislation.

Fully complying with this safeguard, the ENCGRY includes: among others actions related to forest management and sustainable use of forests for the sustainable management of resources.

To date, the following pilot projects, funded by various sources and corresponding to the early implementation phase of the ENCGRY, have been prepared and implemented taking into consideration the key international agreements and national legislation. The Government is executing and coordinating entity of the ENCGRY ensured compliance with the corresponding safeguards:

The pilot projects that have been completed are date:

- 6 reforestation projects in the region of Aserrí, Corinto, Othigüín, Aracataca and Los Ríos, covering an area of 444.8 hectares funded by the UN REDD Programme Targeted Support.
- 2 restoration projects for burned areas of Verapaz and Metapan regions, covering an area of 8431.1 hectares, funded by the Swiss Agency for Development and Cooperation (SDC).

- 1 restoration project for burned areas of Verapaz region, funded by SDC.

**Page 21**

All phases of REDD+, throughout the preparation and implementation of the ENCER and including the achievement of the ER results, ensured (and will keep on ensuring) the promotion of transparency and effectiveness in decision-making and environmental governance structures, and that national legislation and sovereignty is taken into account.

This safeguard, in the national context of Chile, is understood to refer, firstly, to the forest governance structure defined through the institutional arrangements that were and will be used to implement the emission reduction commitment (ECC) under REDD+ and the associated actions. This structure, which sits under the Gerencia de Desarrollo y Forestal Fomento of CONAF, has the role of ensuring entity coordination in the implementation of the ECC and its associated actions. The second safeguard is a permanent technical link with GONAL’s Interagency Committee on Climate Change, made up of GONAL’s technical staff and representatives from other relevant government agencies such as the Chilean Climate Change (CCTCC) by the Office of Agricultural Studies and Policies (DIEA) of MINAGRI, with the aim of ensuring that the actions are in line with national policies. The third safeguard is a permanent link to the highest decision-making body: it is the Council of Ministers for Sustainability, which validates multilateral documents related to REDD+ and other international agreements on climate change and biodiversity, among others. The structure, as described, maintains its transparency through the publication of the advances and achievements in the implementation of the ECC and associated actions by CONAF. The Ministry of Sustainability publishes the summary of the sessions, matters discussed and agreements reached (https://www.minsust.gob.cl/areas-de-trabajo-y-planes-de-gestion/red-de-sostenibilidad).

The country has also addressed this safeguard by ratifying or being party to international instruments such as the United Nations Convention against Corruption (UNCAC) on 13 September 2004, and the UNFCCC on 15 September 2004. The country ratified the Paris Agreement on 9 September 1998. At national level, the Political Constitution of the Republic of Chile (1980), Law No. 16737 (2000) and Law No. 20577 (2014) establish the principles that guide the country’s actions in relation to transparency measures in the country. To ensure the effectiveness of forest and environmental governance structures, reference is made to Law No. 18573/1996 and Law No. 20264/2004 (concerning National Forests and Forest Management) and Decree No. 10/2004 (which establishes a Summary of Information).

The country fully respects and complies with this safeguard. In light of the international and national legal framework, access to information and access to public information was guaranteed by CONAF which has its Comprehensive Information and Citizen Services System (SIAIC) among others to provide various fora across the country for citizens to participate in decision-making processes.

Regarding the effectiveness of forest governance structures, CONAF has an Internal Regulation on Order, Hierarchy and Discipline (Resolution No. 01/2014).

To ensure compliance with this safeguard, the MEC and the institutional attention places (OBS) were the heirs for the Mechanism of Complaints and Suggestions (MEC) of the ENCER, which comply with all the requirements established by international organizations for an MEC. It serves as a mechanism for citizens to report any irregularities or abuses in the implementation of the ECC and associated actions.

The ENCER (more information in the Information Note N° 22, Mechanism of Claims and Suggestions of the ENCER) is available at https://www.conaf.gob.cl/medio-ambiente/red-de-sostenibilidad/informacion-n-22/

Between January 1, 2014 and December 31, 2017, 93,689 applications were registered at the OBS at national level. The average time to resolve by each of these OBS was 5 days (range: 1 day to 12 days) (74%) and requests (21%). The main topic of the applications refer to protected areas, urban tree planting, native forest restoration and other environmental issues. Of all the applications registered in 2017, 93,689 received a response over the same period, with an average response time of 1 to 2 days (59%).

### Participation of Women

Among the international legal instruments ratified by Chile addressing the participation of women, the following stand out:

* **Resolution adopted by the General Assembly of the United Nations in 2011**, referring to the Political Participation of Women (A/RES/65/139)
* **Convention on the elimination of all forms of discrimination against women** (CEDAW). This was ratified by Chile in 1984.

The Plan for the Implementation of Social and Environmental Safeguards for Public Indigenous Consultations (PISSE) is a document that sets out the principles and procedures to be followed at international and national level in this matter. This document guided the development of the PISSE, which was approved by the National Council of Indigenous Peoples. It included the organization of regional workshops and a national workshop, engaging local, regional and national representatives from indigenous organizations, government agencies, civil society organizations, and other stakeholders to discuss issues related to gender equality and the mainstreaming of the gender approach and pluralistic participation.

In addition, in an integrated manner with the Plan, a Strategic Environmental and Social Assessment (SESA) was developed. This document includes environmental and social considerations that ensure the sustainable implementation of the action measures. The measures are in line with the national commitments made by Chile to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change.

In the context of the Safeguards Plan and SESA between 2013 and 2016, 15 regional workshops were held, involving 1,266 people, 36.4% of whom are Women and 9% indigenous peoples. In addition, a national workshop involved 78 participants, including 20 women and 10 indigenous peoples.

The participatory approach was manifested through the gathering of information through the identification of key actors; formation of focus groups for women (additional information can be found in Information Note N° 9 on Mainstreaming the gender approach);

(v) That actions are consistent with the conservation of natural forests and biological diversity,
ensuring that the actions referred to in paragraph 70 of this decision are not used for the conversion or degradation of natural forests. The ENCREC should contribute to the conservation of natural forests and other ecosystem services, and to enhance other social and environmental benefits.

This refers to the prevention of ENCREC action measures from promoting the conversion of native vegetation resources, in addition to promoting their protection and conservation to favor the ecosystem services and co-benefits for indigenous peoples and local communities.

To address this safeguard, the ENCREC should integrate the ratification of international agreements,
conventions and agreements signed by the country, where COMIFOR parties at a focal point are taken into account. This information is presented in detail in the Sol. The following instruments were considered:

* UNCED ratified by decree 2656/1998 and the UNESCO promulgated by Decree 123/1995 with the REED approach, as which COMIFOR is at the focal point.

National legislation and regulations regarding the conservation of natural forests and biological diversity,
preventing the detriment of ecosystems and conversion of natural forests:

* The Political Constitution of the Republic of Chile [1980].
* Law 20.574, which establishes the National Forest Fund and the National Forest Development Fund, aims to protect, recover and improve native forests, in order to ensure forest sustainability and environmental policy.
* Law 20.635, which establishes the National Forest Policy, as well as the protection of the environment, the preservation of nature and the conservation of environmental heritage. Under this law, the use of natural resources is regulated by means of a system of permits that have been previously regulated.

Regarding policies, programs and national plans focused on the conservation of forests and biodiversity, the ones listed below are the main ones:

- National Biodiversity Strategy 2017–2030*: to conserve the country's biodiversity by guaranteeing fair and equitable access for ecosystem goods and services; fostering the capacities to safeguard, restore and sustainably use the heritage and natural heritage.
- The National Adaptation Plan (NAP) under the Paris Agreement (FANCC II 2022–): within its objective to enhance adaptation, seeks to reduce vulnerability and increase the adaptive capacity of the country's human and natural resources.
- The National Plan of Action to Adaptation to Climate Change (2014)*: seeks to strengthen Chile’s capacity to adapt to climate change by promoting sustainable development in priority sectors and key biodiversity sectors.
- Plan for the Response to Climate Change in the Silva-pastoral sector (2006–2021): includes the general conservation of forest resources and the development of new silvicultural methods to face climate change.
- Adaptation to Climate Change in Biodiversity Plan (2014)*: seeks to promote the conservation of biodiversity, including its genetic diversity, through the implementation of sustainable practices; incorporation into instruments of territorial planning and strengthening of the System of Protected Areas.
- National Action Program against Desertification, Land Degradation and Drought 2016–2035 (FANCC II 2022–): seeks to reduce vulnerability and increase adaptive capacity.

Also, the Forest Policy (2015–2035) indicates within its strategic axes: the protection and restoration of the forest heritage, for which it seeks to conserve and increase the State’s forest heritage; develop environmental goods and services; and promote sustainable use of forest resources. 

In issues related to ecological restoration, the National Policy and Strategy for the Ecological Restoration of the National System of Protected Wild Areas of the State (2012) was elaborated to guide the execution of plans, projects and programs in areas with high risk of desertification, land degradation and drought.

Respect for this safeguard was carried out through the integration of the REDD+ policy guidelines of the UNESCO World Heritage Convention into laws and policies. Together with the input of the participatory process of the ENCUE, corresponding to the identification by the key actors of the causes of deforestation, desertification, land degradation and drought in areas of high risk of these phenomena.

These technical and participatory processes resulted in the definition of Forests for REDD+ policies, together with the measures that will be applied to prevent Desertification and Forest Degradation. From which the action measures of the Strategy were developed.

The action measures of the strategy aimed at preventing the conversion of native forest and other vegetation resources into agricultural land are: T-8. Strengthening the National System of Protected Wild Areas (SNAPES) areas in the context of the ENCUE; B12. Incorporate forest conservation variables into Law No. 19,456 that regulates the use of natural forests; B13. Incorporate the concept of restoration of ecosystems post Forest Fires; G1. Adaptation program for the management of vegetation resources in areas with high risk of desertification, land degradation and drought.

(vi) Actions to address the risks of reversals.

Actions that allow to face the risks of reversal that could be generated with the implementation of the ENCERY, as well those that could affect the planned reversals, ensuring the permanence of the reduction of emissions. To address this safeguard, the procedure established in the document "Guidelines for buffering the ER Program"[7] of the PFCP is being applied. This process includes: (i) the identification of risks of reversals, where risk refers to the probability that a reversal will occur; and (ii) the definition of actions aimed at providing support to key actors, the lack of institutional capacities and / or ineffective intersectoral / vertical coordination, as well as the presence of other factors that may increase the vulnerability of forests to natural disturbances (risks of disturbances and natural disasters). And all the elements are defined in order to minimize reversal potential reversals. The details of analysis are presented in the Safeguard Summary of Information.

The Law 20.389/2008 and Decree No. 701/1974 allow addressing some of the risks associated with reversals, given that they establish: (a) the need to obtain a permit for any activity that may affect forests.
In Chile, forest fires are one of the main causes of degradation of regeneration resources, and in Chile, 95% of them are anthropogenic. In this case, the national legislation considers the following regulations:
- Decree No. 430/21 (first approving the Forest Law), of the Ministry of Lands and Colonization, which establishes that the use of fire to eliminate pasture will be prohibited in forest areas.
- Supreme Decree No. 589/76, of the Ministry of Agriculture, to prevent the occurrence of forest fires established that the use of fire, to eliminate pasture in agricultural and forestry lands, will only take place after obtaining a permit from the corresponding regional directorate of the Ministry of Agriculture, which prohibits the use of fire to destroy vegetation in winter.
Reversal Management Mechanisms
The same as for the reference emission reference levels used in the ENCERY was proposed to be used to monitor the emissions associated with reversals. This safeguard, therefore, will be addressed through the Measurement and Reporting mechanism described above. The monitoring and reporting of reversals is described under Reporting and Verification as part of the National Strategy.

As a result of the analysis of reversal risk potential of the Strategic Plan, a reversal factor of 23% of the Elba is established for the period 2015-2030. This figure will be used to assess the validity of the Strategy. This risk will be generated only from emissions reductions due to deforestation and degradation, since increases in remaining forest cover are not considered as reversals.

(vii) Actions to reduce displacement of emissions.

Those actions that prevent territorial displacement of activities that generate GHG emissions associated with deforestation and forest degradation.

The UNFCCC establishes that the implementation of REDD+ activities might generate a risk of the reduction of deforestation and forest degradation in a specific site would push drivers of deforestation and activities that would otherwise have occurred elsewhere. In the case of the National Forest of Sierra de las Minas in Guatemala, Strang has a national scope, there is a risk of displacement of emissions associated with the substantial implementation of REDD+ activities.

In terms of regulations, to address this safeguard, the country refers to:

- Decree No. 430/2011, of the Ministry of Land and Colonization, which sets the definition title of the Forest Law.
- Article 5 of the Forestry Act (Ley de Bosques) which defines the Perpetual Forestry Agricultural (APF) lands to those who because of their soil condition and climate, should not be plowed permanently. These lands are used for the production of timber, fruit trees, coffee, cocoa, sugar cane, growing or intensive livestock.
- It is also defined that lands classified as APF, in addition to natural and artificial forests, include areas with a high degree of biodiversity.

- Decree Law No. 2560/1979, which replaces Decree Law No. 781, of 1974. The purpose of this Law is to regulate the use of forest lands in order to protect and preserve them, to promote the rights of forest owners and to foresee the necessary measures for the prevention of the degradation, protection and recovery of forests.

- Law No. 30/2001, on the Recovery of the National Forest and Forestry Development, a legal instrument which aims at the conservation, sustainable use and development of the national forest in accordance with forest sustainability and environmental policy, in line with the ENCGRY. This Law, among other regulations, establishes that the funds generated by the sale of carbon credits will be used to finance the Conservation, Recovery and Sustainable Management Fund of the native forest and the procedures and mechanisms for its implementation.

In the Summary of Safeguards displacement risks associated with the most relevant deforestation and degradation causes are identified for the ENCGRY among them:

- Expansion of agricultural-livestock activity and urban and industrial expansion
- Unsuitable use of vegetation resources for production.
- Forest fires
- Unsuitable management of forest crops
- Unsuitable use of vegetation resources for livestock

The risks associated with the displacement of emissions are linked to the reduction of the causes and agents of deforestation and forest degradation. The measures to be taken, differing from illegal agricultural activities in forested areas, increasing the vigilance and application of regulations can reduce deforestation in the places to which it is displaced. These measures will be implemented through the Measurement and Monitoring System of the ENCGRY, which includes elements of Measurement, Reporting and Verification (MRV).

C.12 Stakeholder involvement.

Please describe and provide evidence that the Cancun safeguards information was made transparently available to stakeholders.

The design process of the ENCGRY considered as a priority to ensure the effective and inclusive participation of all groups and key actors linked to vegetation resources, with special emphasis on local communities, indigenous peoples, women and other vulnerable people. Specifically, it was considered small owners, social


---
**Page 29**
---

organization (neighborhood associations, farmers' associations, among others), associations and groups of civil society organizations and study centers, non-governmental organizations (NGOs) and local governments and anyone interested in contributing to the formulation and future implementation of the ENCry, were invited to participate in the participatory process for the development of the Strategic Plan for the implementation of Social and Environmental Safeguards for Public and Indigenous Consensus and Self-Determination Fund (available at the link: https://www.encry.chile.cl/files/download/encry_plan-safeguards-encry/file

Once the stakeholders were identified, a Strategic Environmental and Social Assessment (SESA) was developed, which allowed to identify the main environmental and social impacts of the ENCry, as well as the increase of carbon stocks. It was also possible to generate proposals for the active measures of the ENCry, with this information, it is possible to develop specific action plans that propose the maintenance and / or increase of biodiversity.

The Strategic Environmental and Social Assessment (SESA) allowed us to work with the national institutions involved in the project, as well as with international organizations that provide technical and financial support in the formulation of the ENCry. This methodology also has the advantage of incorporating safeguards that not only provide environmental protection but also respect the rights of indigenous peoples, the right to information, the right to consultation, the right to participation, and the right to free, prior and informed consent (FPIC) of indigenous peoples, and the right to participate in decision-making. Thus, the SESA integrated national and international guidelines and standards that contribute to ensuring sustainability to the ENCry implementation phase.

In order to achieve compliance with the safeguards, an addition was made to a social environmental and forestry institutional framework. The participatory process was applied through workshops with the support of national and international agents who actively collaborated. Added to this process was the consideration and completion of the SESA, which allowed us to identify the main environmental and social impacts in linkage with representatives of different Focal Groups (FG), which, through their empirical and technical knowledge, were able to provide proposals for active measures that would allow the ENCry to generate a public policy instrument with long-term environmental and social sustainability to face the effects of Climate Change.

The ENCry has the support and commitment of civil society. During the design and formulation phase of the ENCry, the inclusion of social and environmental safeguards, which follow national and international guidelines and standards, was considered as an essential element for its implementation.

The participatory process in the formation phase of the ENCry carried out through regional workshops and a national workshop, had more than 1,200 active participants from all regions of the country, including:

- Indigenous peoples
- Institutions
- Private sector
- NGOs
- Academy
- Small and medium size forest landowners
- Groups of women
- Community development agents

More information on participatory processes can be found in the following link: https://www.encry.chile.cl/files.php/participation/encry-de-formulation https://www.encry.chile.cl/files.php/participation/encry-de-satisfaccion

In line with the participatory process, the evaluation considered cross-cutting analysis that allowed classifying, weighing and prioritizing the risks and adverse potential impacts identified, as well as ensuring compliance with the principles of FPIC.

## C.2 Use of proceeds and non-carbon benefits

### C.2.1 General description:

Provide a description on how the proceeds will be reinvested in activities consistent with the country's NDC, national strategy, and/or other relevant policies and strategies. The following information should include how the proceeds will be used to meet the criteria that contributes to the long-term sustainability of REDD+ plus activities.

The proceeds of the results-based payment are expected to be used as set out in this section and in a manner consistent with the restrictions set out in paragraph 14 of the terms of reference for the for the REDD RBP Plus Fund, which is attached hereto as Annex I.

The Ministry of Agriculture of Chile, through the National Forest Corporation (CONAF), has prepared the National Strategy on Climate Change and Vegetation Resources (ENCVR) 2017-2025, whose main objective is to increase the capacity for adaptation and mitigation of climate change in order to reduce the emissions of greenhouse gases in Chile by reducing the social, environmental and economic vulnerability generated by climate change. The ENCCVR includes actions that are aimed at the conservation of forest areas and human communities that depend on them. The ENCVR proposes 25 action measures impacting 100,000 hectares of native forests and 300,000 hectares of other vegetation resources in Chile. It also identifies resources and resources that will be needed to leverage from different financing sources (called conditions), such as international aid or national contributions to corruption-free REDD+.

Considering the increasing vulnerability of natural resources to climate change mitigation and adaptation, the ENCVR was proposed as a public policy instrument developed to comply with the national commitments made by Chile in the context of the Paris Agreement. The ENCVR is based on the concept that the contribution of the Forest Sector to the NDC considers the sustainable management and recovery of 100,000 hectares of native forests and 300,000 hectares of other vegetation resources in Chile, as well as the Forest Promotion Law. The NDC also includes the affirmation of 100,000 hectares of native species subject to the application of the Forest Sector Act and the Forest Promotion Law. In addition, it is aligned with international commitments established in the UNFCCC; the ENCVR is also aligned with the UNCED, especially


---
**Page 31**
---

The REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL (GREEN CLIMATE FUND) PAGE AS OF 87

The ENCRR was formulated in three phases following the operationalization of REED+:

Phase I, corresponding to the preparation of the Warsaw Framework, has been fulfilled and is always monitored. In Phase II, which began with this phase, the policy framework related to the conservation of natural forests was also being implemented. Phase III begins with the execution of demonstration projects in the areas of forest restoration and sustainable management of resources, as well as the activities associated with the activities that address the underlying causes of forest loss, with the aim of achieving results consistent with the objectives set out.

The ENCRR establishes eight activities containing 26 action measures, which are intended to address the drivers of deforestation, degradation, depletion of forests and other vegetation resources, as well as those barriers that hinder sustainable management, enrichment and regeneration of vegetation resources. These activities include: afforestation and reforestation; sustainable management, enrichment and regeneration of vegetation resources; farm and livestock management for protection of vegetation resources; prevention and control of forest fires; promotion of the use of non-timber forest products (NTFPs); land tenure security; cropland management measures that include legal issues, regulatory enforcement, outreach and environmental education.

The estimated total budget for administration and implementation of the activities included in the ENCRR is US$ 433 million, for a planning horizon of nine years. This budget includes unconditional costs of 10% of the budget (US$ 43.3 million) to cover administrative costs, as well as conditional costs that are subject to costs subject to availability of funding from various national and international sources: MINAGRI, CONAF and other public institutions; bilateral donors; multilateral donors; and private sector. The ENCRR will be executed and coordinated the implementation of the ENCRR, maintaining close coordination with the Inter-American Development Bank (IADB).

The entire estimated budget for the implementation of the ENCRR will generate as main concrete results: 148,000 hectares planted/ reforested; 30,000 hectares restored (16,000 hectares associated with affected areas by forest fires); 25,000 hectares intervened under sustainable management plans and 75,000 hectares associated with the promotion of NTFPs; 40,000 hectares associated with land tenure security; 30,000 hectares associated to the international commitments of the country; in addition for strengthening of areas such as institutional organization and capacity building, technical assistance, seedling planting, capacity building or transfer of skills, environmental education, awareness rising and research.

Due to the limited availability of resources to support the implementation phase of the ENCRR, funds raised from the sale of carbon credits will be used to finance the activities included in this proposal. In addition, it is expected to trigger public-private financing that achieves greater environmental impacts and social benefits for the country. The ENCRR also seeks to contribute to the implementation of the forest sector actions of Chile's NDC and the ENCRR. The use of the proceeds from the sale of carbon credits will be subject to the approval of the National Council.

As indicated in the ENCRR, adaptation and mitigation activities include elements of innovation in its design, especially changes and improvements in operational practices of afforestation, restoration and sustainable management of forests; as well as the inclusion of new actors (small and medium-sized private sector) and inter-institutional work, with the inclusion of the private sector (including small and medium sized owners, private companies and cooperatives), which is expected to trigger a paradigm shift that catalyzes the long-term impacts of the project.

* * *

**The ENCRR was formulated in three phases following the operationalization of REED+**

Phase I, corresponding to the preparation of the Warsaw Framework, has been fulfilled and is always monitored. In Phase II, which began with this phase, the policy framework related to the conservation of natural forests was also being implemented. Phase III begins with the execution of demonstration projects in the areas of forest restoration and sustainable management of resources, as well as the activities associated with the activities that address the underlying causes of forest loss, with the aim of achieving results consistent with the objectives set out.

The ENCRR establishes eight activities containing 26 action measures, which are intended to address the drivers of deforestation, degradation, depletion of forests and other vegetation resources, as well as those barriers that hinder sustainable management, enrichment and regeneration of vegetation resources. These activities include: afforestation and reforestation; sustainable management, enrichment and regeneration of vegetation resources; farm and livestock management for protection of vegetation resources; prevention and control of forest fires; promotion of the use of non-timber forest products (NTFPs); land tenure security; cropland management measures that include legal issues, regulatory enforcement, outreach and environmental education.

The estimated total budget for administration and implementation of the activities included in the ENCRR is US$ 433 million, for a planning horizon of nine years. This budget includes unconditional costs of 10% of the budget (US$ 43.3 million) to cover administrative costs, as well as conditional costs that are subject to costs subject to availability of funding from various national and international sources: MINAGRI, CONAF and other public institutions; bilateral donors; multilateral donors; and private sector. The ENCRR will be executed and coordinated the implementation of the ENCRR, maintaining close coordination with the Inter-American Development Bank (IADB).

The entire estimated budget for the implementation of the ENCRR will generate as main concrete results: 148,000 hectares planted/ reforested; 30,000 hectares restored (16,000 hectares associated with affected areas by forest fires); 25,000 hectares intervened under sustainable management plans and 75,000 hectares associated with the promotion of NTFPs; 40,000 hectares associated with land tenure security; 30,000 hectares associated to the international commitments of the country; in addition for strengthening of areas such as institutional organization and capacity building, technical assistance, seedling planting, capacity building or transfer of skills, environmental education, awareness rising and research.

Due to the limited availability of resources to support the implementation phase of the ENCRR, funds raised from the sale of carbon credits will be used to finance the activities included in this proposal. In addition, it is expected to trigger public-private financing that achieves greater environmental impacts and social benefits for the country. The ENCRR also seeks to contribute to the implementation of the forest sector actions of Chile's NDC and the ENCRR. The use of the proceeds from the sale of carbon credits will be subject to the approval of the National Council.

As indicated in the ENCRR, adaptation and mitigation activities include elements of innovation in its design, especially changes and improvements in operational practices of afforestation, restoration and sustainable management of forests; as well as the inclusion of new actors (small and medium-sized private sector) and inter-institutional work, with the inclusion of the private sector (including small and medium sized owners, private companies and cooperatives), which is expected to trigger a paradigm shift that catalyzes the long-term impacts of the project.

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL (GREEN CLIMATE FUND) PAGE 31 OF 67

The allocation described above is based on three criteria, corresponding to:

- Equity: To homogenize and allow the normal implementation of ENCORE's action measures in the regions, taking into account the different capacities for reducing emissions (availability of plant resources, etc.), affecting positively or negatively this first distribution, a financial having will be provided to each region according to its capacity for reducing emissions. This will also apply to the owners themselves involved, without limiting it to the specific capacities for reducing emissions, but also to their other activities.
- Efficiency: To encourage the correct use of resources in the territory, prioritizing the most cost-efficient activities of the ENCORE, as well field-operation; a distribution criteria will be considered based on the performance generated at regional level (Performance Allocation, 20% of the total).
- Solidarity: To ensure that no region is penalized by phenomena that affect their regional performance associated to emission reduction and/or increasing forest carbon sequestration for existing forests. In this sense, a buffer allocation will be considered to alleviate these situations by avoiding that a region suffering from these phenomena reduces its emissions below the level of the reference year (Reference Allocation, 20% of the total).

In addition, the distribution criteria are based on the net ERG generated in each region; therefore the risk is minimal. All assignments are based on the net ERG generated at the regional level as shown in the table:

| Region | ER ENCOUR | Total | Performance | Buffer |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Masule | 572,309 | 146,380 | 40% | 0.5% |
| Bobio | 7,152,391 | 10% | 6.0% |
| Aracruz | 1,000,000 | 10% | - |
| Los Rios | 318,088 | 10% | 0.1% |
| TOTAL | 9,452,778 | 100% | 50% | 100% |

Considering these values, a positive performance is considered if the region presents in net balance captures or positive balances. Performance is assigned at regional level.

The project will support the establishment of the regional REDD+ Groups with the expanded membership-with the aim of ensuring that the activities carried out are covered by the SEREMI de Agricultura in each of the regions and will be composed of the SEREMI of Environment, CONAF, INFOR, INDAP, SAG, SERNATUR, etc. (the entities that they enjoy), Private sector, Civil Society and Academy, all relevant actors within the regional forestry sector.

The regional REDD+ Groups will provide transparency and efficiency to the distribution of resources according with the Benefit Sharing System™. CREDECs, decentralized working groups existing at the regional level in each of the regions, will be responsible for the implementation of the activities identified as part of the actions to be carried out in each of the region after the prioritization carried out by the REDD+ Groups.

The allocations per regions will be divided in the two equal parts, the first half for activities identified by

### Output 3: Direct implementation in territory

Under this output, the project in the territory will be carried out for 6 years and support the following actions of the ENCERY:

- Afforestation and reforestation program (MT-6): This activity's objective is to create permanent native vegetation cover that generates ecosystems services and enhances carbon stocks.
- Strengthening the Ecological Restoration Program (MT-5): The objective of this activity is the recovery and restoration of degraded forest areas, including those affected by fire or other forms of degradation processes.
- Programme of restoration of affected ecosystems post Forest Fire (IF-2): Activity aimed at the implementation of ecological restoration projects in areas affected by forest fires.
- Preventive forestry program with emphasis on rural areas (IF-3): Activity for the implementation of preventive management controls in native forests, plantations and other vegetation formations to reduce the risk of fire occurrence or spread, as well as to reduce the potential damage of the fire acting in advance on the vegetation.
- Installation of wood energy plants and strengthening of the wood energy program (U3.1): A functional forest management model will be designed, consistent with the infrastructural requirements of forest areas and other natural resources, including those affected by desertification, land degradation and drought and multi-crisis pressure on vegetation resources, as well as to promote the use of wood for energy.

These actions will be implemented in public or private areas, thus providing associative actions in different territories that promote a sustainable market for the native forest and generating new emission reductions, protecting environmental and social resources (see example of funding for different activities in Figure below).

The beneficiaries of this financing and who are associated with the private land must correspond to small or medium owners, as established in Law No. 20/231.
