## C.2.S. Implementation arrangements

List and describe the institutions involved in the activities that will be funded with proceeds from this pilot programme, and explain their anticipated roles and interactions with one another, including the flow of funds.

The Government of Chile, through CONAF has requested FAO technical assistance for the design and implementation of the REDD-plus RBP. The project is to be implemented by FAD as the executing entity for this project. Responding to this request, FAO will serve both as Accredited Entity and Executing Entity.

As an accredited entity of the GCF, FAO’s overall role is to provide oversight and quality assurance through its Headquarters and the Chilean Regional Office. FAD will carry out both operational and administrative support activities, as well as advisory and technical support functions during the implementation of the Project. As Executive Entity, FAD will be responsible for carrying out operational and administrative support activities which include the provision of the following services:

- Payments, disbursements and other financial transactions.
- Recruitment of staff, project personnel, and consultants.
- Procurement of goods and services, including equipment.
- Organization of training activities, conferences, and workshops, including fellowships.
- Transportation, accommodation, and subsistence for participants in events.
- Shipment, customs clearance, vehicle registration, and accreditation, among others.

GCF and FAO will enter into a FAD, under which FAD shall administer the relevant GCF Proceeds to be used for the financial transactions related to the Project. The GCF funds will be disbursed directly by FAD in accordance with the GCF Proceeds in the project directly and will be implementing under FAD’s Direct Implementation modality.

FAO may also enter into agreements with other organizations and entities which may carry out project activities, including those that are not accredited to GCF. These arrangements will be made to enhance the effectiveness of the implementation of the Project. These entities are accountable directly to Executing Entity and will be selected by FAO and CONAF, as per FAD Rules and Regulations.

FAO may use the following instruments when entering into agreements with other partners:

- UN To An Agreement: used when it exists the need of transferring funds from FAO to another UN Agency for the purpose of programmatic activities.
- Letter of Intent: a non-binding arrangement with academic or nonprofit organizations to obtain specific technical services that cannot exceed USD 500,000 and ruled by the Procurement regulations.
- Contracts: is a contractual arrangement with private institutions to purchase goods or specific

FAO will act in close coordination with CONAF as the technical entity responsible for forest public policies and the ENCCVR, in addition to its status as a National Focal Point to the CNULD and the CINCNIC’s REDD+ approach. The project will be implemented at the national level through the GEF Project Office (GPO) in Chile,

## International competition. The specific responsibilities of the Project Board include:

- Provide overall guidance and direction to the project, ensuring it remains within any specified constraints.

- Provide input on risk management, review risks, and agree on possible countermeasures and management actions to address specific risks;
- Agree on the project budget;

The Board shall be composed of FAO, the Designated National Authority (DNA) and CONAP and their respective alternates. The DNA shall be represented by the Director General of CONAP. The Chairperson will be the main executing agency of the project, chairing and organizing its meetings at least once a year or at the request of the Board.

## intra-ministerial Technical Committee on Climate Change (CTCC)

The CTCC will be a Technical reviewer of the Project and will have within its functions the following tasks: i) the strategic and technical orientation of the project; and ii) the review of the final version of the progress reports. 

The CTCC is composed of all MINAGRI services including representatives of ODEPA, CNR, SAG, INDAP, GREN, CONAF, INFOR, DGR, Agroforests, FIA and is coordinated by ODEPA.

## Project Management Unit (PMU)

Under the overall guidance of the Project Board, according with the technical guidance of the CTCC, the Project Management Unit (PMU) will be responsible for overseeing the day-to-day execution of Project Activities. The PMU will be led by a Project Manager who will be supported by a Project Team and other personnel including the direct supervision of project activities subcontracted to specialists and other institutions, as well as those that are to be implemented through the MRA, if applicable; (ii) coordinating the management of financial resources for the implementation of the project; (iii) ensuring the quality assurance of the project by preparing management reports for the Project Board, intra-ministerial Technical Committee on Climate Change and other stakeholders; (iv) providing technical support to the project team in implementing the REDD+ Project, if required and based on inputs from the Project M&E plan; (v) promoting inter-institutional linkages; and (vi) disseminating project results.

The PMU will consist of one Project Manager, Project Technical Advisors (PTA), Administrative Finance Assistant (AFA) and a Project Coordinator responsible for specific deliverables and hired with GCF resources. The PTA will include a Safeguards Specialist to support the PM in overseeing the implementation of the ESME, the Gender Active Plan (GAP) and the Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework (IPP). FAO will require certification that those persons have completed the Environmental and Social Risk Management Training

### C2.6 Non-carbon benefits:

Provide information on the non-carbon benefits associated with the implementation of REDD+ activities, especially in relation to the conservation and sustainable use of forest resources, and providing evidence to this regard.

Since the beginning of the cycle of demonstration projects of implementation, Chile has considered the development of a strategy in areas where not only carbon capture is enhanced but also where the multiple benefits of the forest are guaranteed. From the beginning of these works, three indicators of environmental co-benefits were identified: (1) improve the regulation of water resources, as well as the water quality; (2) promote soil conservation and increase the fertility of soils; (3) improve the biodiversity of ecosystems.

In this approach, some of the experiences of demonstration projects have been carried out in rural areas that have a Rural Water Management System (SMA), which allows for the capture of rainwater in basins located in springs and distribute it to downstream communities. The committee has its own internal governance structure, which is responsible for managing the resources captured by the SMA.

In addition to this governance structure, forest areas were restored upstream of the SMA facilities, contributing to the three mentioned indicators: water regulation, soil conservation, and biodiversity increase. Based on the analysis of fragmentation indices, it was estimated that these restoration actions contributed favorably to these indicators.

The quantitative economic valuation of the benefits of the ENCVC was undertaken considering the following flows of benefits for 5 regions: carbon capture, water provision, timber production, non-timber forest products (NTFP), and biodiversity. The results show a net benefit in all five regions, with the highest restoration MTC Forest management Programme (USL) and Reforestation (MFA). A with-ENCVC and without-ENCVC scenario was considered for each of these flows. #### D.1. Impact potential

Evaluating the potential of the programme to contribute to the achievement of the Fund's objectives and results areas.


From 2014 and 2016, Chile has generated significant REDD+ results for the following REDD+ activities: deforestation reduction, degradation reduction, carbon stock increase and conservation. In total, this amounts to 18,694,525 tCO2e, making Chile transition from an almost carbon neutral country to one of these activities, into a significant net sink for carbon.

## D.2 Paradigm shift potential

Describe the degree to which the REDD+ activity could catalyze impact beyond a one-off programme/initiative.

According to the 2016 GIG Inventory of Chile, the LULUCF sector represented 35.8% of the GHG emissions reductions achieved in 2014. The REDD+ initiative has led to the adoption of a new Segal framework as well as positive incentives in line with REDD+ objectives, such as the Law 2036 on the Recovery of Native Forests and the Provisions for the Conservation and Sustainable Management of Native Forests. These provisions expanded the inspection functions of the GMRM, improved land planning and introduced measures to improve forest management. The law also established a new framework for the conservation of native forests by establishing the Conservation, Recovery and Sustainable Management of Native Forest Fund, as well as fiscal incentives.

While it should be noted that REDD+ cannot directly be attributed to specific policies or measures, the aforementioned reformed legal framework and its implementation is likely to have contributed, alongside other factors, to the observed results.

The analysis of drivers of deforestation concludes that most of the deforestation and degradation is linked to the unsustainable use of forest for production, followed by forest fires and the expansion of land for agriculture and livestock. The main causes are: lack of tenure security; weak enforcement of laws and regulations by institutions; deficient economic model for native forest production; lack of producer associations; low economic incentives (prices); poor planning and management of forests; and insufficient technical assistance. These started to be addressed through Law 2036, including economic incentives (bonuses), the technical support (assistance) provided by the GMRM, and the creation of a fund for the conservation and sustainable use of native forest.

Between 2009 and 2016, the newly Conservation, Recovery and Sustainable Management of Native Forest Fund was established. This fund has been allocated to projects corresponding to small land farmers; in the first two years, it covered over the substantial reference levels. The total sum of investments in these regions was 79 million pesos.

In the ENCVR, a goal of 264,000 hectares of implementation in the territory is proposed, which is aligned with the goals established in the NDC for the sector Land Use and Land Use Change (LULUCF), which establishes that Chile will reduce its emissions by 15% below business as usual. The main objective in both cases is to generate a reduction of GHG emissions, which presents the first major challenge changes in the Chilean forestry sector, which has been linked due to the positioning of the wood and cellulose industry.

Therefore, to achieve the objectives set out in the ENCVR, the GMRM could contribute to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 15% below business as usual. This would be part of the full national commitments established in the NDC. In addition, the proposed actions include important elements of innovation and adaptation in the design and operational practices of afforestation, restoration and


---

management, which are expected to trigger a paradigm shift that catalyzes the long-term impacts of the project.

This offers an opportunity for all the administrative regions of the country and the country as a whole to test the new institutional arrangements in six out of the sixteen regions of the country. It is also expected to generate a significant amount of environmental, social and economic co-benefits, which will make it possible to demonstrate that the use of resources can contribute to the improvement in the quality of life of local actors, in the improvement of management practices of native vegetation forests, in the protection of biodiversity and in the preservation of the natural heritage and the change of these resources and their communities.

In the execution of the territory, the reinvestment of financing from the GCF is sought to be a trigger for investment in the territories. ### D.5 Sustainable development potential

Describe the wider benefits or priorities, including environmental, social and economic considerations in mind:

- **Environmental co-benefits:** Native forests constitute an ecological, genetic and even cultural national heritage that, in many respects, is unique worldwide. Similarly, native plant forest play a role as environmental services by providing water resources, soil conservation, carbon sequestration, maintaining regular water flows, allowing the slow melting of snow and ice and general orderly and regular delivery of rainwater.

- **Economic co-benefits:** The native forest has a high productive potential. It is - it takes in comparative terms - one of the most important resources for the country. Its productivity can be between 20 to 7 m3 ha / year. Therefore, by encouraging its management and exploitation. One can stop saying that a significant part of the GDP could be added to this net effect in forest plantations.

- **Social co-benefits:** The native forest and firewood have a double significance both for the forests and for the rural inhabitants: the production of wood and the regular regeneration allows them to obtain their energy supply, in an environment where there are no other sources.

- **Educational on benefits:** The proposed resources are directed to be supportive and enabling activities at least in the following areas: (1) awareness raising; (2) training and capacity building programs. These actions will promote sustainability in the long-term of the solutions being implemented.

The Law of native forest supports reforestation and firewood planning for timber and fuelwood production purposes, creating economic incentives for the development and conservation of the native forest, addressing the needs of forest landowners and rural inhabitants.

The proposed investments in the present project seek to provide the sustainability of the policies and actions triggered by the Native Forest Law and that were significant for the achievement of the reported mitigation results by Chile.

- Reduction in the occurrence of fires in areas of urban-rural interfaces through the management actions promoted by the ENCVR to lower the probability of risk that affects the life of the population.

- Training and learning of the owners with technical knowledge that will allow them to be better qualified for the productive-sustainable management of their properties.

## D.4. Seeds of the recipient

### Describe the vulnerability and financing needs of the beneficiary country and population.

Chile is vulnerable to climate change because it meets seven of the nine characteristics of vulnerability according to Article 4 of the UNFCCC, of which four are linked to the objectives of the ENCCR, which correspond:

- Areas prone to natural disasters
- Areas prone to drought and desertification
- Arid areas with low rainfall and areas prone to forest degradation
- Fragile ecosystems, including mountainous ecosystems

Reducing the vulnerability of fragile ecosystems as well as increasing resilience based on the implementation of the action measures that the owners decide to execute within the framework of the ENCCR and establishing new forests, is directly related to and contributes to the provisions of the 5th Evaluation Report of the Interdepartmental Group for Climate Change (GIEC) of Chile. The GIEC has identified as a priority the conservation of fragile ecosystems, in particular for fisheries and aquaculture, water resources, biodiversity, the forestry agricultural sector, climate change adaptation, and the promotion of sustainable development.

Communications to the Secretariat of the UNFCCC and are being duly internalized in the National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAPCC).

The ENCCR will contribute to reducing social vulnerability in the areas with the largest rural population in the country, which coincidentally contain the largest forest formations at the national level; this because, according to the data of the Ministry of Agriculture, 80% of the forests are located in these areas. These areas are mainly in the rural sectors of the country, concentrating mainly between the Maule and Aysén regions. It should be mentioned that these areas are inhabited by indigenous peoples and non-indigenous rural communities, adding the gender factor, and even more so if it is an indigenous woman.

With a comprehensive approach and with the intention of favoring the most vulnerable groups since the formulation of the ENCCR, the following international agreements and conventions have been considered: the UNFCCC, the Convention on Biological Diversity, the Ramsar Convention, the World Heritage Convention, the World Bank’s Operational Policies, the UN-REDD Program guidelines, among others; in addition to the current national legislation that includes the Forest Code, the Water Law, the National Forest Policy, and the Law of 28,500 on citizen participation. ILO Convention 169. Transparency Law 2028. Indigenous Laws. Laws related to indigenous peoples.

It should be noted that the most vulnerable groups in Chile correspond to indigenous peoples and rural local communities, together with the gender factor that increases socioeconomic vulnerability. In relation to indigenous peoples, there is a total of 235,792 inhabitants, which corresponds to 12.0% according to

### D.5 Country ownership

The project is consistent with national environmental policies and the agreements acquired by the country with instances such as the UNESCO and UNCCD. The project, in turn, contributes to the reduction of CO2 emissions through the conservation and sustainable management of Forests, which are natural carbon sinks with vegetation resources. The ENCVR builds upon the framework of the Law on Native Forests to enhance its impact.

For this, COMAF is in charge of the coordination and implementation of the actions and the necessary work to be able to carry out the ENCVR; it also has a long history, technical knowledge and presence in all regions of the country. The project will be implemented through the National Development Agency (AND).

In the development of the ENCVR, since its inception had the support and participation of civil society, ensuring respect for environmental and social safeguards throughout the process and to date.

The NDA has led the definition and development of this proposal by providing feedback. In particular, the NDA has been involved in the preparation of the draft project document and provided technical recommendations through the convening of a Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) for the review of the draft project document. The TAP recommended that the NDA issue the non-object letter.

### D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The results reported by Chile are reflections of a first wave of implementation of policies, programmes and actions for the conservation and sustainable management of Forests. While important, these measures have been implemented in a piecemeal fashion without a clear vision or strategy to guide their development and lessons have been learned and incorporated into the ENCVR of this proposal.

It can be argued that the incentives generated by the Law have so far allowed the current tranches of landowners to maintain their Forests, but it is not yet possible for them to enable additional areas to reach forest landowners to receive support. Actions to promote land-owner associations are an example of enabling actions that could help in this regard.

The effect of the new legal framework can also be seen in the increased budget of the COMAF, which has been increased by 10% since 2014 and the average budget for the period 2015-2016 was US$3.9 million. While for the period between 2008 and 2013 it was US$2.9 million.

As a very rough guide to the cost-effectiveness of the actions implemented in Chile between 2014 and 2016, the average investment per ton of CO2 avoided is US$5.75. The average cost for the period 2008-2013 was US$9.5.

REDD+ Technical Annex amounts to some $16.25 US dollars per ton.

### E.12. For the use of proceeds

#### The System of Safeguards of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) is based on the Performance Standards on Environmental and Social Sustainability (PSS) of the International Financial Corporation of the World Bank Group, which are also known as the IFC Performance Standards.

REDD+ policies in the period prior to the work of the FGB of the WBG, this is the years 2014, 2015 and 2016. The identification and evaluation of the main impacts is based on the cabinet study of the main documents associated with the project, including the Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA), national legislation, national regulations, and interviews with representatives of the government, civil society, indigenous peoples and NGOs.

The preliminary environmental and social risk rating of the evaluation in line with the GCF categories is category B; However, a more detailed review of the process in line with the GCF safeguards still is pending in order to provide greater support in the appreciation of the category and the possible social and environmental impacts expected.

The proposal includes a dedicated Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) (See Annex 1) providing that all relevant Based Payments for REDD+ Small Zone (referred to as BPSZ) projects will be addressed in accordance with FAO ESS and GCF PSS Performance Standards. The purpose of the FAO ESMF is to ensure that environmental and social risks are identified, assessed and managed throughout the project cycle.

Projects are not determined at the onset of the project; the ESIA is a living document that needs to be updated during the project implementation phase in order to reflect any changes in the project design. The ESMF serves as a practical tool to guide identification and mitigation of potential negative environmental and social impacts of proposed investments and serve as a platform for consultations with stakeholders and potential project beneficiaries.

According to FAO Environment and Social Management Guidelines screening to identify specific environmental and social risks associated with individual projects. For each project, at project identification stage, FAO uses its own Environmental and Social Risk Assessment (ESRA) tool to assess the potential adverse impacts of the project classifying the project risk. Based on the project activities and on the nature and significance of potential environmental and social impacts, the proposed project is classified as Moderate.

In the context of the mandatory requirements for ensuring environmental and social performance of FAO projects throughout the life of a project and optimizing sustainability and equity post-project and based on the assessment of the potential adverse environmental and social impacts of potential environmental and social impacts, the proposed project is classified as Moderate.

Between 27 June and 1 July 2019, Information and Participation Workshops were held within the framework of the application to the Green Climate Fund. The date and city where each of the workshops were held are as follows:

- June 27, Temozón, Aracataca region.
- July 2, Puerto Montt, Los Lagos region.
- July 3, San Cristóbal, Los Ríos region.
- July 9, Concepción, Biobío region.

The objective of the workshops was to inform and receive feedback from the relevant actors regarding the mechanisms and criteria for the equitable, transparent and efficient distribution of the resources obtained in the Participatory Process (PP) and the Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA), as well as the risks associated with the implementation of the activities to be financed through the resources obtained by this project.

The workshops were part of the participatory process that the ENCERY contemplated since its formation, allowing all interested parties, especially vulnerable actors, to be informed and consulted. In this case, though, the distribution criteria for the results of the PP and ESIA were not yet defined, thus affecting the distribution arrangements in the payment for results phase, thus ensuring the environmental and social inclusion of the communities.

A core team was formed for the planning and development of the workshops, composed of professionals from the Climate Change and Environmental Services Unit or CONAF's General Offices, Indigenous and Social Affairs Unit (UAS), CONAF's Regional Directors, representatives of the National Forest Service (SERNATRE), CONAF (Los Ríos and Los Lagos) and regional support professionals.

The methodology used in each workshop was based on the guidelines established in the Plan for the Implementation of the Participatory Process (PP) and ESIA, the Guide to the Environmental and Indigenous Assessment, of the ENCERY; a guide document for the participatory process in the preparation phase of the ENCERY. This consideration responds to several objectives: on the one hand, it ensures the coherence of the process with the previous one; on the other hand, it ensures that the actors involved in the process will be addressed; it ensures that they are people who know the initiative. In addition, it will be possible to identify the main actors and their roles in the formulation phase of the ENCVCY, as well as the main measures of the ENCVCY, and finally, it complies with the commitment established during the formulation, regarding considering participation also in the implementation phase of the ENCVCY.

Participation was organized through a workshop. A minimum representation of women on the day of the workshop is at least 30%.

The constitution of the Map of Actors must also consider local, sub-regional and regional geographical representation; since, due to their scope of action, the former tend to make operational proposals, the sub-regional strategic type and the latter have a more political vision.

A participation between 30 and 60 people per workshop was defined. This number is agreed to facilitate the initiation of discussions and analysis in focus groups.

### 2. Focus group settings

According to the map of actors in each region, the configuration of the focus groups was established considering that each of them was representative of a sector of the population/sector. Each group consists of 5 to 10 people from different sectors. The composition of these groups was defined during the preparation for each of the workshops; the formation of specific focus groups of Indigenous Peoples and women were considered.

### 3. Workshop development

The planning and development team developed a logic for the workshops, defining the content information (national and regional), the contexts that would be subject to discussion and the participatory activities through which the feedback on the design and implementation of the Benefit Distribution System in the ENCVCY would be collected.

In this context, participants actively carried out what allowed them to gather the views of the attendees regarding the causes of deforestation, degradation and non-increase of forest cover in their region (prioritizing from 1 to 5), as well as the main measures that could be defined in the ENCVCY. We also consulted on aspects related to governance, benefit distribution of financing and potential risks in the implementation of activities for each country.

### 4. Preliminary results

A total of 157 people (64 women [41%] and 54 representatives of indigenous peoples [29%]) participated in the 5 workshops. In this context, it should be noted that the Maule region does not register indigenous communities or associations.

The invitations, based on the map of actors made in each region, in general met the proposed objectives in terms of participation recording; on average, an attendance of 30 people. This allowed the creation of between 4 to 5 focus groups per workshop, which were composed by sector and gender (women and men).

Forestry extension consultants [3 academic public institutions], NGOs and private.

Regarding participatory activities, forest fires were highlighted as one of the main causes of deforestation, degradation and non-increase in forest cover. However, the active measure that was prioritized by the actors

Regarding the potential risks and impacts identified in the implementation of activities, it is possible to detect in the first instance, that risks related to governance are identified (hierarchy in the processes of distribution of resources, lack of transparency in the decision-making process), risks related to the effectiveness of the actions to be implemented), environmental (low availability of seeds and plants, use of sensitive species, low participation of women and social (not considering the participation of neighboring, indigenous, local and / or women, labor shortage).

Please note that this document is a summary. You can find more detailed information on the attached documents to the feedback you provide.

Risk factors associated with project implementation include mainly technical and operational, institutional, political and social-environmental aspects (Environmental and Social assessment):

- The risks related to technical and operational capacities may impact the collection and dissemination of climate information, as well as the collection of local data necessary for monitoring and reporting.
- The risk is also related to the capacity of project staff and key personnel in implementing agencies and government departments could impact on the quality of the project implementation.
- An institutional risk is linked to the coordination among agencies and stakeholders that could lead to inefficiencies or delays. This risk may be mitigated by ensuring that all relevant actors are involved, as governments may lack the capacity to understand and support the project, limiting the needed support to technical assistance.
- Risks related to limited interest or capacities of family farmer beneficiaries can limit the adoption of technologies and practices.
- A political risk is a potential change in government at the national and local levels leading to a lack of continuity for the project implementation.
- Social and environmental risks include social issues in the project areas. External factors such as climate and climate shocks can negatively impact small farmers’ investments and forest production.

The proposed project includes several mitigation measures to address these risks. The mitigation strategies include training and capacity building, awareness raising of key stakeholders including government officials, clear communication with local communities, strengthening of institutional coordination mechanisms for prevention training, and farm plans and provision of climate information to help farmers prepare for climate shocks. These are the main risk factors that need to be mitigated.

number 2, which states "Equality before the Law (...) men and women are equal before the law", (National Congress, 2014). Additionally, Law No. 28/09/2012, establishes minimum standards for the implementation of gender equality in public administration. Article no.1 states that every State administration body, within their field of competition, will be responsible for implementing and monitoring the application of the principles of equality before the law, non-discrimination, the enjoyment and exercise of their legitimate rights and freedoms recognized by the Political Constitution, international treaties, and international laws and treaties ratified by Chile which are currently in force" (National Congress, 2014).

This called for a new institutional framework for gender matters to bridge any persisting gaps, improve legislation, and ensure that all public policies and programs are consistent with the principles of equality covered by the Convention (CEDAW). Against that backdrop, Law No. 28/0230, creating the Ministry for Women and Gender Equity, was approved in 2015 (CEDAW, 2016). Law No. 28/0230, establishes in its article no.1, the Ministry's responsibilities for implementing gender equality policies and programs, as well as for monitoring their application. The law also creates a National Gender Equality Council (CNEG) to advise on the implementation of public policies and programs destined "to promote gender equality, equality of rights and ensure the removal of all types of discrimination against women and girls". The CNEG is composed by 15 members appointed for four years each. It states that "gender equality conditions the identical or differential treatment between men and women and results in a total alteration of the social structure and the distribution of power, resources and opportunities, as well as of enjoyment and exercise of all their human rights" (National Congress, 2015).

Over the last few years, Chile has made good strides in promoting gender equality. According to the ranking of the Global Gender Gap Report 2016 by the World Economic Forum, Chile has an index score of 0.734, ranking it 54 out of 149 countries (World Economic Forum, 2018). [1] The country has taken concrete actions to promote gender equality in accordance with the National Strategic Plan for Gender Equality 2016-2026 and Development Goals" and the commitments of the Regional Conference on Women in Latin America and the Caribbean. However, despite these advances, a number of challenges remain. A recent study revealed the persistence of discrimination and of obstacles to full equality of opportunity between men and women and girls in Chile. The study also highlighted the need for a more comprehensive and holistic geographic approach to different dimensions of gender equality.

### E3.2. For the use of proceeds

Provide adequate and sufficient information on how the AE will undertake activity-level gender assessments and action plans once the details of the activities become known.

Chile has made progress in recent years in integrating gender perspectives and awareness into forest policies and REDD+ actions. The Chilean Forest Law (Ley de Bosques) was revised in 2013, which includes several references to gender/women, which reflects the increasing awareness and commitment to integrate gender considerations into forest policy.

In Chile, the progress of mainstreaming gender into the ENCBV has been based on a widespread participatory process that included proposals of all sectors of society, focusing on women, indigenous peoples, and vulnerable groups. The process was carried out in 2013-2014 through a series of workshops at the territorial coverage; the key stakeholders engaged, the methodology used, the characteristics of the implementation of the ENCBV, and the results achieved.

All of these are unprecedented measures for the formulation of public policies that seek to represent the country's gender diversity in forest management.

Through this participative process, gender considerations and women’s and indigenous peoples demands, regarding the causes of degradation and deforestation, were integrated into the ENCBV design and action measures. The Gender Action Plan (GAP) was developed as a result of these discussions. It includes actions for indigenous and non-indigenous rural communities, indigenous women, small and medium-sized forest landowners, and women in urban areas.

The Gender Action Plan aims to support the effective participation of women within the ENCBV’s implementation process through gender-sensitive project frameworks (gender-responsive project frameworks) and leadership in ensuring gender considerations into REDD+ actions. In the gender action plan, the monitoring of all activities implemented will be carried out by the AE, with the objective of evaluating the effectiveness of women's participation. Other gender indicators have also been defined with regard to the ENCBV action measures that include the promotion of women’s leadership in forest management and the strengthening of women’s soil management and preventive forestry activities, which will be implemented directly in the territory. The indicator will be measured through participatory monitoring and evaluation (PME) tools.

E4.1. For the period of the achieved results

Provide appropriate and sufficient information to demonstrate that no Prohibited Practices occurred during the implementation of the project, including money laundering and financing of terrorism, which occurred during the implementation of projects based on the provisions of Article 3 of Act No. 19513.

The Chilean Financial Intelligence Unit (Unidad de Análisis Financiero – “UAF”) was created by Law No.19.913. The UAF is an independent, public legal person, with its own patrimony and related to the Chilean Government through a contract of representation. The UAF has a Board of Directors, which includes a National Director, who is appointed by the President of the Nation. The UAF is a member of the Eftpos Group.

The UAF has the duty of receiving, requesting, analyzing and forwarding to the competent criminal prosecution authorities, as well as to the competent judicial authorities, information related to money laundering jurisdiction regarding the prevention and investigation of terrorism financing. The UAF can also provide information on other matters that may be requested by the competent authorities.

Article 3 of Act No. 19513 imposes a duty for all covered subjects – including banks, stock exchanges, casinos, realtors, among many other businesses – to appoint an official or compliance officer responsible for relations with the UAF.

Provide adequate and sufficient information on how the activities to be implemented with the use of proceeds will meet the requirements of the GCF environmental and social safeguards and standards and policies relevant to indigenous peoples and cultural heritage, as well as any other applicable international or national laws and regulations directly applicable to the activities under relevant international treaties and agreements.

The use of proceeds will be in line with the FAO Policy on Indigenous and Tribal Peoples and the FAO Environmental Guidelines for Forestry. The ESF is aligned with the UN Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples (UNDRIP) and Social Safeguard 9 (SSG 9) on Indigenous Peoples and Cultural heritage as indicated in the ESF, as presented in the table below. In addition, the ESF will be aligned with the GCF Environmental and Social Framework (ESF) and GCF accreditation with the GCF. Hence, SSG 9 Indigenous Peoples and Cultural Heritage ensures the approach, respecting indigenous peoples' rights and interests, consistent with national and international policies laws and regulations applicable as well as with the FAO own standards.

The ESMF guarantees that:

- The UN Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples is respected in all GCF's projects and programs.
- Promote the right to self-determination and development with identity of indigenous peoples (rights to decide their own future, including the right to participate fully in decision-making processes).
- Ensure that the rights of indigenous peoples are protected from adverse impacts of FPCs (forestry projects) affected by the project.
- Recognize and respect the rights, lands, natural resources, territories, livelihoods, knowledge, social fabric, traditions, governance systems of Indigenous Peoples.
- Protect the cultural heritage of Indigenous Peoples.

### SSG 9: Recognizes indigenous peoples' traditions and knowledge

SSG 9 recognizes indigenous peoples’ traditions and knowledge present opportunities for many of the challenges that humankind will face in the coming decades. It is of particular significance in relation to indigenous peoples’ traditional ecological knowledge and demand and traditional knowledge with respect to adapting to climate change vulnerabilities and impacts.

Indigenous peoples are an important stakeholder in the region from Made to Los Llanos, yet they share a higher vulnerability than other groups due to their limited access to resources. Their role in natural resource management and poverty alleviation is uncommon if these indigenous peoples’ needs for food security, water, land tenure, and livelihoods are not met.

For this reason, the ESF will be aligned with international legal agreements such as the UN Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples (UNDRIP), adopted by the United Nations General Assembly in 2007. The UNDRIP is a human rights instrument that underpins SSG 9 and provides the ESMF guidance to respect, include and promote indigenous peoples’ issues in

# E. Monitoring and evaluation

Provide information on the monitoring arrangements that will take place for providing annual monitoring reports based on the results of the project.

The execution of the projects in the territory* will be monitored using the procedures described in the Measurement and Monitoring System (MMS) document of the ENCCV*. Following the procedures of the MMS, each site project will be monitored at least once a year. The data collected during these monitoring visits will cover all activities and reported on a biennial basis. These measurements and calculations are going to be included in the Platform for Monitoring and Evaluation (PME) of the ENCCV*, which is being developed by the Center of Information of Natural Resources, CENR and is expected to be fully functional by 2020.

Using the protocol of monitoring of the abovementioned MMS document, each project will have monitoring instruments that will allow for the collection of data on the number of trees per hectare, the base area index, number of species per hectare, the basal area, number of species. With this information, it will be possible to calculate the volume of wood and other indicators related to the progress towards achieving the essential indicators of the goals of the ENCCV*. This information will be complemented by georeferenced digital images that will allow for a more detailed analysis.

The measurement in the field will be carried out by the CENAR's regional offices. The gathered information will be introduced to the Platform of the ENCCV or delivered to the MRE team in the central COF office in Ecuador, which will be responsible for processing and analyzing this data with the aim of evaluating the performance of the projects. The time frame of the monitoring will be from 2020 to 2030, with biennial reports to those authorities.

This system works as a component of the REDD+ monitoring scheme. Efforts to next both systems will be done once a major amount of data is gathered.

Project-level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken in compliance with PAO policies. The project implementation will be monitored on an annual basis, using indicators that are aligned with the project outcomes that culminates with achieving the desired change and the underlying assumptions about how changes can be achieved. These indicators will be used to monitor progress towards achieving the overall monitoring and measurement system to measure the causal and attributable change, and the contribution of the project to the achievement of the goals of the ENCCV*. The indicators will also be used for targeting and verifying the assumptions that the program is making. It should also be used to generate information on the performance of the project in relation to its implementation and planning components. Progress will be measured against baselines, targets and indicators.

The essential indicators that will be monitored correspond to the ones that allow the reporting of the goals of the ENCCV*. These are: forest cover (FC), forest area (FA), forest density (FD), forest age (FA), after forest fires (FF), preventive forestry (PF), forest management (FM) and wood energy (UE) programs, the number of hectares where agroforestry is implemented (AF), the number of hectares where agroecology is implemented (AE), the number of hectares where alternative uses are implemented (AU), the number of hectares where agroecological practices are used (AP), the number of hectares where agroecological management and use of agricultural waste products (AW) as well as for the Adaptation to Climate Change (ACC) program, the number of hectares where genetic resources (GR) is implemented. Monitoring indicators such as the number of trees per hectare and basal area (among others) will allow Chile to contribute a significant amount of REDD+ data.

For the active monitoring related to enabling conditions for the ENCCV implementation, the indicator that will be reported for the environmental education and dissemination program (MTG) will be the number of teachers trained in agroforestry techniques. The indicators for the technological transfer of alternative management and use of agricultural waste products (AWP) as well as for the Adaptation to Climate Change (ACC) program, the number of hectares where genetic resources (GR). The indicator will be the number of contexts where the program is implemented.

At the project level impact, two indicators are proposed in line with GCF investment criteria (impact potential)

FAO will perform monitoring and reporting throughout the reporting period. FAO has a country presence and capacity to support the PM in implementing tools and methods to facilitate management of the project. The methods will support vertical monitoring, from the beneficiaries to management, and will facilitate communication between the two levels. This will be done through regular meetings, progress reports on project activities and develop reports to the Steering Committee that combining financial reporting and progress reports.

The monitoring system will be comprehensive and entail the Project workplan as well as the action plans for gender, indigenous people, biodiversity and the social and environmental framework.

The day-to-day project monitoring and implementation responsibilities rest with an nationally recruited Project Manager (PM) who will be supported by a Monitoring and Evaluation Specialist, who will oversee the PM's Monitoring and Evaluation Unit. The M&E Specialist will coordinate the annual work plan to ensure the effective implementation of the project activities and will provide technical support in addressing any difficulties during implementation, including M&E plans, so that appropriate and corrective measures can be adopted. The M&E Specialist will also provide training on monitoring and evaluation techniques and accountability in monitoring and reporting project results. FAO will support the PM as needed, including through provision of technical assistance and capacity building.

Troubleshooting support will be provided by FAO as needed. The CTRC, OSDC, Regional REDD+ Group, the CORAL team, and other relevant units within FAO will provide technical advice on specific issues.

A project inception workshop will be implemented in order to: a) agree on the project theory of change; b) hold a common understanding on the project strategy and discuss any change in the overall context that might influence the project implementation; c) review the draft work plan with the Steering Committee, including reporting and communication lines; d) review the results framework and discussion, reporting requirements and indicators; e) agree on the annual financial budget, including the financial reporting requirements and; f) planning and scheduling ESC meetings. And if: finalize the first year work plan.

The PM and FAO will provide inputs to the Annual Report for each year of implementation. The PM and the M&E Specialist will ensure that the indicators in the results framework are monitored annually. The Annual report will be submitted by the PM to the Steering Committee within 60 days after the end of calendar year, but it was due to GCF 60 days after the end of calendar year. The final project annual report and the terminal evaluation report will be submitted to the GCF within 12 months from the end of project and final evaluations will be undertaken under the responsibility of FAO Office of Evaluation (OE)[34].

In summary, monitoring and evaluation at the project level will be carried out in accordance with FAO's corporate systems and the evaluation will be undertaken according to the FAO evaluation policy across the project cycle. The monitoring and evaluation system is designed to ensure that the project is managed improving accountability and quality through the principles of results-based management (RBM), while improving coverage from all REDD projects. # QUALITY ASSURANCE IN FAO

## Figure B_QA in FAO

The Project Cycle sets corporate standards and procedures, including quality assurance criteria for phase 1 to 3 of all FAO's projects. These criteria are relevance, feasibility and sustainability which are complemented with the performance assessment during the implementation of the project (Initial Installation, Annual Project Report, Mid-term and Final Evaluation).

The project team and the FAO Country Office will carry out the M&E Plan in accordance with FAO procedures. Performance indicators will be defined for each phase of the project. The M&E plan will also include additional information such as the corresponding means of verification. The M&E plan includes: initial installation, annual work plans, CCF, project implementation reviews, a mid-term and a final evaluation. This information will be available online.

The following sections describe the main elements of this plan. The project M&E plan will be presented and finalized during the inception workshop, with the indicators, means of verification, and a full definition of the M&E responsibilities of the project staff.


## Initial Installation

The Project Inception Workshop will be held within the first 3 months of project start, involving those with assigned roles in the project organization structure: FAO Country Office and, where appropriate/feasible, FAO regional technical policy and technical advisors as well as key stakeholders. The inception Workshop is crucial to building a strong foundation for the project team to plan the first year annual work plan. The Inception Workshop will address several key issues including:

- To assist partners in fully understanding and take ownership of the project.
- To ensure that all parties involved are aware of their roles and responsibilities within FAO Country Office and Regional staff as it vis à vis the project team.
- Discussing the division of tasks, roles and responsibilities within the project decision-making structures, including reporting and communication lines, and conflict resolution mechanisms.
- Based on the project results framework, finalizing the first annual work plan. Review and agree on the indicators, targets and their means of verification, and feedback assumptions and risks.

## Annual Project Report

This important report is prepared by the Project Technical Advisers, consolidated by the Project Manager, reviewed by the Intra-ministerial Technical Committee on Climate Change and final approved by the Project Board for submission to the GCF secretariat prior to the start of the reporting period.

The format and content of the annual report will be based upon the simplified reporting regime which will be established for RBP in the GCF.

## Mid-term evaluation (MTE)

The project will undergo an independent mid-term evaluation at the end point of project implementation. The mid-term evaluation will determine progress towards the achievement of outcomes and will suggest corrective actions if needed. It will focus on the effectiveness, efficiency and timeliness of project implementation; will highlight lessons learned and actions, and will prevent initial lessons learned about project design, implementation and management.

## Final Evaluation

An independent Final Evaluation will be launched within six months prior to project's actual completion date. NTE will aim at identifying project outcomes, their sustainability and actual or potential impacts, including long-term benefits and risks. The evaluation will also serve the purpose of indicating future actions needed to assure continuity of the process developed through the project.

Both mid-term and final evaluation will be managed by FAO Office of Evaluation (DEB) and organized in coordination with the FAO Regional Technical Adviser and the Project Board.

During the final three months, the project team will prepare the Project Final Annual Report. This comprehensive report will cover all aspects of the project implementation. It will summarize the results achieved (objectives, outcomes, outputs), lessons learned, problems met and areas where results have not been achieved. It will also lay out any recommendations or for other steps that may need to be taken to ensure sustainability and replicability of this project's results.

[1] Please refer to FAO OED webpage for further details: http://www.fao.org/about/who-we-are/departments/oed/

# REDD+ RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND | PAGE 54 OF 67

### Covenant that no other party has a competing claim to the results proposed to the GCF in accordance with national, legal or regulatory frameworks.

A national legal definition of emission reductions is not considered to be a precondition to accessing results-based payments. The role of the GCF itself is necessary to transfer the ownership of Emissions Reductions (ERs) to the GCF itself; but rather it is necessary to guarantee that no competing claims to ownership are made by other parties.

In this way, Chile will record in the information hub on the UNFCCC with portfolio the quantity of results obtained between 2014 and 2016 for which payments will be received under the pilot program, expressed in tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e). The GCF will receive these results from Chile through a process being developed and tested. In addition, Chile will transparently record that GCF is the entity paying for results; and emission reductions will be transferred to the GCF.

This task will be guaranteed by CONAF, that according to the national policy and regulations has the mandate as a national entity in charge to implement the ENCRED of Emissions Trading and subscribing ERPA. Official communication from CONAF to the World Bank is attached at Annex 6 of the FIP. The annexes of the FIP are also available on the Chilean Carbon Fund website.

### Ability of CONAF to Transfer Title to Emission Reductions (ERs) in the Carbon Forest of the Fund, Carbon Partnership Facility.

#### Ministry of National Assets issued an official resolution GNA No.152 stating that "CONAF can advance on the third phase, allowing it to subscribe ERs and also recognizing the ability to transfer the ownership of carbon credits".

On March 21st 2017, in the context of the FIP that has been negotiated with the World Bank, the Ministry of National Assets issued an official resolution GNA No.152 mentioning that "after having analyzed the legal and institutional arrangements established at national level and local level with relevant counterparts through bilateral agreements, CONAF can advance on the third phase, allowing it to subscribe these Emissions Reductions Purchase Agreement (ERPA), and distribute the results of the pilot program".

In summary, the official resolution of CONAF as the national agency in charge to manage REDD+ RBP, and the legal and institutional arrangements established at regional and local level with relevant counterparts through bilateral agreements, allow CONAF to advance on the third phase and mitigate the risk of competing ERs among different agencies.

As an additional measure to avoid any risk of competing ERs being issued by different agencies or any other arrangement that might be signed with the GCF of the WBG that the Emissions Reductions Purchase Agreement (ERPA) will take into account mitigation results offered after 2017.

It is important to consider that it is difficult to determine the origins and holders of ERs. To covenant that no other party has a competing claim to the results proposed to the GCF in accordance with national, legal or regulatory frameworks experience gained under the FIP Carbon Fund when developing a program at national level; three general requirements have been analyzed: 1) ensuring that the project activities are not associated with double accounting 2) guaranteeing fair compensation to private forest landowners. 3) implementing measures to ensure that the results of the pilot program are not competing with other forest sources (including agricultural communities and indigenous committees), following specific criteria and requirements.

The distribution of RBP is a follows: 20% of the financial resources that will be received via result-based payments are centralized and managed by CONAF Head Office to cover administrative costs. The remaining RBP will be distributed between the regions where the emission reduction and/or the increase in absorption have been generated during the reference period, in order to finance projects associated with the

**Page 67**

# REDD-plus RBP FUNDING PROPOSAL (GREEN CLIMATE FUND) PAGE 58 OF 67

## Actions carried out under the ENCFCV In particular, REDD+ actions carried out at local level which contributed to EBA are assigned regionally and distributed between regions according to the following criteria:

- **Equity** between regions (50%)
- **Efficiency** in generating EAs and increasing forest carbon stocks (20%)
- **Solidarity** with countries that are disadvantaged socio-economically or that faced special circumstances (free, disasters, pests) (19%)

In light of the current legislation cited, the eligibility requirements of both category of projects that are prioritized via Regional REDD+ Group or regional public competitions, consider the following aspects:

- **Formal requirements**: Legal aspects regarding land ownership of those who apply whether individual or collective rights.
- **Alignment with the action measures of the ENCFCV**: The proposed project activities should be technically and financially sound according to the available resources.

Aspects related to land tenure, such as the elements established by law to regulate proprietary titles over land or forest areas in the country, certainly cover a relevant function; although it is intended that those persons/groups that do not hold tenure rights might also be included in the category of beneficiaries.

The allocation within each region will define the eligibility of areas taking into consideration aspects of commensurability with the national REDD+ strategy and the availability of resources. In addition, the benefit sharing distribution plan will take into account aspects of gender, the presence of indigenous peoples and vulnerable groups, among others.

To conclude, the activities carried out using the RBP funds correspond to those indicated in the funding proposal and in any case they must be aligned with the action measures established under the ENCFCV and the modalities defined by the National REDD+ Strategy. The results will be registered at national level in terms of non-monetary benefits in order to be remunerated in the territory.

The rights and obligations related to RBP are regulated at territorial level through bilateral agreements (convention, agreement, etc.) which will establish the modalities for the transfer of the counterpart, and will contain some elements established within the benefit-sharing distribution plan. The requirements regarding the use of funds (e.g., the need for a very deep geological study) must be clearly defined before they are defined through bilateral agreements.

Regarding use of the emissions reduction, these will be used towards achieving their NDCs. The results achieved in terms of emission reductions will be reported against the NDC commitment under the ENCFCV and its benefit-sharing distribution plan, and the crediting will be registered at national level. The General Director of the National Forest Service (DNF) will be responsible for the registration of the entry of international funds and guarantees compatible with administrative and fiscal rules. GEF will also perform audits to monitor the use of funds internally between the central office of CONAF and the Regional Development Councils.

As mentioned above, the ENCFCV is the key public policy instrument to achieve Chile's NDC objectives; stating that the action measures that have been developed to implement the ENCFCV have effects both on public and private land. The actions carried out under the ENCFCV are not considered as a direct contribution of carbon to carbon accounting, as an individual owner cannot be identified or to demonstrate that he has EBA rights which can be assigned.

Therefore, detailed arrangements have been made under the benefit-sharing plan in order to clarify how the REDD+ benefits will be equally distributed among the beneficiaries who have contributed in generating such payments.

On the other hand, based on current legislation, national assets whose use does not generally belong to the people are called State assets or fiscal assets (article 589 of the civil code). Such goods, especially if derived from nature, cannot be owned by private entities.


---
**Page 68**
---

To implement the ENCGRY, it is foreseen that interested parties may access competitive funds, according to the thematic lines and criteria established in calls for proposals. The thematic lines refer to affiliation with justice and governance, as well as with the Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) and the Operational Manuals for the direct-action projects.

When monetary benefits could be distributed to forest landowners in order to implement certain action measures of the ENCGRY, pre-existing public mechanisms will be used, most of which are supported by the existing legislation.

With regards to the type of forest landowners that will be eligible to receive benefits, "small forest owners" will be prioritized. Equally, those beneficiaries classified as "medium owners" will be considered. The prioritization considers the size of the forest area and the number of people living in the community. In addition, it takes into account aspects such as gender, presence of indigenous peoples and vulnerable population.

Small forest owners include “agricultural communities” according to the law 514/68, the indigenous communities recognized by the law 203/79, the communities formed under the Forest Reform process”, “rain fed companies” formed according to article 1º of the decree 2.217/1978 and the companies formed in accordance with the law 546/79. In addition, it includes the companies held by the original partners or people that have the quality of small forest owners as certified by the competent authority.

It is not expected or considered that the budget aimed at forest landowners would be an issue with recognition of EBI if the activities carried out using the RBP are different from those specified in the Funding Proposal (i.e. if, for instance, the project management expenses are higher than foreseen). In this case, part of the funds will be transferred to land holders according with the benefit-sharing distribution plan (RSDP).

Dear Mr. Glemarec,

We refer to the Project "REDD-plus RBP for results period 2014-2016" in Chile as included in the funding proposal submitted by FAO to us on June 2019. The objective of the Project is to reduce emissions through result-based payments, implement projects that are part of the National Strategy for Climate Change and Forests, and promote the restoration and sustainable management of vegetational resources.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of Ministry of Finance the National Designated Authority of Chile.

Pursuant to GCF decisions B.8/10 and B.13/21, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objection to the Project REDD+ as included in the Proposal.

By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:

(a) The government of Chile has no objection to the Project as included in the funding proposal;

(b) The Project activities are consistent with national priorities, strategies and plans;  

(c) In accordance with the GCF's environmental and social safeguards, the Project activities as included in the Funding Proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objection to the Project has been duly followed. 

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Kind regards,

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### 1.1 Project background

#### 1.1.1 Project description
- The project presents Chile's REED plus results for the period 2014–2016, calculating a volume of around 14.5 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) in emission reductions (ER) derived from reducing deforestation, forest degradation, enhancement of forest stocks and afforestation.
- These results are presented to GCF for results-based payments (RBP) as part of the REED plus pilot programme and are fully compliant with the eligibility criteria set out on terms of reference (TOR) for Chile's National Designated Authority (NDA), which comply with UNFCCC standards and the GCF TOR, are undergoing analysis based on the following outputs:
  - Output 1: implementation and investment;
  - Output 2: enabling conditions for ENCVR implementation.

- As per decision no. 18/07, the proposal is currently being assessed using the scorecard for REED plus RBPs.
- This assessment is being conducted by the Secretariat with support of Land Use, Land Use Change and Forestry (LULUCF) experts and the preliminary results will be discussed with the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP).
- The final score still needs to be discussed and agreed with the ITAP.

The project provides a good case for complementarity and coherence by blending GCF investments with other ongoing initiatives to support Chile implementing its REDD+ strategy through the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility's Carbon Fund.

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding plan with the terms and conditions set out in the term sheet and addendum XIII, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations."

## Assessment of performance against the investment criteria

### 2.1 Impact potential Scale: High

- Chile implemented a number of policies and legal reforms between 2009 and 2013 that resulted in the REDD+ Focal Point (FP) between 2014 and 2016. Of these emissions, 789 TgCO₂e (14,530,220 tCO₂eq) are being offered to the GCF in the revised proposal. Chile has not received other REDD+ payments for the results of 2014–2016. These results are in part the outcome of a new law that came into effect on January 3rd, 2017, which includes provisions such as the Law 20283 on the Recovery of Native Forests and the Promotion of Forestry issued in 2015. The results achieved by Chile through its implementation of REDD+ and the inclusion in the REDD plus RFP pilot programme, are included in the technical annex to the biennial update report submitted by Chile to the UNFCCC on June 3rd, 2016. These results were presented in accordance with the Conference of the Parties decisions on REDD-plus. The achievement of these part-year results is due to the implementation of the national strategy for forest and result area and furthermore, through the use of proceeds, will lead to a continued reduction of emissions from deforestation, forest degradation, enhancement of forest stocks and conservation.

- Although the forest reference emission level (FREL) is subnational, Chile demonstrates ambition to scale up to national level, mainly through the implementation of ENCERD and the link to the National Forest Inventory. The total area covered by forests in Chile represented for the subnational level are: Maule, Biobío, La Araucanía, Los Ríos and Los Lagos, covering the central and southern part of the country. These areas represent 41 per cent of Chile's national territory (5,853,287 ha) and represent 11 of the 12 tree types present in the country.

- The project contributes to an ambitious and powerful approach to REDD-plus based on the activities contained in ENCERD and NDCs. In the context of its NDC, part of Chile's commitment is to focus on restoration of forests and reduce emissions from forest degradation. This approach offers a unique opportunity for Chile to continue implementing its REDD+ strategy through the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility's (FCPF) Carbon Fund.

### 2.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

- The initiation of ENCERD helped Chile streamline and coordinate REDD-plus efforts through an ambitious and strategic approach. This helped the country reduce emissions from


---
**Page 77**
---

degradation, forest degradation, enhancement of forest stocks and forest conservation and later report and record the results in full compliance with the UNFCCC and now GCF. The project will help to continue the transformation of the forest sector that Chile has undertaken for several years, through synergies and complementarity and coherence with other initiatives, such as the PCPF Carbon Fund.

12. The reinvestment of the proceeds into ENCVCY will help Chile to further reduce emissions from deforestation in the coming years and involve the PCPF Carbon Fund and the private sector in the implementation of the project. This is consistent with Chile’s goals and commitments under its NDC. The strategic investments from GCF for implementation of ENCVCY are expected to be a catalyst for additional funding, which will be essential to attract other donors and financial mechanisms to blend in for implementation of the very ambitious project.

13. There is furthermore a strong potential for building a platform to engage the private sector, which is expected to trigger a paradigm shift that will catalyze the long-term impacts of the project. Specifically, market development and transformation, innovation of practices, and the policy environment are all areas where Chile has already made significant progress and can provide additional experience and valuable lessons learned to other donors and national stakeholders.

14. Chile is one of the pioneering countries in the world including four REDD+ plus activities in their results submitted for consideration to the UNFCCC, including not only deforestation reduction but also forest degradation reduction, enhancement of forest stocks and forest conservation. This supports Chile’s transition towards a carbon neutral country in terms of these activities, into a significant net sink for carbon.

15. Finally, the benefit-sharing system that is expected to be put in place for reinvestment of the proceeds is built in accordance with ENCVCY and will allow further implementation of the strategy of GCF, which includes mechanisms aligned to performance-based incentives schemes at subnational and local scales.


### 2.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

16. The GCF project will create an enabling environment for sustainable development by achieving systemic change at the local, subnational and national levels. ENCVCY focuses on sustainable land management and forest conservation in indigenous territories. As proposed through this proposal explicitly concentrate on economic, environmental and social aspects and seek to improve the livelihoods of rural poor in Chile.

17. In addition, the proposed project is expected to engage and improve the livelihoods of women and youth in the target areas. The project will strengthen governance in indigenous territories and provides support for improving their livelihoods.


### Chile is a signatory of the International Labour Organization (ILO) Convention No. 169 on Indigenous and Tribal Peoples, which guarantees rights of these peoples. The project is in compliance with provisions regarding environmental and social safeguards (ESS), emphasizing indigenous and gender dimensions.

18. Gender assessment and a gender action plan are key elements of the project. The project will build on this gender action plan aligned to ENCVCY. The project will use the various


---
**Page 78**
---

regulations of the policy instruments in the activities in full compliance with national policies,
international treaties and the gender policies and guidelines of the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO).

28 The project addresses the incorporation of women in actions through specific indicators that will be monitored by the project team. It also aims to monitor gender aspects and generation of jobs from the activities that will be implemented.

21 The project targets poverty reduction and, through the focus on enhancing the provision of ecosystem services (e.g., soils, water, biodiversity) will lead to increased resilience of the ecosystems and their services, which in turn will contribute to improved livelihood conditions for rural poor. Also, by fostering ecosystems' restoration and the integral value of the forests, it contributes to climate change mitigation.

23 The project activities have a positive impact on the environment. Special consideration is given to the occurrence of forest fires in the subtropical forests of Chile, which constitute one of the main threats to the conservation of these ecosystems and their services for local populations and their livelihoods. Improvement in the conditions of the forest territories through the increase of the forest mass and the reduction of fire risks is an activity prioritized in ENCry. The project will contribute to this objective by promoting the implementation of the reinvestment of the proceeds, as a buffer assignment is considered in order to trigger and increase the reforestation activities in these areas.

24 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

23 The focus of the use of proceeds will target strategic sections of ENCry complementing other public and international financing sources and thus partially covering the financial needs for implementation of the strategy.

The project's UNCCD counterpart has a strong focus on capacity-building of the various stakeholders at national and subnational levels involved (indigenous peoples, local communities and government at subnational and national levels). There is a strong focus on indigenous peoples in particular. The project will not only support the implementation of this project but also provide a strong platform for the entire strategy that aims to support the development of indigenous peoples who are part of such a strategy.

23 Chile is particularly vulnerable to the occurrence and exacerbation of forest fires, in part due to the presence of some forest areas. The specific vulnerabilities and impacts have also been documented in national communications to the UNCCD secretariat and are being duly internalized in ENCry.

26 The main beneficiaries of these payments include the rural population and indigenous peoples from the regions of Chile. Indeed, among the most vulnerable groups in the country are indigenous and non-indigenous rural communities concentrated mainly between the Maule and Aysén regions. This has been duly internalized in ENCry.

25 Country ownership Scale: High


---
**Page 79**
---

27 The project is fully aligned with national policies and NDC priorities, and the proceeds will be used in concert with the full implementation of Chile’s very ambitious ENCREV.

28 During project preparation, the national designated authority (NDA) has experienced a high level of engagement and country appropriation. The NDA has had the definition and development of the project proposal to proceed through several stages of review. In particular, the NDA has been involved in the definition of the institutional arrangements for the programme execution and has provided technical recommendations during the convening of the independent expert panel (IEP) and the TAP. The IEP reviewed the funding proposal. Only after reviewing the proposal, the TAP recommended that the NDA issue the non-objection letter.

29 In the context of the preparation of this proposal and the ENCREV, the Government undertook a highly consultative process with relevant stakeholders, including indigenous peoples and local communities. Evidence on this process was provided in an annex as part of the funding proposal.

30 Moreover, the National Forestry Corporation (CONAF), a governmental body with more than 50 years of experience, plays a key role for implementation of the project. For the purposes of project implementation, the success of the project relies on the presence of CONAF in the territory at the regional and provincial levels.

### 2.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: High

31 The REDD-plus results for 2014–2016 presented to GCF and the suggested budget both clearly highlight that a fixed price of USD 5 per ton CO2 will be applied to the project, which is consistent with the current market price. In addition, the payment for these past results is reinvested into a transformation of the forest sector, which will allow Chile to achieve its goals in reforestation, deforestation degradation, enhancement of forest carbon stocks and conservation.

32 The reinvestment into ENCREV further helps Chile reach economies of scale together with other donors currently supporting ENCREV and brings different donors and partners together. This is a key element for the project as it will allow for the continuation of ongoing support from FCPC. The project is a model by showcasing how GCF funds can help complement existing funding mechanisms, such as those provided by the World Bank and FCPC Carbon Fund’s payments have a clearly defined role and timing, which is described in the project with specific details on the amount to be paid for each year and for which period.

33 Furthermore, there will be numerous lessons learned and best practices from the implementation of activities and coordination with other sources that will be crucial not only for Chile but for other REDD-plus countries engaging in GEF. This will be important with regard to the development of a registry system as described below.

34 Finally, Chile is developing two systems to have an accounting of emissions and a distribution of benefits. The registry system allows the accounting of emissions traded and will avoid double counting. The second system is a registry for carbon credits. Both documents and guidelines obtained in the phases of development and implementation of ENCREV and ensure the management and distribution in the regions and locations in a transparent and equitable manner.
