### III. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 3.1 Environmental and social safeguards

**30.** A proposal is submitted by the government of Chile to GCF under its REDD-plus RFP for the payment of the greenhouse gas (GHG) emission reductions generated by its REDD-plus activities for the period of 2014 to 2018. The REDD-plus RFP will be used to support the implementation of the National Forest and Land Use Change Strategy in five priority regions of Maule, Biobío, La Araucanía, Los Ríos and Los Lagos. The prioritization strategy is based on the results of the ENSCRV (Estrategia Nacional de Sostenibilidad del Cambio Climático). This ENSCRV aims to contribute to the achievement of the country's nationally determined contribution (NDC) under the Paris Agreement, as well as to meet its international obligations. The goal of ENSCRV is to reduce vulnerability associated with land degradation in at least 24 million hectares of forest and non-forest lands by 2030.

**31.** In order to achieve this objective, Chile has developed a National Forest and Land Use Change Strategy (ENSCRV) under the sector by 2015 compared to the reference level during the period 2001 to 2013. To achieve its goals, ENSCRV will implement 26 action measures consisting of seven direct actions and 19 enabling action measures. The REDD-plus RFP proposal includes a description of the implementation of five direct action and five enabling action measures that address the main drivers of deforestation and forest degradation as well as mobilize additional resources to implement the other ENSCRV action measures.

**32.** An ex post review of the extent to which implementation of the activities that generate emission reductions in Chile has been consistent with the safeguards agreed under the UNFCCC (also referred to as Cancun safeguards) was included in the funding proposal submitted by the government of Chile to GCF. The review includes an assessment of the entity's (AE) environmental and social safeguards and relevant policies. Also included in the proposal are the processes for site identification, assessment and management of risks and impacts associated with activities supported by the proceeds from the RFP that are compliant with the requirements of the ES and related policies of the AE.

**33.** In terms of the ex post review, the AE prepared an environmental and social assessment (ESA) that describes the extent to which the past activities that generated emission reductions were consistent with the Cancun safeguards as interpreted by the country's interpretation of the AE. The development and implementation of REDD-plus activities require that such activities address the main drivers of deforestation and forest degradation in Chile, while interpreting the Cancun safeguards according to the country's context.

**34.** The review of how each of the Cancun safeguards was addressed and respected throughout implementation of the activities supported by the proceeds from RFP is provided below. The review also offers a comparison of the REDD-plus safeguards with the country's interpretation, including consistency between the AE's safeguard policies and regulations with the environmental, social and economic sustainability framework of the AE.

**35.** In terms of ex ante review, the AE developed an assessment of risks and management framework (RMF) to identify, assess, manage, monitor and report on risks and impacts associated with the activities supported by the proceeds from RFP will be identified and managed. The risks and impacts are initially assessed using the screening process of the AE.

**36.** These risks and impacts are then evaluated against the AE's GCF, UNFCCC and indigenous peoples planning framework (IPPF). As details of the specific activities, including locations, have yet to be determined, the AE has not yet developed a detailed risk assessment for management and control of risks and impacts associated with the activities.

# Consistency of the design and implementation of REDD-plus activities with the Cancun safeguards and GCF IESS

The EIA prepared by the AE, in coordination with the Government of Chile, is a well-organized retrospective review of the extent to which, in the context of the REDD-plus RFP, the AE has taken into account the Cancun safeguards, and manage environmental and social risks and impacts are consistent with GCF IESS.

The EIA relied on numerous sources of information for the period covering 2014 to 2016. The review was conducted in a retrospective manner, as part of the process of its assessment. The EIA acknowledges challenges associated with an ex post review of the implementation set of actions and processes undertaken by the AE in relation to the GCF IESS, and the environmental and social standards of the AE. To address this complexity, the assessment focused primarily on the Cancun safeguards and the environmental and social standards of the AE.

The compatibility of the AE with GCF IESS was determined during the GCF accreditation process. The EIA also considered the extent to which the environmental and social standards of the AE and GCF IESS are consistent. The assessment identified similar or complementary ways in which both sets of standards were addressed, and undertook to substantiate the consistency of those actions with GCF requirements.

The EIA provides a detailed description that underscores general consistency only applicable at the project level. The EIA also describes the process used by the AE. It elaborated on the range of stakeholders consulted or engaged during the review period; the various ways in which they were involved, and the types of information collected and provided to them about the project. This includes a detailed description of the information on how the stakeholders participated in the activities. Much of this more detailed information was obtained from the AE, but some additional information was also included in the EIA, and provided additional information about participating processes undertaken in conjunction with the ENCVR. Such processes included regional participatory workshops, meetings with indigenous communities, and interviews with project staff and specialists who were exposed to the impacts.

The EIA also describes the needs of stakeholders and terminal work with impacted stakeholders, who "must understand the project to be interested in participating," and better understand evolving management practices associated with Chile's overall shift in institutional arrangements for REDD-plus implementation. The AE provided information on training and technical assistance. However, in the context of stakeholder engagement in relation to the ENCVR, the EIA notes that the AE has not yet developed a strategy to strengthen ways to incorporate a training initiative into both the ENCVR and the specific environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) process. The EIA also describes the level of participation in technical and managerial aspects of ENCVR implementation.

Regarding information disclosure, the EIA includes a detailed description of the Chilean Government's transparency framework for REDD-plus projects, including the most pertinent information Office, Claims and Suggestions (OBS). The EIA also described the AE's approach to information disclosure. This approach is disseminated, consistent with Cancun and GCF information disclosure and transparency policies. The EIA "views" and suggests that the process as the project's grievance redress mechanism is described in the AE's analogous framework. The system also satisfies the GCF requirement for an institutional grievance redress mechanism, as well as processes required by the establishment of REDD-plus activities; and

# Vulnerable Groups, Including Indigenous Peoples

Uncertain land tenure and ownership of lands considered as indigenous territories, under traditional ownership or customary use may lead to the exclusion of indigenous peoples from their lands and resources. This can result in further loss of livelihoods and further impoverishment which may result in exclusion and failure of indigenous peoples to partake in the project benefits. In addition, there may be risks associated with the introduction of new technologies and innovative forest management practices with the introduction of new monitoring techniques, including remote sensing and GIS. These require thorough assessments, development measures to mitigate the potential risks and adverse impacts and conduct free, prior and informed consent of indigenous peoples consistent with the FPIC and ENCCBCV.

Among other things, the project will ensure that all land tenure issues are established a process to guarantee the protection of rights to land tenure and possession of lands and resources. The project will also incorporate a free, prior and informed consent process in line with the country's policy requirements and the policies and standards of the AE. Building the capacity of indigenous peoples to participate effectively in the planning and incorporation of local knowledge into the RES+plus strategy action measures.

The community is considered as an Indigenous People because they are legally protected under Art 15:23 and have obligations under the ILO Convention 169, which the country ratified and which entered into force in September 2009. The All will be implemented through the National Land Commission (NLC) and the National Indigenous Development Corporation (CNID) which reports to the Ministry of Social Development and Environment. The CNID is responsible for conflict resolution, safeguarding legal and customary rights, advising and assisting project proponents in the preparation of land tenure and ownership assessments as part of the consultation process (mandated by Supreme Decree No 66 of 2013 of the Ministry of Social Development and Environment and the Ministry of Environment). Pursuant to the ILO Convention 169 and the existing laws of the country, a process for obtaining free, prior and informed consent through dialogue and participation is required as an essential component of any measure that will be implemented. The methodology for dialogue and participation for the entire project will be developed in consultation with the CNID.

## Land Tenure and Involuntary Resettlement

The project does not foresee adverse impacts related to involuntary resettlement and any form of physical displacement of indigenous peoples, including those who are excluded from remote reforestation, land restoration, and sustainable management of forest resources. The exclusion of these groups is due to the fact that they do not have access to small landowners and indigenous peoples to project benefits. Further due diligence will be conducted in consultation with the relevant agencies for a detailed assessment of the project to ascertain land tenure and ownership conditions in areas where the project activities are located, including those related to involuntary resettlement, dispossession of assets and physical displacement will form part of the projects list of excluded activities. To avoid and/or minimize conflicts between indigenous peoples and project proponents, the project will ensure that the relevant agencies to address legal and policy gaps relative to the activities that have been identified as being potentially adverse to indigenous peoples' free, prior and informed consent process; implement a fair and equitable benefit-sharing scheme; incorporate local norms and practices in the planning and

### Implementation of Activities and Enhance Inter-Institutional Coordination on Issues Pertaining to Indigenous Peoples

#### (iv) Loss of livelihoods: changes in territorial planning to enhance landscape restoration, reforestation, and enforcement of protected areas may lead to loss of economic opportunities for indigenous peoples. In close collaboration with the government, the project will support the livelihoods and income-generating activities of indigenous peoples by promoting a balance between productive practices and conservation measures. In addition, economic incentives will be provided through the development of tools that facilitate access to the project benefits for people working in the informal sector. Supporting these initiatives, the project will integrate educational programs on sustainable forest management and sustainable forest management among affected local populations.

#### (v) Biological invasions: screening identified potential risks of introducing alien invasive species and the conversion of natural forest lands into monoculture forest plantations. The promotion of plantations and the introduction of exotic species in areas where they are not native can adversely affect ecosystem services and reduce biodiversity. As part of the mitigation measures, the project will work with indigenous peoples and those that pass through the vetting and assessment processes of COPAF in the reference area to identify risks associated with biological invasions and to strengthen policies that prioritize biodiversity and forest conservation as well as enforce compliance with each of such policies to prevent land-use changes and conversion of natural forests.

#### (vi) Exacerbation of conflicts: the screening identified potential exacerbation of local conflicts due to competition for resources, lack of access to information sharing, and poor governance. The project will ensure that benefit-sharing mechanisms are designed in a participatory manner with all stakeholders available, and inclusive processes. The design and implementation of activities will incorporate local and customary practices to avoid internal conflicts. The benefits from the project will be shared among vulnerable groups such as indigenous peoples, women, youth and elderly to avoid further exclusion and marginalization. Efforts will also be made to ensure that those most affected by the process of free, prior and informed consent will be undertaken.

#### (vii) Displacement or leakage of emissions: activities designed to reduce deforestation and forest degradation in the reference area may lead to displacement or leakage of emissions in other areas not addressed by the project. To complement the country's FREEL and non-market-based carbon credits, the project will work with the government on emission displacement and leakage. The project will work with the government to strengthen policies that prioritize sustainable land management and governance to avoid illegal and unsustainable forest management practices; and

#### (viii) Excluded activities: the ESME provided a list of excluded activities that may generate significant impacts on the overall risk category of the project. Activities with non-eligible environmental aspects include those related to fire, growing, colonizing species, close-based forest plantations use of agricultural land for commercial purposes, and production of products list of the government; conversion of natural forest cover; and establishment of grazing areas in headwaters of micro-basins and natural slopes. Activities with non-eligible social aspects include those that generate significant impacts on

3.2 Gender policy

The AIF has submitted a gender assessment, therefore it complies with the operational guidelines of the Climate Policy and Action Plan. The gender assessment discusses gender issues at the country level and refers to the policy, legal and institutional framework for promoting gender equality in Chile. At the national level the government has established a Ministry of Women’s Affairs (MAG) which is responsible for developing and implementing guidelines for gender units of state agencies. The assessment also provides information on general trends related to women’s participation in decision-making processes, their role as a labor force and unpaid domestic and care work at the national level and in the zones targeted for the use of project resources.

Most of the communities in the prioritized areas of action of ENCIVRI live in rural zones and depend on forest resources with men and women having different dependencies on natural resources. Men are involved in harvesting timber, including its large-scale trading while women are mainly responsible for managing small plots of land and maintaining non-timber forest resources. In addition, most land is owned by men in rural areas for which legal regulations do not allow women to own or inherit land. Women also have limited access to finance from financial institutions. The gender action plan addresses access to project benefits by ensuring that project activities are designed to allow women to derive value from forest resources.

For the period of achieved results, information on gender mainstreaming is mainly related to the active engagement during the preparation phase of the project. Men and women leaders from communities were involved in identifying entrepreneurs involved in forestry, participated in workshops to discuss the project. Focus group discussion on the formulation and validation of the action plan was also held with men and women representatives. Furthermore, proposed actions gathered during stakeholder engagement exercises include having environmental education programs for children and youth that will help them understand their maintenance and care, and greater institutional presence in the territories; actions requested from non-governmental organizations (NGOs) to support the development of sustainable development of forests to ensure productivity. The use of proceeds responds to requests related to activities that will be implemented through activities that will be implemented through local investments such as training on conservation matters which will have a target of 60 per cent women beneficiaries.

The AIF has included a programme-level gender action plan. The AIF is recommended to review this document and ensure that it is aligned with the gender assessment. This contains indicators with some sex-disaggregated targets and responsibilities; however, these shall be reviewed in order to ensure alignment of the gender action plan with the proposed actions listed in the action plan as activities under the two outputs of the programme for the use of project resources. If the gender action plan is aligned with the gender assessment that have been listed, they shall be aligned to indicators, clarifying which indicators and targets are related to. For this purpose, a gender analysis at the project level rather than an overall methodology for developing projects with gender analysis due to the fact that the indicator and target are not sex-disaggregated. The AIF is recommended to review the proposed gender expertise and the AIF will maintain oversight in ensuring that the action plan is implemented as per the gender assessment. The AIF has also included a gender action project management unit of the project. There is an indicator in the action plan to train CONAF staff on

3.4 Results monitoring and reporting
55. Following the requirements of the TOSR, the proposal will follow a simplified reporting procedure as outlined in the specific template which was developed by the secretariat. The frequency of reporting will be annual following the accreditation master agreement (AMA).
56. Monitoring of the use of proceeds will be conducted at the main activity level according to the description provided in the funding proposal.
3.5 Legal assessment
57. The AMA was signed with the AE on 8 June 2018, and it became effective on 4 October 2018.
58. The proposed project will be implemented in Chile, a country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks to be further assessed by the secretariat. On 12 December 2018, the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) of the GCF held a meeting with a government official from Chile and provided additional information on the rationale for the draft project document and the proposed mitigation measures.
59. The heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and Independent Integrity Unit have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. As a result, the Secretariat has determined that the proposed project GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country or has been provided with other suitable mitigation measures.
IV. List of proposed conditions
60. In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board be made subject to the following conditions:
(a) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and
(b) Completion of legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP120

## Proposal name: Chile REDD-plus results-based payments for results period 2014-2016

### Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

#### Project/programme size: Medium

## II. Main findings

### B.1.0 Consistency between the forest reference emissions level (forest reference level (FRL/FRL) and the greenhouse gas (GHG) inventory:

The Assessment Team (AT) found that the values for the FRL in the modified submission are significantly different to those included in the national GHG inventory for the same period (2001-2010). In the case of deforestation, Chile explained


---
**Page 111**
---

that differences are due to better activity data (AD) and more intensive use of country-specific emission factors (CEF). In the case of forest degradation, Chile explained that the main reasons for the inconsistencies are related to the AD, the calculation methods used and the CEFs. The AT noted that while there was a general agreement on the importance of the increase in forest carbon stocks, there were also significant differences between the FRLs/FRIs and the GHLG. Chile explained that this is due to the inclusion of tree plantations in the FRLs. The AT considered that the FRLs/FRIs represent an improvement over the previous GHLG because they are based on more recent activity data, emission and removal estimates (see para. 40 of the third assessment report (TAR)). In this regard, the AT commended Chile for the improvements and noted that Chile could consider including tree plantations in future FRLs/FRIs. The AT also considered that those that use the more accurate methodologies in updating their national GHG inventory as part of its third assessment report (TAR) should be encouraged to do so. In the previous definition, the FRL excluded planted forests and thus considered only native forests. However, Chile explained that it is now considering including all types of forests. This exclusion was explained with the fact that more than 97 per cent of forest areas in Chile are natural forests and not plantations. The AT commended this progress. During the FREL/FR, the technical assessment of Chile stated that plantations will be included in the FRL/FRI once these plantations are used as permanent cover and are consistent with the definition of native forests (NF) under the National Inventory Guidelines (NIGs) under the Paris Agreement (see para. 35 of the TAR). The ITAP fully recognizes that this is a positive step forward, which will improve the consistency between what takes into consideration that the FRL and GHLG apply two different forest definitions. Though the AT commended Chile for its progress in improving the FRL/FRI, it lays the ITAP estimates a score of 1 because there is no consistency in the forest definition requested by the AT.

### B.I.(iv) Consistency with previous FREL/FRIs

Chile's FREL/FRI is the first revision of the FRL/FRI submitted to the AT. The corresponding score is 1, "no adjustment made or no previous FREL/FRI submission."

### B.I.(iv) Aggregated uncertainties of the FREL/FRI (table 6-3 of the ITAP submission)

The AT commended Chile for its progress in improving the FRL/FRI and noted that this test of the submission, as well as the funding proposal, mentions an uncertainty of less than 4 per cent. Consequently, the score is 1, an aggregate uncertainty below 50 per cent and assumptions and sources of uncertainties are identified and assessed for their relative contribution;


---
**Page 112**

### (c) Transparency of the data and information provided in the technical annexes

The LULUCF experts noted that Chile’s technical annex does not include sufficient information to allow an assessment of consistency with the data used in the latest GEGI (2018) report. The Ministry of Agriculture has been working on updating its methodologies for emissions and removals, as well as improving the quality of its data. It is expected that these efforts will ensure consistency among the emissions and removals that will be reported to the UNFCCC in 2023. Chile has also updated its methodologies and data for all three processes as part of the stepwise approach and presented them in a technical annex, which was submitted with the national inventory report submitted in 2016 (see para. 22 of the technical report of the technical annex (TATR)). The score is 1; significant issues were raised and were resolved, or issues that were not resolved due to the limitation of time and data availability have been identified for their respective consistency with the transparency of the results, and the country has provided a plan on how it will seek to overcome them.

### B.2 (c) Accuracy of data and information provided in the technical annexes: Some issues pertaining the accuracy of the data and information in the technical annex were raised by the LULUCF experts. The Ministry of Agriculture has been working on updating using cadastre maps with different initial years for different regions of the country, whereupon it will be possible to identify changes in land use over time from start and end dates for different regions. Both sets of ADs (the same land use and land-use change) were used as a basis for the calculation of emissions and removals definitions pertaining to arable material. The LULUCF experts were of the view that Chile may be underestimating its forest area, given the use of satellite imagery (e.g., Landsat images) and pixel-based land-use change classifications for the results period, while using higher resolution data (i.e. Mete Rapideur and SPOT images) with visual interpretation for the baseline period. The Ministry of Agriculture has been working on updating its methodologies to ensure consistency between the two sets of ADs. The LULUCF experts noted that Chile may wish to consider recalculating the entire set of AD using satellite imagery (e.g., Landsat images) and pixel-based land-use change classifications pertaining to arable material. The LULUCF experts were of the view that Chile may be underestimating its forest area, given the use of satellite imagery (e.g., Landsat images) and pixel-based land-use change classifications for the results period, while using higher resolution data (i.e. Mete Rapideur and SPOT images) with visual interpretation for the baseline period.

### B.2 (c)(ii) Aggregate uncertainties for results: 70% of the BEED’s technical annexes were reviewed by the LULUCF experts in 2018, which showed that Chile is at a per cent. The score is <50 per cent, and assumptions and sources of uncertainties are identified and assessed for their relative contribution.

### 2.1 Impact potential

Scale: High

In 2019, under the leadership of the Ministry of Agriculture and DINAFC, an broad consensus was reached at the national and international levels for the development of the National Strategy on Climate Change and Vegetation Resources (ENCIVR), which was approved by the country in November 2016. ENCIVR is considered by the Government as an instrument to implement the commitments made under the Paris Agreement, in line with Chile’s international and national commitments. The overall goal of ENCIVR is to increase the


---
**Page 113**
---

resilience of ecosystems and mitigate climate change through the enhancement of forest carbon stocks.

5. Chile has 14.3 million hectares (ha) of unique forests with a high level (50.3 per cent) of endemism. Historically, approximately 5 million ha of these forests were converted for agriculture and livestock production. The remaining 9.3 million ha are now designated as Chilean forests. Key drivers of degradation are firewood, livestock and forest fires.

6. ENCRE (Estrategia Nacional de Restauración y Conservación de Ecosistemas) is a plan actions included in ENCRE will focus on a significant part of the national territory where there is a high concentration and diversity of forests, including temperate rain forests, in the 34 departments that make up the Chilean Forest Zone (CFZ), which covers 10.5 million ha. These regions represent 22 per cent of the total area of the country, host 41 per cent of the native forest, and are home to more than half of the country’s population.

7. ENCRE considers 26 active measures for addressing the drivers of deforestation and forest degradation and the barriers to the restoration, conservation and sustainable management of forests in Chile. The implementation of these actions is a key component of ENCRE. CONAF is the national focal point for coordinating the REDD+plus process and establishing the national REDD+ registry, including the development of a national registry, assessment and reporting of FRLs/REDD+, and the preparation of the technical annexes on REDD- plus results.

8. In total, 26,000 ha of territory is proposed in ENCRE for the implementation of measures proposed in the strategy, which is aligned with the goals established in Chile’s NDC for the LULUCF sector. The main target is to restore and sustainably manage another 100,000 ha.

9. CONAF plays a key role as the national focal point having basic information for the NFMS (Article 4 of the UNFCCC [2006]) on recovering native forests and forest degradation in Chile, but CONAF is responsible for maintaining a permanent forest cadaster.

10. The NFMS was established in 2018 to designate and report every two years at the national level, and will be expected to provide support at the national level from 2020 on reducing the risk of displacement. The NFMS is integrated into the ENCRE measuring and monitoring framework.

11. The first FRL submitted by the country to the UNFCCC secretariat for conducting technical assessments is subnational, but Chile has the ambition to scale up REDD-plus activities to the national level in 2023. The national REDD+ registry will be established at the national level and the FRL at the subnational level are Biobío, La Araucanía, Los Ríos y Los Lagos, covering the northwestern part of Chile.

12. Chile is the first country applying for REPP from GCF, which has included in REDD-plus four activities (reducing emissions from deforestation, reducing emissions from forest degradation and enhancing carbon stocks) that are eligible under the 2013 decision on the five considered eligible for REDD-plus in paragraph 79 of the Cancun Decision [Decision LC/COP/DEC.7/INF.4]. The application was submitted to GCF in June 2018 due to the lack of spatially explicit information for appropriately attributing emission reductions and/or removals resulting from sustainable forest management activities.


Available at <https://www.envigo-chile.cl/sites/documentos/nfms-5-sistema-de-medicin-y-monitoreo-de-la-cambio-climatico-y-recursos-vegetales-en-Chile>.

The potential mitigation benefits associated with these measures are 500,000 tCO2 equivalent annually.

## Paradigm shift potential

The REDD-plus mechanism, negotiated and established within the UNFCCC process and based on the RFP for increasing the resilience of ecosystems and strengthening the role of this system in climate change adaptation and mitigation, has been designed as a tool to promote transformational changes in the sustainable management of the forest and land-use sector at the global level.

Though the country has not yet included all REDD-plus activities (only four out of five have been implemented) in its REDD-plus programme, which is allowed in this mechanism, the national strategy for climate change adaptation and mitigation is an integral part of the broader ENCVR. Such an approach is also transformational and should be exemplary for other countries implementing the REDD-plus programme.

* *REDD stands for sustainable management model of vegetation resources, according to the ENCVR.
* *USE stands for adaptive management of the vegetation resources to climate change, desertification, land degradation and drought.


---
**Page 116**
---

26. Based on Chile’s approach for the use of proceeds, payments will be reinvested into the implementation of activities planned from the ENCVRG overall plan and the generation of new emission reduction units contributing to the fulfillment of Chile's NIC commitments.

27. The ITAP would like to highlight the innovative approach of the benefit-sharing scheme which is designed to ensure a fair distribution of the proceeds among the five regions. This benefit-sharing approach is based on three key principles: equity, efficiency and solidarity. Equity refers to the principle that payments should be proportional between the amount of tonnes of emissions reduced and/or increased in forest carbon absorption generation. Efficiency refers to the principle that payments will be proportional according to the corresponding RBP, and Solidarity refers to the principle that payments will be proportional based on the region’s share of forest area (forests, plantations or other) which require more intense interventions and therefore, interventions of greater duration and cost.

29. The ITAP is of the opinion that such a conceptual approach to the benefit-sharing process should be replicated by other countries as well, ensuring fairness and transparency in the sharing of benefits among communities involved.

30. As indicated in paragraph 26 above, ENCVRG and the REID+ plus process, its integral component, significantly contribute to NIC implementation through the management of the LEEDP funds. The REID+ plus programme has been a key instrument for the Chilean forestry sector and has been boosted due to the positioning of the wood and cellulose industry.

2.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

31. The REID+plus programme of Chile is integral part of country's ENCVRG, which was constructed based on more than three years of broad consultations with all potential


---
**Page 117**
---

stakeholders. The objective of the strategy is to reduce the country’s social, environmental and economic vulnerability caused by climate change, desertification, land degradation and drought, and with this contribute to all three pillars of sustainable development.

## As a result of long-term consultations, ENCCRF has the support and commitment of civil society. The project implementation phase for ENCCRF (the inclusion of social and environmental safeguards), which follow national and international guidelines, was carefully established in close collaboration with stakeholders.

### According to the activities planned in the strategy, it is expected that the successful implementation of measures could generate a significant amount of environmental, social and economic benefits. These include: improved access to water resources for agriculture; the improved quality of life of local actors; improved management practices of native vegetation resources; and increased income generation opportunities for the most vulnerable and/or most vulnerable local communities.

#### The funding proposal is in compliance with all environmental and social safeguard standards established by international organizations, GCF and the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO). The Canarias safeguards are confirmed in the relevant section of the scoping document. The project implementation plan has been reviewed by an accredited entity (AE), and as such, its safeguards are analysed in the funding proposal.

##### The benefit-sharing system (see para 22 above) expected to be put in place for the remuneration of carbon capture projects is designed so that the benefits generated will significantly contribute to the sustainable development process being constructed based on the three main principles of equity, efficiency and environmental sustainability. It is estimated that 80% of these payments include the most vulnerable populations, such as the rural population and indigenous communities, who are also the ones most affected by climate change in the target regions. Chile plans to bring this benefit-sharing approach from the regional level to the national level in the coming years.

###### At the start of the design stage of the demonstration projects, Chile considered developing these projects in areas where not only carbon capture is enhanced, but also where the multiple benefits of ENCCRF are maximized. The following criteria were established to be achieved according to the REDDS-plus pilot programme: improve the regulation of water resources; reduce soil erosion and desertification; increase forest cover and reduce erosion; and improve the landscape, forest conservation and biodiversity.

####### Scenarios with and without ENCCRF were developed and the economic valuation was determined. The results show that the project has a positive net benefit for Chile’s economy in terms of GDP. However, it also finds that the estimated non-carbon benefits of ENCCRF are 1.2 times larger than the carbon benefits (funding proposal, pp.39–40).

######## A consultancy is currently being initiated to expand this methodology at the national level. The project will also develop a database with baseline data for Chile at the national and sub-national levels and systematize official data for the implementation of territorial baselines for at least six co-benefit indicators, covering environmental and/or social indicators. These products will be included in the ENCCRF Platform, making them available to interested persons and institutions in a transparent and expeditious manner.

######## The project concludes that the sustainable development potential of ENCCRF and the funding proposal is high.

### Country ownership

#### Scale: High

* **Country ownership:** ENCCF and its REDD+ programme section can be assessed as very high based on the information provided in the funding proposal and on the ENCCF website.
    * In particular, the strategy was formulated and finally approved in 2016 after more than three years of preparation. The process involved a wide range of stakeholders from all regions of the country, including indigenous peoples; the institutional sector; non-governmental organizations (NGOs); local communities; small-scale landowners; groups of women; consultants; and extension agents.
    * In support of country ownership, the reform established by Law 20.528/2016 should be considered as a milestone in the process to reach a larger number of beneficiaries through its incentive schemes, including payment for ecosystem services schemes, to implement a sustainable forest management model. The law also includes indigenous peoples who do not yet have regular land titles might be included as potential beneficiaries of national programmes.
* **Country ownership:** is fully supported by the implementation of funding proposals through Comités Regionales de Cambio Climático (regional climate change committees), which are decentralized bodies that operate at the subnational level in all regions of the country. These regional climate change committees are chaired by the highest regional authority and include representatives from different sectors, including public administration at the national level. They are constituted by the public sector, academia, non-governmental organizations and the private sector, depending on the region. Figure 6 of the funding proposal shows the relationships between the institutions.

## Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: High

There is evidence below demonstrating the high efficiency and effectiveness of the funding proposal.

- Anticipated results to be achieved from the reinvestment of GCF proceeds into ENCCRV 2017–2030, as projected by the project proponents as 580,000 tCO₂eq accumulated by 2025 and 2,400,000 tCO₂eq accumulated by 2030. This volume represents 3.6 per cent of CO₂ accumulated globally.
- Offered by the country reinvestment scheme increases the efficiency of this funding proposal from the perspective of GCF: Total emission reductions resulting in results-based payments to the project proponents will be 1,854,729 tCO₂eq (generated in 2016–2018) and 3,062,444 tCO₂eq accumulated by 2030. Offered by GCF is USD 3.70.
- From Chile’s perspective, income from emission reductions generated through the project will be USD 59.5 million (generated in 2016–2018) and USD 104.5 million accumulated by 2030.

The effectiveness of the funding proposal should be very high, taking into consideration the benefit-sharing mechanism described under the paradigm shift criteria.
- As a safeguard against potential reversals, Chile has established a reserve fund of 21 per cent of the emission reductions, accounting for a total of 3,642,220 tCO₂eq. This fund will remain in place during the validity of the strategy and will be used to cover any reversals that may occur as a result of increased emissions due to increased removals due to increased stocks or conservation exposed to natural and anthropogenic disturbances.
- ENCCRV facilitates public-private partnerships and works intensively with private sector representatives at the decision-making level as well as at the implementation level, which also increases the effectiveness of the funding proposal.

Monitoring of safeguards, the displacement of activities and emissions, as well as other elements of the REDD+ programme is being carried out through the measurement and monitoring system (MMS) in Chile. The MMS includes a verification process and validation, which should ensure the accuracy and transparency of generated emission reductions.
- CONAF is responsible for tracking the movement of all emission reductions generated in the country. The registry for the accounting and tracking of emission reductions is being finalized by the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MARN).
- The ITAP is of the opinion that the entire REDD+ approach, benefit-sharing concept and use of GCF proceeds, risk management and monitoring are efficient and effective, and could be considered a best practice case.

*16,930,220 tCO₂eq (generated in 2016–2018) + 24,000,000 tCO₂eq (accumulated by 2030)

| (i) Is the FRE/FRL consistent with the greenhouse gas (GHG) inventory, including the definition of forest used? | 1 | According to paragraph 19 of the TAR, the AT noted that the average emissions per hectare for forest degradation in the modified submission are significantly different to those included in the national GHG inventory. The AT also noted that Chile has a 3 times higher deforestation and 20 times higher for forest degradation (compared with the national GHG inventory). Chile provided a detailed explanation for these significant differences, which were due to the inclusion of tree plantations replacing native forests. According to paragraph 19 of the TAR, the AT noted that the average emissions per hectare for deforestation, Chile explained that differences are due to better AD and more accurate methodologies in updating its national GHG inventory for forest degradation. Chile explained that the main reasons for the increase in deforestation and forest degradation were the change from the gain/loss method in the national GHG inventory versus stock change in the proposed FRE/FRL. The AT also noted that there are significant differences between the FRE and the GHG inventory in that the former are approximate estimates, while the latter is based on actual data. In addition, Chile considered that the inclusion of more accurate methodologies in updating its national GHG inventory as part of the FRE/FRL would provide a more accurate estimate (as fact used in their third BIR, according to the funding proposal). The AT considered that this was an improvement and noted that it is in terms of the use of higher-tier methods leading to increased accuracy. Chile also explained that it will continue to use the TAR. **Page 124**
---

Regarding forest definitions, paragraph 34 of the TAR states that China provided its definition of forests used in the construction of the FRELs and FRLs. According to this definition, a forest will be considered as such if it meets one or more of the following criteria according to the current Chinese legislation: it is different from the definition applied for the national GHG inventory. The latest national GHG inventory uses the forest definition established by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change and with plant formation, in which trees predominate and cover over a surface area greater than 0.5 hectares or more than 10 percent of an area with >40 meters, with arborescent canopy cover that surpasses 10 per cent of the total vegetation cover. The FREL uses another definition established by the ATF, which is different from the national definition, namely: "forest composed of indigenous species deriving directly or naturally from the original forest and having a continuous tree canopy, with the same species in the area as its original distribution, which results in the exclusion of carbon from forest plantations of introduced species". The forest definition applied in the FREL is also different from the definition used by China for its national GHG inventory territories, because in the latter, native forests and planted forests are included. The ATF has proposed a new definition of forests at the request of the ATF that this different use of definitions, namely that over 10 per cent of the area is covered with trees of a single species composed of introduced species, resulting in the exclusion of carbon from forest plantations from the construction of the FREL. The ATF has also indicated that new planted forests will be included in the FREL if they are destined for permanent afforestation and are included as part of nationally determined contributions under the Paris Agreement (not permanent afforestation). The ITAB fully recognizes that FREL uses an improved methodology and definition of forest compared to the national GHG inventory. Taking into consideration this, the FREL and the GHG inventory apply two different definitions of forests. In the case of the FREL, the score is 2 for country and approved by the law; the ITAB estimates the score as 1 because there is no monitoring required by the agreement.

### (ii) In the PREL/FRL based on historical data and it is equal to or below the average annual historical emissions during the reference period, unless a country is an HFLD country?

Chile is not a high forest cover, low deforestation country. Chile developed its nationalized PRELs and FRLs for the temperate native forests in five administrative regions (Maule, Biobío, La Araucanía, Los Ríos, Los Lagos). The PREL/FRLs are calculated on an annual basis and FREL/FRL in the future, incorporating all biomass/forests in the country. Chile’s PREL/FRLs for the temperate native forests are based on a proposal by Chile are based on the historical data and equals to total forest area (ha) minus emissions from forest fires (ha), deforestation/removals.

Chile's FREL is calculated for two reference periods, once every five years. The first reference period is 2015-2019 with no change for land use [i.e., forest remaining forest] (2005–2019). This allows Chile to account for the increase in carbon stock (Ct) and biomass (CH4) and emissions from forest fires (non-CO2), as well as deforestation/removals.

### (iii) In the FREL/FRL in accordance with the guidelines in decision 1/CP.17?

The AT noted [see para. 37 of Chile’s TAR] that the data and information used by Chile in constructing its PRELs and FRLs are transparent and complete, and are in overall accordance with the guidance provided in the annexes to the guidelines in the annexes to decision 1/CP.17.

### (iv) Is the data and information provided for the PREL/FRL transparent? (Has this information been provided to allow an understanding of how UNFCCC guidance on the submission of information on reference levels has been addressed?)

The AT noted [see para. 37 of Chile’s TAR] that the data and information used by Chile in constructing its PRELs and FRLs are transparent. The AT also noted that Chile provided transparent PREL/FRL submissions, which were supported with additional data and information during and after the submission process. The AT further noted that Chile has been providing the UNFCCC Secretariat with a detailed description of the data used by Chile for providing each data, which increased the transparency of its submissions and allowed the AT to assess the consistency in assessing the submissions [see para. 43 of the TAR].

### (v) Is the FREL/FRL complete? (Has information been provided that allows for the reconstruction of the FREL/FRL?)

The AT noted [see para. 37 of Chile’s TAR] that the data and information used by Chile in constructing its PRELs and FRLs are complete, transparent, and in accordance with the guidelines. The information supplied significantly in the modified FREL/FRL submissions is consistent with the guidance provided for the modified submission, including the description of methods and data sources, increased reproducibility of FREL/FRL calculations (see para. 39 of the TAR).


---
**Page 126**
---

The PREL/FRL submitted by Chile is based on the AD extracted from the historical series data included in the land-use cadastre made by the National Forest Corporation (CONAF) (period 1997 to 2018). The AT notes that Chile has a database with different base years for Chilean regions. This information will be used in the RHEP project and the PREL/FRL. The AT noted that Chile could ensure a more consistent time series of land-use data by using the same methodology (LandSat or RapidEye) to ensure the consistency of AD (in spatial and temporal terms). Chile has already started this process, and it will be used in the RHEP project as well.

(vii) Is the PREL/FRL consistent? (Were data and methodologies applied consistently over the time series used for the construction of the PREL/FRL?)

At least one of the following criteria should be met to ensure consistency:

- The same methodology is used throughout the time series.
- The same base year is used in all regions.
- The same base years are used for maps for each region and uses several sources of data.

As noted by Chile, the PREL/FRL is consistent because it considers carbon stocks of different activities considering the different forest types. Chile used methods and equations from the 2006 IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories, as well as tier 2 and tier 3 methods (Bahnfleth et al., 1998; Bahnfleth & Houghton, 2004). PREL/FRL combine different tiers depending on national availability of information. Chile uses a tier 1 method for the estimation of above-ground biomass and DOM, while tier 2 was used to estimate below-ground biomass (BGB) and dead wood. Tier 3 methods were used to estimate forest carbon stocks, where tier 3 methods were used to estimate the above-ground biomass (AGB), below-ground biomass (BGB), ground biomass, dead wood and litter (DOM). Immediate oxidation is assumed for all types of land use change. The PREL/FRL users a tier 3 method developed by Bahnfleth et al., to estimate carbon stocks in forest ecosystems. Chile uses the relevant methodology estimates only CO2 emission. Chile used the relevant


---
**Page 127**
---

equations from the 2009 IPCC Guidelines to estimate the net GHG emissions from forest fires. The AT noted that while Chile has developed stock charts for the forest type "Semiperenne", which includes species such as Nothofagus dombeyi and Nothofagus antarctica (Nothofagus alpina), Chile still needs to develop stock charts for other forest types, including those of the temperate rainforest.

The AT commended Chile for the extensive use of tier 2 and tier 3 methods in its REDD+ activities. The AT also noted that Chile has also commended Chile for its efforts to further develop stock charts for other forest types.

The AT also noted that the uncertainty analyses are detailed and provide a good explanation of the uncertainties (see para 37 of the TAR).

According to paragraph 8 of the TAK, Chile in its modified submission, proposed FRELs and PRLs for four activities: reducing emissions from deforestation; reducing emissions from forest degradation; conservation of forest carbon stocks; and enhancement of forest carbon stocks. The AT noted that in the case of sustainable management of forests, owing to a lack of geophysical data, Chile has not included the reference emission level but decided to include the carbon stocks resulting from sustainable management as part of the reference emission levels or emissions from forest degradation (if the result is emissions) or under enhancement of forest carbon stocks (if the result is removal). Moreover, Chile has also indicated that it will continue the development of management plans based on explicitly explicit information about the potential for enhancing carbon stocks such as the sustainable management of forests and activities that normally lead to an increase in forest carbon stock (see para 25(2) of the TAK). The AT acknowledged that Chile included four of the five activities required by the REDD+ framework, but not all of them in accordance with national capabilities and circumstances. The activity "conservation of forest carbon stocks" is not implemented by Chile under the activities "reducing emissions from forest degradation" or "enhancement of forest carbon stocks (para 33 of the TAK).

The AT noted that all significant sources of emissions are included in the FREL/PRL.

**(a)** Is the information provided in the construction of the FREL/FRL [data, methodologies and estimates] guided by the most recent applicable Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) guidelines and/or good practice guidance as outlined in the policies of COP21?

Note that most recent guidance and guidelines provided in the 2006 IPCC Guidelines as a basis for estimating annual changes in carbon dioxide equivalent emissions of the four activities [see paragraph 11 of the TAR].

**(iii)** Have any significant issues related to the application of IPCC guidelines/good practice guidance been raised in the TA report?

**Answer:** No issues were raised.

**(iii)** What is the reference period for the FREL/FRL?

**Answer:** Chile presented a modified submission with two reference periods using different methods: (1) 2000–2013 for activities and sub-activities that cause land-use change (deforestation, substitution, increase in forest cover, abandonment of forests); (2) 2005–2013 for activities that occur on forests remaining forests [conservation of forested areas], afforestation, reforestation, and the recovery of degraded lands] [see para. 78 of the TAR]. The reference period is consistent with Chile’s previous submissions.

**(iv)** How does the reference level for the results included in the proposal compare with the previous reference level that applies to the same activity?

**Answer:** Chile’s FRELs and FRLs are the first reference levels submitted to the UNFCCC by the country as a result of results-based payments. The AT noted in paragraph 25 of the TAR that the modified submission includes an updated assessment of the carbon stock assessment, and the AT commended Chile for its efforts on including non-CO2 gases using appropriate equations. Emissions are estimated using non-weighted error propagation (2006 IPCC Guidelines, equation 12). The AT also acknowledged Chile’s progress in developing a methodology to account for activity, region, carbon pool and parameters used to estimate the emissions and removals.

The REDD+ pilot phase of Chile is an integral part of the ENCORS approved by the Government of Chile in 2016. Activities implemented as part of the REDD+ plus programme and the development of a national forest strategy are aligned with the country's compliance with this strategy developed based on broad stakeholder participation, which includes the integration all national forest programmes and international commitments of the country.

All the phases of REDD-plus, throughout the preparation and implementation stages, will be subject to independent verification of emission reduction results, ensured (and will keep on ensuring) the process is transparent and accountable. This will include forest and environmental governance structures, and that national legislation is respected at all times.

The highest decision-making body in the Council of Ministers for Sustainable Development (CONAFOR) has established a system of national communications to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and its Kyoto Protocol. The National Forest Corporation (CONAF) is the national focal point for Chile's REDD+ pilot phase, responsible for the preparation and reporting of ERs, and preparation and reporting of FREL and the technical verification of results, and coordination with the National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS), which will be in place by the time of the previous year data to the system.

The United Nations Collaborative Programme on Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation in Developing Countries (UN-REDD) is the implementing agency for the REDD+ pilot phase. The UN-REDD, which falls under the CONAFOR, has the role of serving entity and coordination body between the Government of Chile and the ENCORS; in this case, the UN-REDD maintains a permanent technical link with the National Forest Monitoring System (NFMS), which is made up of CONAFR technical managers.

Similarly, the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Livestock's Technical Committee on Climate Change, led by the Office of Agricultural Research and Innovation (MINAGRI) with the aim of strengthening the technical decisions that are taken in the REDD+ pilot phase. The transparency of the whole management system is ensured by the relevant national legislation and regulations. The UN-REDD has a web page that is rich with information and facts on the


---
**Page 137**
---

### Implementation of REDD+ pilot programme <https://www.cccm.fund>

ENCOFV ensures respect for the knowledge and rights of indigenous peoples in all communication, complying with international obligations ratified by Chile, its circumstances and national legislation, including the United Nations Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples, which was approved by the United Nations General Assembly.

In order to ensure that the REDD+ pilot programme is implemented in accordance with the existing legal framework, Chile generated, through GONAL, specific policy guidelines for the participation of indigenous peoples and women in the development and implementation of the programme. These guidelines are based on the principles of respect for the dignity, human rights, economies and cultures of indigenous peoples and provide social and economic benefits that are culturally appropriate to the needs of indigenous Peoples.

The full and effective participation of indigenous peoples and women in the development and implementation of the REDD+ pilot programme is ensured by their active involvement in mainstreaming ministries in the implementation process. Each of these forms of participation have associated national and international instruments, which include specific management instruments that are designed to allow for “respecting” this safeguard and addressing any potential conflicts with respect and comply with this safeguard as it is found.

The Plan for the Implementation of Social and Environmental Safeguards for the REDD+ pilot programme (Self-assessment) has been prepared, taking into consideration the guidelines established at international and national level in this


---
**Page 138**
---

matter (available on GCFDF website). This document guided the development of the entire participatory formulation process of the ENCCF between 2015 and 2016. The Plan included the organization of a series of workshops and consultations, ensuring local, regional and national representation, considering a multi-stakeholder approach and mainstreaming of the gender approach and pluralistic participation.

In accordance to the funding proposal. Chile fully addresses and respects this safeguard in the achievement of its emission reduction.

To address this safeguard, the ENCCF considers the integration of the ratified international agreements and conventions signed by the country. ## Actions to reduce displacement of emissions.

Pass


### Risks of emission displacement associated with the mitigation of deforestation and degradation activities will be monitored within this ENCCRF. These risks include: competition for land use between agricultural/livestock activity and urban and industrial expansion; unsustainable management of forest lands, including over-exploitation and fire; unsustainable management of forest crops; and unsustainable management of other forest resources.

There are several laws on reducing emission displacement processes. Among them, Law No. 26/283 / 2008, on Recovery of the Environment, which is a legal instrument that can be applied in the context of emission displacement reduction. This law is an important legal instrument whose objectives include: the protection, recovery and sustainable use of natural resources; to ensure forest sustainability and environmental policy, in line with ENCCRF. The law also provides for the classification of forest types, management plans, environmental protection norms, the Conservation, Recovery and Sustainable Management Fund.

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP 120)

## Proposal name: Chile REDD-plus results-based payments for results period 2014-2016

## Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

### Impact potential
FAO welcomes the "High" assessment score. Chile successfully reported in a transparent, consistent and accurate manner the results of the ENCVR implementation. Chile ENCVR aimed to reduce deforestation and degradation by promoting sustainable land use for agriculture, forest degradation and the barriers to restoration, conservation and sustainable management of all types of forests. As mentioned by the ITAP, the REDD-plus process on Chile is a good example of how countries can achieve results-based payments funds for achieving results in terms of climate change mitigation and adaptation in line with their national political commitments.

### Paradigm shift potential
FAO welcomes the "High" assessment score.

### Sustainable development potential
FAO welcomes the "High" assessment score and shares the recognition and appreciation for the activities implemented between 2014-2016 to reduce deforestation and degradation, increased carbon stock and forest conservation in Chile, contributing to sustainable development.

Also, the ITAP acknowledged that the benefit-sharing system to be implemented for the reinvestment of the proceeds is built in accordance with the ENCVR. This will make a significant contribution to the well-being of vulnerable groups such as indigenous people living in the five REDD-plus program areas/villages in Chile.

### Needs of the recipient
FAO welcomes the "High" assessment score.

### Country ownership
FAO welcomes the "High" assessment score.
ENCVR was formulated and approved in 2016 after three years of intensive consultations with national and international key players, taking into consideration all national and international commitments of Chile. In addition, the Nationally Designated Authority (NDA) actively supported the implementation of the Chile National Technical Advisory Panel by providing technical recommendations through the Technical Advisory Panel (TAP).

# I. Introduction

According to the requirements of the GGF Gender Policy (UN Women, GGF, 2017) Chile REDD+ RBP proposal must include a gender assessment and a gender action plan in order to provide a detailed analysis of the scope of the ENCVRP which REED+ integrates with regard to its ability to fulfill international and national commitments in the field of gender equality and women's rights.

Over the last decade, Chile has made good progress in promoting gender equality. According to the ranking of the Global Gender Gap at the World Economic Forum for this year 2018, Chile has an index of core 0.717, ranking it 54 out of 144 countries (World Economic Forum, 2018). The country has taken concrete actions to promote and integrate gender equality in its policies and programs, including the National Plan for Gender Equality and the commitments of the Regional Conference on Women in Latin America and the Caribbean. Some of these actions are reflected in the Chilean REDD+ Strategy, which includes measures aimed at reducing the incidence of discrimination and of obstacles to full equality of opportunity between men and women in the country. Additionally, the provisions of the National Forest Policy (PNF) 2015-2030 include a gender perspective for forest quality.

The actions REED+ of the ENCVRP will focus on a significant part of the national territory where there is a high concentration and diversity of forests in the country, comprising five of the fifteen administrative regions of Chile: Maule, Biobío, Araucanía, Los Ríos and Los Lagos. These regions are characterized by their mountainous country and host 41% of the Native Forest, with representation of 11 of the 12 Forest Types defined at the national level. In addition to this, these regions have a high concentration of forest resources, including areas of high diversity of forest fires, high demand for firewood, poverty and vulnerable populations among others. From the beginning the ENCVRP has been designed as an initiative that is not only focused on public forests (state-owned or communal) but ruling out that later it will be extended to other regions of the country. The main objective of the ENCVRP is to promote the conservation and sustainable use of native forest resources, including those in private ownership and used by small and medium-sized owners, including women and indigenous communities (CONAF, 2017).

The gender assessment/action plan presented in this document assesses the gender sensitivity of the ENCVRP REDD+ targets, activities indicators and results. The objective is to provide an analysis tailored to the gender dimension of the project, which will be used as a basis for the implementation of the ENCVRP, and make recommendations in line with the Gender Policies of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and of FAO.

## II. Gender inequalities in Chile

### a) Education

Educational level of boys and girls

Chile has made progress in recent years in expanding the coverage and accessibility of education. The access to formal school education and higher education for males and females in the last few years has remained in equal conditions, with an increase of 1% between 2009-2014 and 2014-2018.

With regard to access as per educational level, figures also show clear characteristics of gender inequality. The percentage of girls who have access to higher education is slightly higher in males; higher education is the only category that females have more access to (Table 1).

| Education Level | Number | Percentage | Number | Percentage | Number | Percentage |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Pre-school | 736,250 | 4.8 | 918,521 | 6.0 | 1,654,771 | 4.07 |
| Special | 176,818 | 3.7 | 106,726 | 2.24 | 70,092 | 1.47 |
| Transitional | 1,502,032 | 9.6 | 1,051,811 | 10.88 | 2,553,843 | 6.58 |
| Primary | 3,029,032 | 21.6 | 537,611 | 10.88 | 511,421 | 10.75 |
| Secondary | 3,029,032 | 21.6 | 537,611 | 10.88 | 511,421 | 10.75 |
| Higher education | 1,766,731 | 100 | 2,986,574 | 56.42 | 3,354,196 | 49.58 |

Despite progress, women's access to formal school education still depends on significant differences by area of residence (rural/urban) and ethnic origins. According to data from the Ministry of Education (Mineduc in 2015), the educational level is lower for indigenous people: only 39% of rural women have a schooling of less than 5 years, in relation to the urban area (10%) of schooling in the same age group. The percentage of indigenous women who do not identify with an ethnic origin or who are not native as indigenous, are compiled with a lower educational level than women who do not identify with an indigenous people and who have a higher level of education: 52% compared to 18%, respectively (INE, 2018).

The access to education becomes more difficult for women living in rural areas and belonging to indigenous communities. The gap between the language used at home and the language taught in school is significant. In terms of figures, 14.9% of indigenous women manage to complete secondary education compared to 18.6% non-indigenous women. With respect to higher education, only 3.6% of indigenous women manage to finish it, compared with 20% of non-indigenous women (CASEN, 2015).

### Illiteracy rates

The literacy rates of the country's population have considerably decreased in the last 15 years. Between 1990 and 2015, for men and women, the reduction accounted for 39%. In 2015, the trend remains where women are the ones who, in a larger percentage: do not know how to read or write.

b) Labour force participation

In Chile, female labour force participation (LFP) and employment have risen only slightly over the last 20 years. Women have increased their labour market participation rate faster than men; labour participation has been maintained with no major variations for men; for women, from 1990 to 2015 it has increased by 14.8 percentage points (Figure 8).

# Food supply and nutrition

With regard to the food and nutrition of individuals, in Chile there are food and nutrition gaps between men and women, and also with respect to the areas in which they reside. According to the Ministry of Health, the healthy eating index shows that in urban areas, 7.3% of women have an unhealthy diet and 9% of men have a healthy diet. In rural areas, these percentages are 10.5% for women and 8.2% for men.

On the other hand, with respect to the overweight and obesity of the individuals, there are also gaps by area and gender. The Ministry of Health states that in urban areas, 33.9% of women and 44.8% of men are overweight, and 50.6% of women and 57.2% of men are obese. In rural areas, the percentages for women are 31.3%, while those for men are 40.3%. Of these, 39.3% of women and 22.7% of men are obese. Thus, it is observed that although there are no large gaps in terms of overweight or obesity between urban and rural areas, since the case of overweight, women suffer less in this trend compared to men, but in the case of obesity women suffer this condition to a greater extent than men (National Food Consumption Survey, Ministry of Health, 2014).

## f) Unpaid domestic and care work

In Chile domestic and reproductive work in the household, as well as the care responsibility for dependents, continue to be culturally recognized as the female functions. According to data from the 2015 Time-Use Survey, the distribution of paid and unpaid work is very highly unequal by gender and socioeconomic status (terms of hours). The average weekly working time in Chile was 49.8 hours per week for men and 5.9 hours (INE, 2016).

When analyzing the total workload (which measures unpaid and paid work together with transportation time) it is possible to observe that women spend more than twice as much time on this type of activity compared to men: in the employed population of 15 years old, the total workload presents pronounced gender differences: 9.78 hours for

e) Social protection

In Chile, the pension system underwent profound changes after the implementation of the 2008 reform. Previously, the sector’s operational reform in 1981 replaced the pay-as-you-go system with an individual capitalization model.

The system is financed by contributions from employees and employers equivalent to 10% of wages. Taxes on deposit contributions in the call Pension Fund Administrators (AFP).

By 2015, the rate of affiliation to the pension system (total number of people who declared that they have contributed to an AFP) was 83.7%. The gap between men and women exceeds 10 percentage points.

The Chilean Social Protection Survey (EPS). The gap between men and women exceeds 10 percentage points, with enrollment rates at 85.3% for men versus only 72%.

This gap, although still significant, has shown a clear tendency to decrease over time. In the 2006 survey, the difference was even greater (9.4 percentage points). The rate of affiliation among women has increased in the enrollment rate of active women in the system, which had risen from 63.5% in 2006 to 69% in 2009.

For both sexes, the highest rates are observed for individuals with a higher educational level (87.4%), followed by those without children (81.2%). The lowest rate is among individuals with a lower educational status: 53.3% of men and 62.0% of women). In contrast, the youngest section, from 18 to 34 years, reaches only 74.1%. Among those who indicate that they are married or in a stable union, the rate is 90.4%, while for those with no marital status, the highest rate of affiliation is observed among "separated, divorced or annulled" individuals, with 88.4% of men and 75.2% of women). The enrollment rate tends to be increasing with respect to the educational level (from 59.2%, among those who indicate not having completed secondary school, to 90.3%, for those who have a university degree).

The rate is also higher in families with children: 71.2% for individuals without children, 85.1% with 1 child, 86.5% with 2 children and 81.7% with 3 or more children) (Ministry of Labor and Social Security, 2013).

## II. Legal and administrative framework protecting women and promoting gender equality in Chile

### a) International protocols and frameworks ratified by Chile in support of gender Equality, women's empowerment and Human Rights

Chile has made great legislative advances towards equality and social inclusion. The international-level instruments that Chile has ratified include the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action adopted at the Fourth World Conference on Women in 1995, and the Programme of Action adopted by the International Conference on Population and Development (1994), the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action. Chile has also ratified the Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities, which reinforces that "the active participation of women, on equal conditions than men, in all levels of decision-making is essential for achieving equality, sustainable development, peace and democracy." The country has also ratified


---
**Page 155**
---

# National gender equality and women’s empowerment frameworks in Chile

At the national level, the State Constitution of Chile includes the gender equality principles, in its article 19, number 2, that states: “Equality before the law – men and women are equal” (Constitution of the Republic of Chile, 1980). Additionally, the Law No. 20.659/2012 establishes measures against discrimination and provides for its Article no. 1, which states: “The State is committed to promoting and implementing policies related to guaranteeing every individual, without arbitrary discrimination, the enjoyment


---
**Page 156**
---

## Institutional framework: National machinery for the advancement of women

Since its creation in 1997, the National Service for Women has succeeded in mainstreaming the gender perspective across all State institutions so that, in their activities, they take into account the differing needs of men and women, thereby promoting gender equality. However, the Service's capacity for independent action is limited by the fact that it is a technical body within the Ministry of Women and Gender Equality. That called for a new institutional framework for gender matters to bridge any persisting gaps, improve legislative coherence, and speed up the achievement of substantive equality in all areas covered by the Convention (CEDAW).

Against that backdrop, Act No. 20.820, creating the Ministry for Women and Gender Equity and amending legal provisions on gender equality, was approved by Congress on December 15, 2014, and published in the Official Gazette of Chile on January 30, 2015. It establishes in its title No. 1, "The Ministry's responsibilities for the collaboration with the President of the Republic in the development of public policies that promote gender equality, ensure the full enjoyment by women of equality, equity of rights and ensure the removal of all types of arbitrary discrimination against women". Likewise, this law defines that: 'gender equality consists of the absence of differential treatment between men and women and results in a total absence of any mean of arbitrary discrimination against women for being such, with regard to the ability
