# National Policies, Plans and Programs on gender equality

## Chile has achieved significant progress in gender-sensitive public policies from the Management Improvement Programme (PAE). Gender considerations are included to promote gender equality.

### The National Institute of Statistics established within the National Statistical Commission, the Subcommission on Gender Statistics, is cooperating with the Ministry for Women and Gender Equity. This partnership provides information as a basis for high-impact public policies with a gender perspective.

#### Concerning institutional mechanisms of gender, the following advances in this matter are highlighted at 22 ministerial levels: a) 10 ministries have gender coordinators, b) 34 ministries have gender focal points, c) 57 ministries have gender offices, d) 126 public services have gender managers. d) Fifteen regions have Regional Commissions on Gender Equality, and the Ministry of Women and Gender Equity has a National Commission for Public Health, Education, Agriculture and Social Development (Ministry of Women and Gender Equity, 2018b).

## Chile's national policies related to gender equality have been developed from the 1990s until the current period.

### A Social Protection System based on rights was created in 2012 (law 20.595) during the second government of Michelle Bachelet. The following subsystems are running: a) Child Protection Subsystem, b) Subsystem of Securities and Opportunities for families in poverty and extreme poverty that replaced the Chile Solidarity Program, c) Rights and Opportunities for women in poverty and extreme poverty, d) Subsystem of Long-Term Care in order to provide care to people with long-term care needs: elderly and dependent persons, and their caregivers. However, the proportion of women in this subsystem is 73%, while men represent only 27%. e) Subsystem of Health and Opportunities (78.3%). Child Protection Subsystem: 17% and Subsystem of Care and Support: 3.7%. According to some studies, the gender gap in access to health services is still present today, despite the significant progress for the existing demand. In spite of the fact that these measures allowed access to health and retirement benefits equal to men, women continue to be excluded from the social security system due to the lack of existing policies of education, health and social security.

### At the local level, one of the main goals of the Ministry of Women and Gender Equity has been the strengthening and expansion of the Municipal Offices for Women and Gender Equity, with the aim of promoting equal care (Anigada y Miranda, 2019).


---
**Page 158**
---

participation in local development plans. In the framework of a complementary action with other local actors, the Fourth National Plan for Women’s and Men’s Equality 2018 – 2030 proposes several measures to reinforce the alliances with Regional Governments and Local Government (municipalities) (Ministry of Women and Gender Equality, 2019a). 

- Since 2015, the Ministry of National Assets has organized discussion groups for women, particularly indigenous women, in order to disseminate the benefits of the regularization of small real-estate holdings for women married under the regime of community property. Article 37 of Decree-Law No. 2.835 governs such rights and provides that women who are married under this regime of property, and whose married women shall be considered subject to separation of property for all legal purposes related to the asset concerned (Ministry of Women and Gender Equality, 2019b).

- On the basis of its strategic principles for the period 2015-2018 and the intercultural approach cutting across its policies and programmes, the National Service for Women and Gender Equality, in cooperation with the Ministry of National Assets, has organized several workshops on the issue of land rights with indigenous women with a view to the participatory identification of those women’s interests and needs and their right to access land (including communal lands), their right to participate freely in political rights, and their right to a life free of violence) in accordance with their world view and culture. The International Board on Mapuche Women, created in 2015, aims to promote intercultural work addressing the gender inequalities faced by Mapuche women in the Araucanía region (CEDAW, 2018).

- Ministry of National Assets. (n.d.). Resolution No. 37/2019 RIL approving the Framework Cooperation Agreement on the “Intercultural Committee for Mapuche Women”. Temuco, 15 December 2019.

### III. Gender Issues Around Forests and REDD+ National Strategy

China has progressed steadily in the strengthening of its environmental regulatory and operational framework (international, national and sectoral levels), which has led to the identification of the main local constraints and synergies for implementing gender equality.

To achieve this (2014 - 2016) gender was mainstreamed into activities undertaken on the basis of the enforcement of the related legal framework (see section above) and strengthened with the CONAF Unit for Gender Equality. The National Strategy for Gender Equality in Forestry (NSGEF) is a key instrument within the scope of the National Women's Service recommended for the Constitution of Gender Units by Sector to all State agencies, including CONAF (Official Letter No. 414, August 2015). The NSGEF is the framework of the strategic gender plan at all levels and sectors of the organization of CONAF. The NSGEF participates also in the ENCRRV formulation process and currently participates in the Advisory Commission of the Minister for Equality of Opportunities (CIMEO) and the National Commission on Equal Opportunities (CNCEO). The NSGEF supports the implementation of the Commissions on Equal Opportunities (CIOE) work plan, aimed at proposing strategies to reduce gender gaps and reducing inequalities between women and men in vulnerable groups in the sector.

A central axis of the CONAF consists on mainstreaming the gender approach in all its phases: preparation, implementation and development results, in constant feedback from the continuous strengthening of the objective (LCCSA). The objective of this approach is to address the necessary broad and equal inclusive interests, needs and proposals of women, thus assuring that benefits are equitable between man and woman (ENCRRV, 2017).

Additionally, the gender mainstreaming strategy of the ENCRRV integrates a methodological perspective based on international guidelines such as the "Gender Equality in Forestry: A Gender Perspective. Human Rights and Interculturality", which contains guidelines to evaluate this process in the different phases of the ENCRRV (LCCSA, 2016).

The monitoring and evaluation framework will be also evaluated through specific indicators included in the Framework of Social and Environmental Indicators applicable to the ENCRRV, that has considered adding indicators that allow monitoring and assessing the effectiveness of the measures adopted for the inclusion of women (LCCSA et al. 2017).

#### a) Gender gaps within the target groups of the ENCRRV REDD+ actions

The REDD+ actions included in the ENCRRV strategy, will focus on a significant part of the national territory where credit is generated and where the main forest types are located (FFH). These areas are located in three administrative regions of Chile: Maule, BioBio, Araucania, Los Ríos and Los Lagos. These regions represent 22% of the total area of forests in Chile and 30% of the Native Forest, with representation of 11 out of 12 Forest Types defined at the national level (ENCRRV, 2017).

In the national territory, these regions are those that have shown greater pressure on their forests due to the occurrence of forest fires, high demand for firewood, poverty and vulnerable population among others. The main causes of deforestation in these areas are: a) the use of wood as fuel; b) the conversion of native land for beef, which they do so, for which purpose they try to extract the greatest possible volume of high-value logs, as well as of fuel wood; c) the conversion of native land into pastures and livestock farms, which is done by introducing livestock into the forest for feeding purposes, particularly during the winter seasons (for shelter in view of the low temperatures), and d) the use of native forests for firewood, which is used to heat homes or as fuel wood for livestock breeding, thus hindering the regeneration process and failing the possibilities of recovery of the forest cover. In addition, these areas are characterized by a high poverty rate (30% of the population), with a regular income from their lands (monthly income) (OPF, 2012). The ENCRRV field activities will be focused on four main components: i) reforestation; ii) restoration of the forest in fragments and iii) reforestation with non-wood


---
**Page 160**
---

to estimate changes in land use, forest fire management to reduce emissions from degradation, forest restoration that provides ecosystem services (UCCSA, 2015).

According to the National Institute of Statistics (INE), the total population residing in these regions reaches 9.3 million people, with an average of 32 inhabitants per Square Kilometer, with Biobío being the most densely populated region and Los Lagos having the lowest density population. From the beginning the ENCRV will consider the entire territory of Chile as a priority area for REDD+ actions, but it was not until 2017 that ruling out that later it will be extended to other regions of the country (ENCRRV, 2017). Indigenous organizations, small and large landowners, and local communities are the main actors in these territories.

Most of the local populations in the prioritized areas of the actions REDD+ of the ENCRV live in rural zones and depend on forest resources, including a high use of firewood for domestic use in heating and cooking. Men and women have different roles in the management of forests and their products, which are related to health, facing environmental issues or the consequences of climate change. According to the National survey CASEN (2015), in some regions such as Los Ríos, Biobío, Araucanía and Los Lagos, there is a high level of illiteracy rates, especially in Maule, Araucanía and Los Lagos (Figure 9). It should be noted that, due to the lack of educational and employment opportunities among the younger population, these are regions with high rural migration to urban areas (ENCRRV, 2017).

In these regions, women use and make the most of the different services that forests provide, both in order to obtain food that assures livelihood and/or for small-scale trading, which, occasionally shifts to being a significant family income. Women are also involved in activities such as hunting, fishing, gathering mushrooms, berries, fungi and others, production of handicrafts, beekeeping promotion, dye gathering, tree nurseries, etc. In case of men, there is a higher participation in the commercialization of forest products, which can range from small-scale trade to timber harvesting through their own products for livelihood, from firewood gathering for heating to large-scale trading, and even to the production of charcoal. Women are also involved in activities related to the conservation resources; however, generational changes are reflected in it, e.g., the fact that the activities which were traditionally performed by women are now assumed by men, meaning that men now have a positive valuation of the activities


---
**Page 162**
---

considered for women, which were usually not valued with the importance they have always had, among others that have advantages for women: ensuring sufficient food and diversity at a family level.

The educational gaps between men and women are also compounded in the regions of Aracuariá, Los Lagos and some gender gaps, in particular concerning the average years of schooling in those regions. According to the results of the CASEN 2015, Maule, Araucanía and Los Lagos regions have lower average years of schooling (9.74, 9.97 and 9.73 respectively) than the national level (10.6). However, in terms of the percentage of students enrolled in regular higher education, no large gender gaps are observed (INE, 2014-2016) (table 1).

In general, the access to benefits, funding or incentives related to forests and vegetation resources seems hindered for the most vulnerable population when they do not have the legal regularization of their property's ownership. In Chilean rurality, most of the area is owned by men, which is complemented by the fact that in terms of access to credit instruments, women are at a disadvantage with respect to men. This is because even if being their formal proprietor, they do not have access to bank instruments (ENCVR, 2017). It is common for property management to be transferred from one generation to another through inheritance or by means of marriage (becoming widowed or separated), among other less common cases, but which do not allow them to have the formal ownership. In addition, in Chilean rurality, it is common that women are not recognized as having the same rights to inheritance than men. Based on the results of the seventh national agricultural and forestry census (2007), the figures for land tenure in monopartridal firms show that only 31.7% belonged to female leaders. Regarding forest resources, it is estimated that women have a lower access to these resources because there is a higher gender gap with respect to land rights and exploitation, where women are at a clear disadvantage compared to men.

Under the ENCVR, Chile has designed a specific list of Action Measures that addresses land tenure through adjustments in order to include owners with poor legal security of tenure on the land. This measure considers two procedural aspects: (i) The regularization of property titles by means of the elimination or correction of improper documentation, and the design of the regulatory modifications that will enable the inclusion of an improvement in the conditions for the formalization of property rights; a process that is currently being carried out, and an amendment process will be carried out at the corresponding bodies. There are international experiences with respect to this type of measures, such as those implemented by the World Bank in several countries cases.

(ii) Targeted program for the rectification of ownership titles. Forest owners from communities prioritized by the ENCVR will receive technical support and training on forest management practices. In addition, these owners intend to join any of the management, forestation and / or restoration programs associated with ENCVR will receive financial incentives in order to formalize their property rights as part of the program's activities. This support for the owner will be conditional to specific technical requirements and criteria that will be defined for the

# b) ENCRRV frameworks and policies supporting gender equality and women’s empowerment

The ENCRRV considered a number of Strategic Activities and Action Measures, which as well as providing a contribution in fulfilling the international environmental commitments, include adjustments to the main national legislation on forests and land use. The ENCRRV is aligned with the National Forest Policy 2015-2035, which corresponds to the goals set out for the Forest Policy 2015-2035, approved in 2016, which correspond to: Forest institutionalization; productivity and economic growth; Equity and social inclusion; and Protection and restoration of forest heritage. The Forest Policy 2015-2035 is aligned with the National Strategy for Sustainable Development (NSSD) 2014-2029, which includes active with strong gender component and includes goals for 2015, 2020 and 2025 aimed at supporting regional associations of women in forest management. The ENCRRV also aims to reduce the existing gender gaps in decent work and it also fosters the implementation of training programs and continuous technical assistance.

In addition, the ENCRRV is aligned with the National Action Plan on Climate Change (PANCC) II (2017-2022) and the National Action Plan on Climate Change (PANCC I) (2008-2012). The National Action Plan on Climate Change (PANCC) II is aligned with the National Strategy for Sustainable Development (NSSD) 2014-2029, which includes actions to address climate change and awareness strategy to address climate change, that is expected to identify the connections between the gender and climate change. The ENCRRV also aims at integrating both approaches in the implementation of all the associated actions in exchange climate change initiative, initiating this process with the National Strategy for Sustainable Development (NSSD) 2014-2029 (MINAGRI, 2017). It should be noted that CONAF, with other governmental institutions, was actively involved and worked at the inter-institutional level in the development of the Climate Change Action Plan from 2012 to 2014 and has directly participated in two sectorial plans: “Plan for Climate Change Adaptation for Agriculture” and “Plan for Climate Change Adaptation for the Agroforestry Sector” (MINAGRI, 2017).

The formulation of the ENCRRV has been developed in accordance with national instruments that support the implementation of the actions and measures of the country’s environmental policies. Additionally, there are regulations in force which establish the consultation procedures in accordance with the provisions of Article No. 6, letter a) of the Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD), through the National Consultation Procedure by virtue of Article No. 1, letter A; and No.2 of the Convention. The legal framework with which the ENCRRV is aligned includes:

- The Indigenous Law N° 15253, which established norms on protection, promotion and development of indigenous people, and created the CONADI, this Law grants “... it is duty to society in general and at the State in particular to protect, promote and develop the rights of indigenous peoples, taking into account their culture and communities, adopting appropriate measures for such purpose and protecting indigenous lands, ensuring their proper use and management according to their own traditions and life styles” (MINAGRI, 2017).

- The Law No. 26349 that created and protected the coastal marine space of the native peoples, expressly establishes in its article 3: “... Create the coastal marine space of native peoples, whose objective will be to safeguard the customary use of said spaces, in order to maintain the traditions and the use of natural resources


---
**Page 165**
---

by communities linked to the coastal edge.” It also establishes the scope of the delineation, administration, access and use of the spaces, with a total area of protection that then recovers the State (MINAGRI, 2017).

- The Decree No. 68/2013 of the Ministry of Social Development, which regulated the procedure for indigenous Consultation under Article 6 No. 1 and No. 2 of ILO Convention No. 169, which aims to “[…] give execution of the right of consultation to indigenous peoples” through a procedure established in said regulation. In this case, since the ILO Convention No. 169 is not applicable for the formulation, application and evaluation of development plan or program, an indigenous consultation process is not relevant in this matter, but rather a process of “Participatory Consultation” (MINAGRI, 2017). The Decree No. 68/2013 also establishes that the application of Article N° 1 are considered that “[…] said peoples must participate in the formulation, application and evaluation of national development plans or programs, as well as in the design of projects that involve traditional lifestyles relevant to the conservation and sustainable use of biological diversity and will promote their broader participation in decision-making processes related to the conservation and sustainable use of natural resources and practices, and will encourage the benefits derived from use of that knowledge, innovations and practices are shared with said peoples” (MINAGRI, 2017).

On other non-binding agreements considered in the matter, Law No. 19.300, on General Bases of the Environment and Regulation of the SEA, which in its article 4, referring to the participation citizen, urges the organs of the State to “[…] ensure that the public participates in decision-making processes regarding the access of environmental information and “[…] put forth for the adequate conservation, development and strengthening of cultural heritage, including the protection of cultural traditions of indigenous peoples, communities and individuals”, among other aspects (MINAGRI, 2017).

### c) National REDD+ strategy design from a gender approach

During the formulation and validation stage of the ENCIVR and its SEAS, a widespread participative process was conducted in which the participation of women was assured. This process included multi-sector and multi-level criteria: to incorporate the opinions and proposals of all sectors of society, particularly focusing on women, indigenous peoples, and vulnerable communities in general.

#### The Participatory Process as a methodological base for mainstreaming the gender approach

This participative process included the organization of 15 Regional Workshops for the Participatory Formation of the ENCIVR (SEAS workshops) in all regions of the country, with small-, medium- and large-sized producers, social organizations, indigenous peoples, women’s organizations, local governments, universities and research centers, among others), associations and groups made up of the public, universities and educational centres, companies, non-governmental organizations, etc. The first regional workshop was held on July 26th, 2013 and the remaining workshops were held during 2015 and 2016. From the methodology used in these workshops, it is possible to highlight that they were organized with the participation of women producers, indigenous peoples, non-governmental organizations (NGO) associations such as women’s, agricultural and livestock sectors of the country, as well as different international agencies linked to the ENCIVR, such as:

Since the participatory process developed had as objective to obtain inputs for the design of the guidelines and the validation of the ENCIVR and the SEAS, the early participation of women was assured establishing that beyond the gender approach, it was necessary to ensure that women were present in all stages of the process: from a cross-section in the ENCIVR and its projects, ensuring benefits and respecting women’s rights, as key actors of this process. The participative process developed by the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources obtained the approval sanction at the national and international level at the end of 2016, with the participation of different sectors such as women producers, non-governmental organizations (NGO) associations such as women’s, agricultural and livestock sectors of the country, as well as the different international agencies linked to the ENCIVR, such as:

## Inputs obtained from the women FGs and ENCVR response

Women’s proposals during the participatory process were multiple and diverse, and were used for the formulation of the ENCVR’s SESSA, particularly for identifying the benefits likely to be generated with the implementation of the adopted strategies and actions by ENCVR. The participatory workshops conducted as part of the ENCVR’s formulation phase, allowed to recognize and gather the beliefs and perceptions of both genders, where women’s pivotal focus was on the conservation of natural resources and their sustainable use. This was conducted with a solid background in environmental education as the tool for raising awareness in new generations about the importance of conserving biodiversity and the environment. Women were interested and assured the services derived from forests and other vegetation resources. In contrast, men’s perspective was mainly focused on the conservation of natural resources and their sustainable use from an economic productivity perspective, where technical training should be primarily focused on forestry, and farming and livestock development. The main inputs obtained from women’s proposals during the participatory process are presented in the table 14.

**Footnote:** For more information on the participative workshops of the ENCVR's formulation phase, go to: <http://www.cofa.gob.bo/encvr/en/encvr.html>

# Women's proposals during the participatory process and ENCRR response

## Women's concerns

- Environmental education as a central tool for promoting any change in the behavior of individuals and communities towards the conservation and sustainable use of natural heritage.

## Women's proposals

- Cross-cutting environmental education.
- Empowerment of women to meet their specific needs.
- Creation of studies for visualizing the effects or impacts on flora and fauna.
- Transparency in the allocation of state resources.
- The right to participate in decision-making processes.
- Better opportunities for young people, therefore reducing migration from rural areas.
- For activities to be executed in collaboration with local organizations.
- For gender equality to be a priority in all actions.
- That the engaged stakeholders are better trained ethnically and technically.

## ENCRR response

The ENCRR answers this proposal by incorporating within its cross-cutting action measure the Environmental Education Action Program (EEAP) for promoting intergenerational dialogues between different stakeholders linked to forests, for different education levels, and seeks to benefit more than 8.000 people by 2021.

### The availability, rights and regulation of the use, maintenance and sale of water courses

- Efficient management of water resources.
- Monitoring and control of pollution.
- Coordination between different society stakeholders.
- Communication between different society stakeholders.
- Promoting cultural exchange instances for knowledge feedback among different communities.
- Strengthening the capacity of women to participate in decision-making processes at all levels of society, especially regarding forest identity.
- Mobilization and training programs in forests and/or saxifrage formations.

### ENCRR response

The ENCRR incorporates a facilitating action measure named Adaptation Program for managing vegetation resources within the framework of climate change, desertification, and degradation and drought (Measure QA 6). In its objective, it states that "the ENCRR will promote the implementation of an Action Program for the management of vegetation resources, with the aim of an Adaptation Program for managing vegetation resources, which will generate inputs that are relevant to the design and execution of different actions related to climate change adaptation."

#### The need of a greater institutional presence in the territory

- On-the-vote that are programmed, permanent and with extended hours.
- Improving and sharing CCAA's existing information and communication systems.
- Strengthening the capacity of women to participate in decision-making processes at all levels of society, especially regarding forest identity.
- Participatory workshops with different stakeholders in regional instances, with emphasis on gender equality.
- Easy access web contact with permanent updates.
- Creation of a network of local representatives.
- Extension of rural and municipal offices.

### ENCRR response

New programs that will necessarily imply a greater presence of services in the territory in coordination with communities within which an action is being carried out. In this sense, the ENCRR will promote the implementation of the Communities Prepared Against Forest Fires Program (Measure QA 4), and the Adaptation Program for managing vegetation resources within the framework of climate change, desertification, and degradation and drought (Measure QA 6).

### d) Gender in the ENCRRV REDD+ Governance

Chile has taken steps in REDD+ implementation to ensure equitable representation of women within the ENCRRV REDD+ governance, and to develop Institutional mechanisms/arrangements for gender equality and gender mainstreaming.

At the national level, to advance equity of opportunities and gender equity, the Ministry of Agriculture (MINAGRI) has implemented a National Gender Equality and Equity in Agriculture, Forestry and Indigenous Consultation and Self-assessment, whose function is advice the MINAGRI on matters related to equality of opportunities and gender equity in the Social and Environmental Indicators' Framework, the Safeguard Information System, the Strategic Environmental and Social Assessment (SESA) of the ENCRRV and its Management Framework (UCCSA, 1999 by Decree No. 180 of the General Comptroller's Office of the Republic). In this framework, the MINAGRI has assumed several engagements on gender equality (Ministry of Agriculture of Chile, 2017):


- Increase the participation of women in the forestry and agricultural sector, in Programs to support the development of productive activities.
- Development of human capital and productive capacities of women in the forestry and agricultural sector, to favor their insertion into the labor market.
- Strengthen the capacities of organized women linked to the forestry and agricultural sector, in the field of production and marketing of goods and services.
- Increase the regularization of property titles for the benefit of silvicultural owners through the delivery of clear and secure land tenure.
- Promote the correct use and management of agrochemicals in the activities / occupations of agri-industry that are carried out by women.
- Carry out actions or actions within the MINAGRI, its associated Services and Organizations that favor equal opportunities for men and women to participate in the forestry and agricultural sector. This includes a gender approach to the promotion of their goods and ministerial services to inform rural women on the forestry and agricultural sector about their Sexual and Reproductive Rights.
- Promote actions aimed at the prevention of all violence against rural women, in the countryside and in the forestry and agricultural sector.

Within MINAGRI, the institution responsible for the design, implementation, follow-up and monitoring of the ENCRRV REDD+ governance, is the National and regional CONAF through the Climate Change and Environmental Services Unit (UCCSA), with the support of the Unit of Indigenous and Social Affairs (UAS) and the Unit of Gender Equality and Equity in Agriculture, Forestry and Indigenous Consultation and Self-assessment within the ENCRRV implementation are led in the UIG. The UIG includes one representative at national level and one representative from each technical and administrative management unit of CONAF (GEDEP, GEDEF, GEDEPT).

GEF, GMAP, GIPREF (agric department). It also integrates by 11 regional representatives of the UIG. The national manager of the UIG is charged of supporting the development of the National Gender Plan, the coordination of the Regional Gender Unit, being also responsible for the following-up of the implementation of the National Management and Coordination Program (PAG) on Gender Equality. One of them is in a training mission to implement the mechanisms for promoting gender mainstreaming. The UIG is dedicated to core team (15 regional representatives of the UIG and the representative at national level). # National Level

## Institutional mechanisms and/or arrangements for gender mainstreaming

### Objectives/measures of gender mainstreaming

The functions of the UG are to:

- Ensure gender equity in the formulation and implementation of policies and programs.
- Promote gender equality and women's empowerment by coordinating actions on gender issues.

The UG participates in the ENCCIV formulation process. The regional representatives of the UG participate in the work plan, in accordance with their respective mandates. During 2014–2015 the UG has implemented annual meetings to discuss progress towards the implementation of the Gender Program and to initiate actions on gender issues.

The challenges of the UG are:

- The lack of a representative of all CONAF serums in the National Secretariat.
- The need for the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock to enhance headquarters as well as field offices with programs, heads of departments, and other personnel to implement gender mainstreaming works and reports, including the production of gender analysis diagnostics.

# Regional Level

## Institutional mechanisms and/or arrangements for gender mainstreaming

The regional representatives of the UG are responsible for addressing gender issues in their respective regions.

# e) Gender considerations in the national REDD+ safeguards

The COP 19 established methodological guidelines for developing countries to demonstrate compliance with the social and environmental safeguards in the implementation of REDD+, with three requirements to access payments.

- **REDD+ activities**, regardless of the source and type of funding, are implemented in a manner consistent with the safeguards adopted by COP 16 (Cancún safeguards).
- Countries must establish a Social Information System (SIS) to provide information on how they are addressing and respecting the Cancún safeguards.
- Countries must provide a summary of the information on how they are addressing and respecting all the Cancún safeguards, including those related to gender.

The application of the UNFCCC REDD+ safeguards requires adapting them to the reality of each country, taking into account national and regional context, and aligning them with existing policies and national REDD+ actions. The safeguard approach must be consistent with the principles of respect for human rights, non-discrimination, participation in decision-making processes, protection against discrimination and violation or minimization of socio-environmental risks and the non-infringement of rights in the implementation of projects, in addition to ensuring that all REDD+ activities are carried out in a manner consistent with national social and environmental safeguards throughout all the phases of the Strategy. To adequately follow this rule, the UCOSA has an action plan for gender mainstreaming in the REDD+ strategy, which is currently at the development stage being led and carried out by the Indigenous Affairs Unit of CONAF. The gender approach has been raised in different ways throughout the ENCORV (table 16).

The full and effective participation of relevant stakeholders, including indigenous peoples and local communities, is a key element of the ENCON process (see paragraphs 70 and 72 of LCP/16). Methodological design of the participatory process for the formulation and validation of the action measures was based on the experiences gained from previous participatory processes with indigenous peoples in the context of the Environmental Strategic Assessment (ESA) and Social Assessment (SEA), which included a total of about 300 participants. The ENCON process involved 15 workshops, out during 2016, attended by 97 participants in 80 activities. Through the participatory process, the stakeholders identified potential mitigation measures, providing site suggestions for potential mitigation measures (COMIP 2017). Strategic Environmental and Social Assessment (SESA) is the main tool used to identify environmental and social risks associated with the project, including its potential impacts on indigenous peoples and local communities. The SESA will provide a basis for identifying appropriate action measures that can be implemented in order to avoid or mitigate these risks and impacts, and maximize the potential benefits associated with the project. The ENCON includes a specific focus on the identification of environmental and social risks and impacts related to the protection of the safeguards applicable to the initiative. The ENCON also includes a specific focus on the identification of environmental and social risks and impacts related to the protection of the rights and interests of indigenous peoples. The IFPP will serve as a guideline for preparing site-specific Indigenous Peoples' Participation Plans (IPPPs) in accordance with the principles of free, prior and informed consultation/affirmative action. Principles of free, prior and informed consultation/affirmative action include instruments to be applied such as Plans for Indigenous Peoples and/or Affected Communities, Community Development Plans, and other relevant mechanisms that are consistent with the initiative's environmental and social safeguards framework. The ESA and the ESAM inquire into operational instruments as well as prevention and mitigation measures to address environmental and social risks. These measures should aim at avoiding or mitigating adverse impacts, negative lists to exclude certain activities that are considered high-risk, and positive lists of best practices and innovative approaches to address specific issues related to the initiative's environmental and social safeguards framework.

### IV. Conclusions and recommendations

Chile is currently in the second phase of implementing policy reforms and REDD+ pilots and transitioning towards the third phase of performance-based payments. During this third phase, the ENCRRV REDD+ policies and activities will be fully implemented in 5 regions of the country. This gender analysis has been conducted to inform the design and implementation of the ENCRRV. The results and findings of the gender analysis has also informed and guided the development of a Gender Action Plan.

Chile has made progress in recent years in integrating gender perspectives and awareness into forest policies, programs, and projects. In particular, women's organizations, indigenous peoples are aware that the national REDD+ strategy includes several references to gender equality, which reflects the increasing relevance of gender considerations within forest policy and management.

In Chile, the progress of mainstreaming gender into the ENCRRV has been based on a widespread participative process that included proposals of all sectors of society, focusing on women's indigenous peoples' organizations, and other stakeholders. The national REDD+ strategy includes several references to gender equality (see SESA). The national-level territorial coverage, the total of key stakeholders engaged, the involvement of women's organizations, and the inclusion of indigenous peoples in the process are all unprecedented measures that seek to represent the country's territorial vision with regard to forests and vegetation resources.

Through this participative process, gender considerations and women's and indigenous peoples' demands were integrated into the design and implementation of the ENCRRV. The results of the participative process informed the design and action measures. They contributed to identify the target groups of the ENCRRV: vulnerable groups in rural areas of the country, indigenous and non-indigenous rural communities, indigenous peoples, women's organizations, and other stakeholders.

The need for Environmental education: The Measure MT 6 d of the ENCRRV includes the Environmental Education and Dissemination Program that seeks to benefit more than 8,000 people.

The availability, rights and regulation of the use, maintenance and care of water courses. The ENCRRV incorporates a facilitating action measure named Adaptation Program for managing vegetation resources in the framework of climate change adaptation (Measure GA 1), which will generate inputs that are relevant to the design and execution of afforestation, revegetation, restoration and sustainable forest management programs.

The need of a greater institutional presence in the territory and further assistance, service and accompaniment for landowners particularly for smaller landowners. Different programs will implement this measure, among them:

- Strengthening the Forest and environmental supervision program (Measure MT 7).
- Strengthening the Communities Prepared Against Forest Fires Program (Measure E 4).
- Adaptation Program for managing vegetation resources within the framework of climate change adaptation (Measure GA 1) to address forest fires and drought.

Effective gender integration in these programs depend on an initial gender and territorial analysis is done, whether it is applied to the projects, and whether gender is reflected in all relevant


---
**Page 179**
---

projects components. Additionally, the commitment of project teams to achieving gender equality results and to reporting them, including disseminating gender related lessons learned, is critical.

The following recommendations can help ensure women and indigenous benefit from REDD+:

Establish gender baseline data for targeted ENCRRV's groups

Ensuring gender sensitive in REDD+ processes will require additional efforts related to the establishment of gender baseline data. This should be done through a gender analysis, for all the programmes that will be implemented by the ENCRRV Unit now, some forestry initiatives are being developed and implemented by other units of CONAF. The collection of disaggregated data is a mandatory contribution to available national and local-level databases.

A gender analysis should ideally be carried out in the beginning of REDD+ actions in order to: a) summarize the key characteristics of the target groups (in terms of assets, livelihood activities, how they use resources, etc.), b) identify the main constraints and opportunities for women and men, c) identify gender-based risks and adverse impacts throughout the project's implementation [particularly in the development of REDD+ action plans], d) identify potential strategies to mitigate these risks.

Gender capacity building will be indispensable to generate Gender-specific data collection, that may include specific forestry data (employment, income, market information), land data (ownership, rights use over resources, etc.), and other socio-economic data. The gender analysis should also consider the links with other relationships (intersectoral approach) such as ethnicity, culture, class, religion, income, education, age, etc., which are all important factors that influence women's and men's access to resources.

Gender analysis should therefore be cautious not to treat women and men as homogenous groups. Following these guidelines, the ENCRRV should continue reinforcing the integration of gender and social-cultural inclusion considerations, based on a Human Rights approach.

Strengthening gender equality in local and national planning processes

At CONAF's institutional level, a work methodology that safeguards gender equality mainstreaming and interculturality is starting to be implemented. Different actors are involved:

Challenges involved in this process are related to the availability of a gender expertise, both at national and sub-national levels within the UUS, the UCSSA and the UASIS teams being responsible for the difficult consultation of projects and the ENCRRV's direct management action researches. The UASIS team is part of the Gender Equality Unit (UEG) which is an intersectoral unit (administrative management unit of CONAF) and 15 Regional representatives. The UASIS and the UCSSA teams are responsible for gender equality mainstreaming in all projects, including those carried out by other units within CONAF. These representatives are not exclusively dedicated to work on gender issues within their units. It would be desirable that these units have a specific gender focal point or coordinator. In order to enhance regional headquarters as Administration of parks and programs, heads of departments and project managers should be trained in gender equality issues for projects, works and reports, including the gender perspective, visualizing the challenges and images of women and men in their work. It is also important to mention that this has been made through the development of technical materials such as the Mapuche Intercultural Environmental Model (MIME), Mapuche Intercultural Forest Model (MIFIM) or other participation measures carried out to include indigenous people in the decision-making processes, such as the National Consultation of Peoples developed in 2016, characterization of the actors involved the firewood supply chain within

37


---
**Page 180**
---

the Wood Energy Strategy. Additionally, other instruments are used to increase and regulate the participation of these groups: the Guidelines on the Free, Prior and Informed Consent of the UN REDD Program and the Program and the Project Evaluation Guide with a gender, human rights and intercultural perspectives approach, that oriented them towards promoting the sustainability of energy models and projects in which women’s health, gender equality and interculturality included transversely, among other tools.

Despite the availability of these frameworks, there is still a need for more inclusive thinking in terms of appreciation of gender issues and in terms of overall project design and conceptualization of impacts. Some policy gaps to gender mainstreaming are identified: lack of awareness on the importance of gender equality and gender and gender mainstreaming; limited attention by policy makers to the heterogeneity of communities and their specific needs, which leads to a lack of consideration for the diversity within subgroups often overlooked despite their unique contributions and characteristics, different levels of education and access to information, etc. Moreover, the lack of coordination between working groups limits their ability to fulfill their assigned duties and responsibilities; the absence of gender equality policies and practices.

Challenges involved in this effort are related to the availability of gender expertise within the ENCIVR REDD+ institutional arrangements, as well as with the availability of gender specialists in local decision-making processes. To address these challenges, it is necessary to ensure that gender issues among regional representatives of UICG, UCSSA and UAIS, the coordination between agencies at different levels to achieve a greater link between forest development and gender equality policies and practices.

Ensure the effective participation of women through reducing gender gaps

One of the main challenges while implementing China’s REDD+ priorities will be to empower women throughout the process of capacity building and leadership for ensuring gender consideration into REDD+ actions (beyond the misconception that gender refers merely to the inclusion or targeting of women in projects). To ensure this, it is necessary to consider the following: a) The need to be done more comprehensively explore the ways in which men and women interact and other differences between them; b) The need to include gender analysis into all aspects of REDD+ actions, including these dimensions into national efforts on REDD+. Gender analysis needs to be prioritized in the ENCIVR’s project. c) The need to consider the different roles that women play within gender and socially differentiated interests, behavior, involvement, constraints and opportunities.

Supporting the effective participation of women within the ENCIVR's implementation requires an enabling environment for women to participate in decision-making processes and their engagement. In order to ensure the meaningful participation of women, it will be necessary to consider the following: a) The need to include gender analysis into all aspects of REDD+ actions; b) The need to provide women with access to land, credit, training, information, etc., as well as the establishment of gender-responsive baselines and the assessment of gender-differentiated impacts of value chain development among smallholders and other resource-poor groups involved in value chains. c) The need to ensure that women are included in capacity building projects (that is part of the approach that will guide the execution of ENCIVR's program). d) The need to include a gender analysis on this project on this topic, could up-scale CONAF’s gender tools and methodologies, to other regions.

The ENCRRV actions include changes and improvements in operational practices of afforestation, restoration and sustainable management, promoting associations among communities vulnerable to climate change and other environmental risks, and strengthening their capacity for adaptation.

- With regards to the type of landowners that will be eligible to receive benefits, "small forest owners" will be considered. Small forest owners include: agricultural companies according to the law 01/1964; the entities formed by the conversion of the communal forests according to article 23 of decree 52-77/1977; and the entities formed by the conversion of the communal forests according to article 8 of decree 15-1993. The communities on common grounds classified as "medium owners" will be considered. The prioritization considers aspects of environmental, economic, and social vulnerability to be specified at regional level.

- Small forest owners include: agricultural companies; according to the law 01/1964; the entities formed by the conversion of communal forests according to article 23 of decree 52-77/1977; and the entities formed by the conversion of communal forests according to article 8 of decree 15-1993. "New land farmers" formed according to article 1° of the decree 2-2014/MP, which is in force since January 1st, 2014 and which allows the creation of new companies with a capital of up to 60% of the social capital of such companies held by the original partners or people that have the quality of land farmers according to the Agrarian Reform Law and Services.

Aspects related to land tenure, such as the title over land or the relevance of customary law, certainly cover a relevant function, although it is intended that non-beneficiaries who do not hold tenure rights might also be included in the category of beneficiaries.

- CONAF will promote the participation of women in forest governance structures such as the Regional Commission on Climate Change (CORECC), that will lead a multi-stakeholder engagement process at the subnational level. The regional commissions will have a role in the coordination and management of the sector depending on the region, the figure below shows the relationships between the institutions.

- The funds allocated within each region will allow the definition of eligible areas, taking into consideration aspects such as gender and social vulnerability. In addition to the criteria for equitable allocation of resources, the benefit sharing distribution plan will take into account aspects of gender and social vulnerability in indigenous peoples and vulnerable populations, to ensure positive social and environmental impacts.

Other recommendations that result from the gender assessment are:

- Diagnose on land tenure problems by gender and gender gaps on natural resource management. The information is only available at the national level (not disaggregated at sub-national level).

- Incorporate gender perspectives in forest policies and programs, taking into consideration the role of women on agro-forestry development: production, sustainable resource management, environmental protection, education, communication, caring responsibilities, social protection, etc.

- Strengthen the gender equality of women's organizations and associations. Women should be included as members and promoters and leaders with a gender vision with equal opportunities to participate in decision-making processes at all levels.

- Facilitate women's access to resources: credit technology training technical assistance inputs and implements in order to influence; the strengthening of their organizational capacity, the development of skills and knowledge on forest management and the promotion of their participation in the use and management of the conservation of natural resources. The generation and increase of incomes.

FAO will monitor and evaluate the overall performance of the Gender Action Plan against the objectives and requirements of the Environmental and Social Standards within its project Quality Assurance System and against the ESMP. During implementation, the gender action plan will be reviewed at least once a year to ensure that it remains relevant and appropriate. The indicators for monitoring progress towards achieving the targets set out in this plan will be adjusted according to experience and advice required, as well as any feedback from stakeholders. Monitoring of activities should involve direct participation of affected communities, where possible. Monitoring reports as set out in the ESMP will be produced. Such reports will provide an accurate and objective record of project implementation, including compliance with the ESMP and improvement of project quality.
